**Summary.** This work uses the time-dependent DMRG method to examine spin transport in a zigzag spin chain model representing a spin nematic liquid. By tracking magnon-pair wavepackets, the authors demonstrate that while propagation occurs generally, specific conditions (k=pi) lead to localization due to flat dispersion modes arising from antiferro-quadrupole order. This highlights how subtle details of the underlying quantum ground state dictate transport behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of advanced numerical techniques like tDMRG to study transport phenomena in strongly correlated magnetic systems provides insights into how underlying quantum order (like quasi-long-range order) dictates macroscopic dynamics, which is highly relevant for understanding exotic quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the spin transport properties of a spin nematic liquid confined within a frustrated zigzag spin chain subjected to an external magnetic field.

**Main result.** While magnon pairs generally propagate coherently, the wavepacket remains localized when considering the specific momentum k=pi due to the flat dispersion structure associated with gapless antiferro-quadrupole quasi-long-range order.

**Method.** The analysis employs a time-dependent Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (tDMRG) method to track the time evolution of magnon-pair wavepackets governed by the system's Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The model is a spin nematic liquid realized in a zigzag spin chain, described by a Hamiltonian involving nearest and next-nearest neighbor interactions ($J_1$ and $J_2$) plus a magnetic field term. Periodic boundary conditions are used to maintain uniform ground states.

**Key observables.** Magnon-pair density ($N^{--}(i, t)$), wavepacket propagation velocity, and coherence preservation over time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the case where the initial wavepacket momentum is $k_0=\pi$, and compares results for zero ($\sigma=0$) versus finite ($\sigma>0$) initial width.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Periodic boundary conditions are assumed to avoid edge-induced magnetization structures, and the low-energy excitation dynamics are modeled by bound magnon pairs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of the magnon-pair density in both open (showing expansion/reflection) and periodic boundary conditions. Specifically, Figure 4 contrasts coherent propagation ($\sigma=0$) with localization ($\sigma>0, k_0=\pi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and Hamiltonian, then details the tDMRG methodology for time evolution. It compares results between OBC (showing edge effects) and PBC (preferred). The core analysis contrasts wavepacket dynamics under different initial conditions ($\sigma=0$ vs $\sigma>0$), leading to the conclusion about localization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spin transport property of a spin nematic liquid in a frustrated zigzag spin chain in a magnetic field from the perspective of the time evolution of wavepackets by a time-dependent density-matrix renormalization group method. We use the periodic boundary condition to avoid an edge-induced magnetization structure in the open boundary condition. We find that at the saturation, a magnon-pair wavepacket of momentum k=pi stays localized, since the gapless dispersion due to the antiferro-quadrupole quasi-long-range order has a rather flat structure, indicating zero propagation velocity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores spontaneous disentanglement using modified quantum dynamics applied to several integrable many-body models. It finds that non-local entanglement tends to destabilize in the macroscopic limit, offering a theoretical framework linking microscopic quantum correlations to classical macroscopic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental questions regarding the emergence of classicality from quantum mechanics by quantifying how entanglement decays or stabilizes as system size increases, which is central to open quantum systems theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spontaneous disentanglement, aiming to provide a theoretical bridge between the quantum nature of microscopic systems and the classical behavior observed in macroscopic limits.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that non-local entanglement tends to become unstable in the macroscopic limit for certain models (like Lieb-Mattis and Kitaev), while local entanglement stability remains possible, suggesting a mechanism for decoherence.

**Method.** The research employs modified Schrödinger equations with nonlinear terms derived from Kraus operators to model time evolution. It analyzes various many-body quantum models using established entanglement diagnostics like the partial transpose criterion.

**Model / system.** Three specific integrable many-body models are analyzed: the Lieb-Mattis antiferromagnet, the AKLT ring, and the Kitaev chain. The dynamics are governed by a nonlinear extension of the Schrödinger equation incorporating disentanglement terms.

**Key observables.** Entanglement measures ($	au_{TS}, 	au_l, 	au$), partial transpose eigenvalues (for non-separability), mutual information ($I_{a,b}$), and correlation functions $\langle C_{s',s''}angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The macroscopic limit ($N \gg 1$ or $L \gg 1$) is the primary focus. The ratio of disentanglement to thermalization rates ($\gamma_D/\gamma_H$) is also key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that spontaneous disentanglement can be modeled by adding nonlinear terms to the standard quantum evolution equation, and it relies on exact solutions for specific integrable models.

**Figures summary.** Figures plot expectation values of Hamiltonians ($\langle H angle$) and entanglement measures ($	au_{TS}$) as a function of system parameters ($p$), illustrating state changes across different model sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the spontaneous disentanglement hypothesis via modified dynamics, applies this framework to specific integrable models (Lieb-Mattis, AKLT, Kitaev), calculates entanglement diagnostics in the macroscopic limit, and draws conclusions about the stability of quantum correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recently proposed spontaneous disentanglement hypothesis is formulated using a modified Schrödinger equation having an added nonlinear term. The hypothesis is motivated by some outstanding issues in the foundations of quantum mechanics, including the problem of quantum measurement. Spontaneous disentanglement is explored in the current study for the macroscopic limit. This is done using some many--body models having known exact solutions. For the under--study models, it is found that non--local entanglement becomes unstable in the macroscopic limit. On the other hand, stability in the macroscopic limit of local entanglement is not excluded. These findings demonstrate that the spontaneous disentanglement hypothesis can bridge between the quantumness of the microscopic realm, and the classicalness of the macroscopic one.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an improved Car-Oriented-Mean-Field (iCOMF) theory to analyze stochastic traffic flow models based on cellular automata. By incorporating nearest-neighbor pair correlations, iCOMF overcomes the limitations of classical mean-field theories that fail in congested or inhomogeneous states. The results demonstrate excellent agreement with simulations for the VDR model, providing a more accurate statistical mechanical description of vehicular traffic.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of extending mean-field theories to incorporate short-range, non-factorizable correlations via cluster methods is highly relevant to open quantum systems and many-body physics where simple product states fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To improve the analytical understanding of stochastic traffic flow models by accurately capturing both short- and long-range correlations neglected by classical mean-field theories.

**Main result.** The proposed improved Car-Oriented-Mean-Field (iCOMF) theory provides accurate descriptions of fundamental diagrams and headway distributions for the VDR model, suggesting it may be an exact description in certain limits.

**Method.** The authors develop iCOMF by combining aspects of COMF theory with a two-site cluster method, leading to the formulation and solution of master equations for nearest-neighbor pair probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is single-lane vehicular traffic modeled using Cellular Automata (CA), specifically focusing on the Velocity-Dependent-Randomization (VDR) model with a maximum velocity of 1.

**Key observables.** Fundamental Diagram J(rho) (flow vs. density), zero-headway distributions P_v(0), and non-zero headway distributions P_{u,v}(n).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis covers different regimes including the Cruise-Control Limit (STS regime, p=0) and general models where braking probabilities are involved.

**Assumptions / limitations.** iCOMF assumes a product state over nearest-neighbor pairs; classical COMF fails when particle-hole symmetry is broken or in inhomogeneous steady states.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare fundamental diagrams and headway distributions (0-headway, 1-headway) derived from iCOMF against computer simulations across various traffic regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of classical mean-field theories for traffic flow. It then develops and applies the improved iCOMF theory by formulating master equations for pair correlations. Results are presented by comparing iCOMF predictions against simulation data across different physical regimes (e.g., STS vs. general model).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an improved mean-field analysis of cellular automata models of single-lane vehicular traffic. By combining aspects of the Car-Oriented-Mean-Field (COMF) theory and the 2-site cluster method, which have been previously successfully applied to similar models, we aim to capture both short- and long-range correlations more accurately. In contrast to classical mean-field theories, the improved method is well suited for models with inhomogeneous stationary states and able to capture the essential properties of phase separation, e.g. in models with slow-to-start rules. The improved accuracy and new physical insights are illustrated through an application to the VDR model with $v_{\text{max}}=1$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents advanced techniques for using single NV centers in diamond to map magnetic fields at the nanoscale. By analyzing stray fields from patterned magnetic films (stripes and discs), the authors developed a robust calibration method that determines crucial unknown parameters like the probe distance ($d_{NV}$) and NV orientation ($\phi$). This significantly enhances the quantitative capability of SNVM for fundamental studies in condensed matter magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core technique is magnetometry, the ability to precisely characterize geometric parameters ($d_{NV}, \phi$) *in situ* without external controls makes this a powerful tool for quantum sensing platforms, relevant to understanding coupling strengths in solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enable nanoscale, quantitative imaging of magnetic stray fields using single Nitrogen Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond by accurately determining unknown parameters like the NV-sample distance and its orientation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a method to directly infer the NV center's standoff distance ($d_{NV} = 31.5 	ext{ nm}$) and its azimuthal orientation ($\phi$) with high precision, all without requiring external vector magnet control.

**Method.** A multi-step calibration protocol is employed using patterned magnetic samples (PMA stripes and discs) to map the NV fluorescence signal, which is sensitive to the local magnetic field projected onto the NV axis.

**Model / system.** The system uses a single NV center in a diamond nanopillar probe scanning over perpendicularly magnetized thin films. The measurement relies on modeling the stray magnetic field ($\mathbf{B}$) from these patterned samples using analytic expressions involving complete elliptic integrals for discs, and simpler forms for stripes.

**Key observables.** NV fluorescence signal (Zeeman shift), which is proportional to $	ext{BNV} = \mathbf{n}_{NV} \cdot \mathbf{B}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $d_{NV}$ (standoff distance), $\phi$ (azimuthal orientation), and the magnetic magnetization ($M_{st}$) of the sample.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include negligible thickness for discs ($t \ll d_{NV}$) and that the measured field is solely generated by the patterned sample structure, allowing calibration to remove unknown geometric factors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of NV placement over PMA stripes and discs, showing how stray fields encode $\phi$. Other figures show SEM/AFM images of these patterns and fluorescence dip analysis from inverse AFM measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need to calibrate unknown parameters ($d_{NV}, \phi$) in SNVM. It details a three-step calibration protocol: using stripe line-cuts for $d_{NV}$, then using disc maps to find $\phi$, and finally employing inverse AFM measurements on silicon needles to map lateral NV placement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Individual, scannable nitrogen vacancy (NV) centers in single crystal diamond nanostructures enable nanoscale, quantitative imaging of magnetic stray fields. Nevertheless, important parameters like distance between the NV center and the sample and the orientation of the NV high symmetry axis are often not known precisely and enter data evaluation as free fitting parameters. We here use scanning NV imaging on micro-patterned, perpendicularly magnetized stripes and discs. From these measurements, we directly infer NV - sample distance d_NV and the NV's azimuthal orientation without the need for an external vector magnet control. We determine d_NV = 31.5 nm, while we infer the azimuthal orientation with a precision of 3°. We additionally employ commercially available silicon needles to image the apex topography of our diamond nanostructures to detect surface contamination. Simultaneously, monitoring NV fluorescence as a function of the needle's position allows us to estimate the lateral placement of the NV inside the diamond nanostructure.

</details>

### [Constructive realization of self-referential prediction limits in quantum control: Resource bounds and Gödel-safe architectures](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04779v1)

**Authors:** Salman Sajad Wani, Álvaro Perales-Eceiza, Saif Al-Kuwari, Mir Faizal

**Summary.** This work translates the mathematical impossibility of universal self-prediction into a concrete engineering constraint for quantum hardware. By constructing a diagonal protocol, the authors demonstrate that any predictor embedded in a closed control loop will fail when analyzing its own operation. This establishes 'Gödel-safe architectures' as necessary safeguards for reliable autonomous quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, hardware-level constraints on autonomous quantum computation, showing that self-monitoring loops introduce fundamental computational limitations analogous to Gödel's incompleteness theorems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental theoretical limit on whether a quantum system can universally predict its own experimental outcomes when embedded in a closed-loop control process.

**Main result.** It proves that any deterministic, bounded-time predictor cannot correctly predict its own outcome if the architecture allows for self-reference (the 'forbidden causal path').

**Method.** The proof uses a diagonal construction based on Kleene's recursion theorem, translating computability theory into an operational laboratory obstruction.

**Model / system.** The system is programmable quantum control, realized physically using single-photon Mach-Zehnder Interferometers (MZI) and theoretically modeled via reversible quantum circuits requiring Bennett uncomputation.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is a classical pointer bit ($X$) that deterministically contradicts the predictor's forecast; other observables include the diagonal bit ($b_{	ext{diag}}$) and payload records ($R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters involve computational bounds ($	au(k)$), resource scaling (depth $D_{	ext{log}}$, width $W_{	ext{log}}$), and physical error rates ($p$, visibility $V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the existence of a 'reflective closure condition' allowing the protocol description to influence its own execution path; physically, it assumes ideal single-photon behavior and controlled timing.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely illustrate the circuit diagrams for the diagonal construction, showing the MZI setup used to realize the contradictory pointer bit, and potentially scaling plots of resource requirements vs. prediction complexity.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from abstract computability theory (Kleene's theorems) to defining the necessary architectural condition (reflective closure), then details the contradiction using a diagonal construction ($	ext{C}_A$), and finally maps this proof onto concrete physical realizations using MZI optics and fault-tolerant quantum error correction scaling laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable quantum control systems increasingly rely on predictive modules for certification, real-time feedback, and autonomous decision-making. This development raises a fundamental question: can self-analyzing quantum platforms universally predict their own experimental outcomes? Wolpert formalized a general impossibility of universal self-prediction. Here we translate that limitation into an explicit laboratory obstruction that can be realized with finite resources. We consider settings with programmable quantum control in which predictors can be embedded as subroutines within the experiments they analyze. Our diagonal construction uses Kleene's recursion theorem to transform any deterministic bounded-time predictor into a reversible protocol encoding its own specification. The resulting protocol invokes the predictor on that specification and deterministically produces a classical pointer record that contradicts the forecast. For efficient predictors, the compilation has polynomial overhead and admits concrete physical realizations as a fault-tolerant quantum circuit and as a minimal Mach-Zehnder interferometer. These realizations connect computability-theoretic self-reference to programmable quantum hardware. We also introduce and formally define Gödel-safe architectures. These architectures block the forbidden causal path from the protocol description to an actuator that can affect the pointer during the same run. We analyze their implications for real-time quantum error correction, including the resulting expressiveness trade-offs. As quantum control loops grow in computational expressiveness, the limits of self-reference cease to be mere mathematical abstractions and become explicit engineering constraints for the reliable operation of autonomous quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates the utility of Dynamic Quantum Circuits (DQCs) on a hybrid superconducting qubit-cavity processor. By leveraging mid-circuit measurements and ancilla reuse, they successfully implemented complex algorithms like Shor's algorithm with high fidelity. This establishes DQCs as a promising, scalable route for programmable quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, benchmarked demonstrations of advanced quantum algorithms on a superconducting platform using dynamic circuit techniques, which is highly relevant for researchers developing scalable quantum hardware architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate the advantages of Dynamic Quantum Circuits (DQCs) for quantum computation by reducing physical-qubit overhead through mid-circuit measurements and classical feed-forward control.

**Main result.** The authors successfully implemented complex algorithms, including a 10-bit Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm ($	ext{SSO}=82\%$) and Shor's algorithm for factoring 15 ($	ext{SSO}>99.8\%$), on a hybrid superconducting platform.

**Method.** The study employs DQCs utilizing a high-dimensional cavity qudit as the register and a reusable transmon ancilla, enabling adaptive control sequences that surpass static circuit performance.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single hybrid superconducting qubit–cavity processor (cQED) housed in a dilution refrigerator. It consists of a high-dimensional cavity qudit coupled to a dispersively coupled transmon ancilla, modeled by a dispersive Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Success Probability ($	ext{SSO}$), Estimation Errors ($<10^{-3}$ for QPE), and fidelity measurements (e.g., $	ext{F}_{	ext{QND}} = 99.09\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity coherence times ($\sim 1 	ext{ ms}$); Transmon coherence times ($\sim 100 \mu	ext{s}$); Coupling strength ($\chi_{QC}/2\pi = 2.59 	ext{ MHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance is limited by the fidelity of mid-circuit measurements, rapid resets, and the accuracy of GRAPE optimization for high-photon-number state control.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the DQC sequence for BV (showing probability distributions), QPE (showing phase estimation results), and Shor's algorithm (showing $	ext{SSO}$ values for factoring 15).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces DQCs, then details the experimental realization on the superconducting platform by characterizing high-fidelity components (readout/reset). Finally, it demonstrates increasing algorithmic complexity: BV $ightarrow$ QPE $ightarrow$ Shor's algorithm, concluding with an error analysis isolating decay and control imperfections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamic quantum circuits (DQCs) provide a hardware-efficient route to quantum computing by reducing physical-qubit overhead and compressing circuit topology through mid-circuit measurements, qubit reset and reuse, and classical feed-forward control. Here, we demonstrate the advantages of DQCs on a single hybrid superconducting qubit-cavity processor by implementing a hierarchy of algorithms with increasing complexity. This hybrid architecture consists of a high-dimensional cavity qudit serving as the computational register and a dispersively coupled superconducting transmon ancilla that is repeatedly measured, reset, and reused to enable dynamic control. Using this device, we implement a 10-bit Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm with an average success probability of 82%, surpassing state-of-the-art dynamic and static implementations in both scale and performance; an 8-bit quantum phase-estimation protocol with estimation errors below 10-3; and the first dynamic-circuit implementation of Shor's algorithm on a superconducting platform, factoring 15 over all coprime bases with squared statistical overlap values above 99.8%. These results provide concrete benchmarks for future DQC implementations and highlight the versatile advantages of DQCs with the hybrid qubit-qudit architecture, establishing it as a promising route toward scalable, programmable quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the intermittent coexistence of laminar and turbulent flow in pipe flow at intermediate Reynolds numbers. Using DNS simulations, the authors characterize 'laminar gaps' (anti-puffs) that maintain stability through a self-regulating mechanism involving two distinct fluid fronts. They conclude that these gaps are fundamental to intermittency and predict their disappearance marks the onset of fully developed, homogeneous turbulence.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a transition controlled by metastable, dynamically stable localized structures (laminar gaps) whose disappearance signals a phase change is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium transitions in complex physical systems, analogous to pattern formation or critical phenomena studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes the intermittent phase where laminar and turbulent states coexist during the transition from laminar to fully turbulent flow in pipe flow at intermediate Reynolds numbers.

**Main result.** Laminar gaps, analogous to turbulent 'puffs,' exhibit self-regulation via matching front speeds and are predicted to become unstable and disappear at a finite critical Reynolds number ($	ext{Re} \sim 2900$), marking the onset of homogeneous turbulence.

**Method.** The analysis relies on Direct Numerical Simulations (DNS) applied to pipe flow, coupled with detailed diagnostics of spatio-temporal structures like traveling fronts and gap lifetimes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is steady, incompressible pipe flow (Poiseuille flow). The dynamics are analyzed by tracking localized coherent structures—'puffs' in the lower $	ext{Re}$ range and 'laminar gaps' (anti-puffs) in the higher $	ext{Re}$ range.

**Key observables.** Turbulence fraction, gap size, front speeds ($c_+$ and $c_-$), mean lifetimes ($	au$) of laminar gaps, and cross-sectionally averaged energy profiles ($\mathfrak{q}(z)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reynolds number ($	ext{Re}$), specifically focusing on ranges like $(1750, 2300)$ and $(2300, 3000)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The persistence of laminar gaps is modeled as an escape process from a chaotic saddle; the transition to uniform turbulence is assumed to be sharp at $	ext{Re} \sim 2900$.

**Figures summary.** Figures visualize space-time evolution comparing puff regimes vs. gap regimes, schematics illustrate the self-regulating mechanism involving two fronts, and profiles compare large contracting gaps (slugs) versus small spontaneously generated anti-puffs.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the existence of coexisting laminar/turbulent states at intermediate $	ext{Re}$. It then details the dynamics of these structures (anti-puffs), showing their self-regulation and exponential decay. Finally, it predicts the critical $	ext{Re}$ where this mechanism fails, leading to homogeneous turbulence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pipe flow at intermediate Reynolds numbers, between the laminar and fully turbulent regimes, takes the form of several spatially and temporally intermittent phases in which turbulent and laminar states coexist. In the lower range, $Re\in (1750,2300)$, turbulence appears in the form of localized traveling structures called "puffs", which form long-lived chaotic dynamical states, whose stochastic decays and splits control the steady state intermittency. At the other end, $Re\in (2300,3000)$, puffs are replaced by an extended turbulent state, with laminar pockets intermittently forming and disappearing within it. Using direct numerical simulations of pipe flow at $Re = 2400, 2450, 2500, 2550$, we provide evidence that these laminar gaps form a distinct dynamical state analogous to puffs: a traveling laminar pocket in a turbulent surrounding, stabilized by a shear-dependent self-tuning mechanism. We analyse the mean spatial profile of these gaps and show that their lifetimes are exponentially distributed, suggesting that gap closing corresponds to an escape from a chaotic saddle. Finally, we suggest these laminar gaps become unstable and disappear at a finite Reynolds number, $Re\sim 2900$, which can be interpreted as the onset point of spatially and temporally homogeneous turbulence.

</details>

### [Poled-fibre phase modulator for efficient high-dimensional quantum measurements](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04112v1)

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates the use of Poled-fibre phase modulators (PFPMs) to overcome major bottlenecks in high-dimensional quantum measurements. By offering low loss and polarization independence directly on standard telecom fibers, PFPMs enable a highly efficient receiver for advanced QKD protocols. The results establish this technology as a key building block for scalable, fiber-integrated quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and photonic QI as it provides an ultra-low-loss, fiber-native component crucial for scaling up complex quantum network protocols beyond laboratory settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving efficient, high-dimensional quantum state detection for advanced protocols like QKD, where conventional modulators introduce significant optical losses and polarization sensitivity.

**Main result.** The use of Poled-fibre phase modulators (PFPMs) enables a fully fiber-integrated platform that significantly reduces receiver loss and achieves record finite secret-key rates in four-dimensional QKD demonstrations.

**Method.** The authors implemented an active quantum measurement basis selection using the PFPM within a high-dimensional QKD setup, comparing its performance against traditional $	ext{LiNbO}_3$ modulators.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes Poled-fibre phase modulators (PFPMs) integrated into a fiber platform for active quantum state measurements in four dimensions ($d=4$). This technology is inherently compatible with standard telecom fibers and offers low loss and polarization independence.

**Key observables.** Finite secret-key rate per pulse, receiver insertion loss (e.g., 2.79 dB vs 5.76 dB), and the ability to perform basis switching using a single modulator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Four-dimensional qudits ($d=4$), sub-dB insertion loss, $10 	ext{ dB}$ channel attenuation, and operational stability up to $100 	ext{ kHz}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the mode-dependent loss contribution to QBER is negligible compared to the measured baseline interferometric QBER.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare receiver losses using conventional modulators versus PFPMs for basis selection; other figures detail characterization setups and system cross-sections.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for low-loss, high-dimensional measurement devices. It then details the fabrication and characterization of the PFPM. Finally, it presents experimental results demonstrating its use in a four-dimensional QKD session, quantifying performance improvements over prior art.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient detection of quantum states underpins advanced device-independent quantum-information protocols that provide the ultimate level of security for tasks including quantum random number generation and quantum key distribution (QKD). High-dimensional encoding is a natural route to boost the performance of such protocols, offering enhanced noise resilience and higher information capacity, yet their practical implementation remains challenging. A key experimental bottleneck in higher dimensions is the typical need of active modulators for basis selection, which incur substantial optical losses and polarization-sensitive operation. Poled optical fiber phase modulators (PFPMs) are a fiber-native electro-optic technology that naturally addresses these challenges, combining sub-dB insertion loss, intrinsic polarization independence, and direct compatibility with standard telecommunications fiber. Here we report the first use of a PFPM for active quantum-state measurements in a fully fiber-integrated platform. Basis selection in our receiver for four-dimensional qudits is achieved using a single PFPM, substantially simplifying the receiver architecture. As a benchmark, we perform a four-dimensional QKD session and obtain a finite secret-key rate per pulse that, to the best of our knowledge, surpasses all previously reported QKD demonstrations. Our results establish poled-fiber electro-optic modulation as a broadly applicable platform for high-efficiency detection in fiber-integrated quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory for quantifying the minimum error rate when distinguishing between two sets of unknown quantum states (state qualification). By analyzing different spatial arrangements of these state regions—disjoint, adjacent, or overlapping—the authors derive universal scaling laws. These results show that the measurement precision transitions between exponential decay and power-law behavior as the number of samples increases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical tools for quantum sensing and verification protocols, showing how measurement precision scales universally based purely on the geometric arrangement of allowed state parameters, which is relevant to optimizing quantum measurements in any field.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum state qualification, which is formulated as a composite quantum hypothesis test to determine if an unknown quantum state belongs to one of two predefined parameter regions.

**Main result.** It derives universal scaling laws for the minimum error probability ($P_{\min e}$) depending on whether the admissible regions are disjoint (exponential decay), adjacent (power law), or overlapping, identifying a second-order phase transition between these regimes.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum hypothesis testing theory, exploiting symmetries of the parameter space to simplify the optimal measurement structure and derive asymptotic expressions for $P_{\min e}$.

**Model / system.** The problem is generalized from specific examples like Polarization-Direction Qualification (PDQ) and Purity Qualification (PQ) applied to spin-1/2 states, treating the state parameters via Bloch vectors or general parameter regions.

**Key observables.** Minimum error probability ($P_{\min e}$), Quantum Chernoff divergence ($\xi$), and Quantum Fisher information ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of copies ($N$) is crucial, as are the geometric configurations of the state sets (disjoint, adjacent, overlapping).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies heavily on exploiting symmetries to find optimal measurements; for some cases, prior distributions are assumed to share specific symmetries like $SO(3)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate region configurations for PDQ and PQ (a-d) and numerically verify the derived scaling laws for $P_{\min e}$ across disjoint, adjacent, and overlapping regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the hypothesis test framework, analyzes specific physical examples (PDQ/PQ), derives the universal scaling laws based on region geometry, and concludes by characterizing the phase transition between these regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qualification of quantum states judges which of two sets of quantum states an unknown state lies in, where the two sets are labeled by two distinct parameter regions. We formulate this problem as a composite quantum hypothesis test and uncover universal scaling laws for the minimum error probability for $N$ copies. Taking polarization-direction qualification and purity qualification as examples, we show that the $N$-copy permutation symmetry and the geometric symmetries of the parameter regions identify the optimal measurements and the "worst pairwise states". The minimum error probability scales as $N^{-3/2}\exp(-Nξ)$ for disjoint regions and as $(NF)^{-1/2}$ for adjacent regions, where $ξ$ and $F$ are the quantum Chernoff divergence and quantum Fisher information associated with the "worst pairwise states", respectively. With the minimum error probability serving as an order parameter, the transition between the scaling behaviors becomes a second-order phase transition as $N\to\infty$. Our approach determines whether a quantum state belongs to a given set without full state tomography, thereby enabling qualification of large ensembles using finite samples.

</details>

### [Analytic correspondence between multipartite entanglement and quantum phase transitions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04467v1)

**Authors:** Huynh Le Dan Linh, Vu Tuan Hai, Le Bin Ho  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.04467v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.04467_figures/2608.04467_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Comparison between the normalized GOP e O (left), normalized CE eC (center), and their absolute difference | e O −eC| (right) for two representative one-dimensional spin models with N = 5: (a) the transverse-field Ising model, and (b) the generalized cluster-Ising model. In all cases, CE accurately reproduces the phase boundaries identified by the corresponding GOP. The normalized root-mean-square deviations are approximately 7% and 8%, respectively. The remaining discrepancies are localized predominantly near the critical manifold, while the agreement within each gapped phase is excellent, consistent with Theorem 1.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper establishes a deep theoretical link showing that multipartite entanglement (Concentratable Entanglement) behaves identically to traditional order parameters when characterizing Quantum Phase Transitions in 1D spin systems. By proving this correspondence and demonstrating its measurement feasibility via SWAP tests, it offers a universal, model-independent diagnostic tool for studying quantum criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, entanglement-based diagnostic tool that bypasses the need to calculate model-specific order parameters, making QPT detection more accessible for experimental quantum simulation platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a rigorous, analytic correspondence between multipartite entanglement measures and quantum phase transitions (QPTs) in 1D quantum spin systems.

**Main result.** It proves that Concentratable Entanglement (CE) shares the same analyticity structure as generalized order parameters (GOPs), identifies the same quantum phases, and serves as an experimentally accessible, model-independent probe for QPTs.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving theoretical theorems showing shared analytic dependence on single-particle correlation matrices ($\Gamma$) for Gaussian ground states, complemented by numerical validation on specific spin models.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional quantum spin systems, specifically the Transverse-Field Ising Model (TFIM) and generalized cluster-Ising models. The Hamiltonians are local spin-1/2 interactions parameterized by coupling constants ($\lambda$).

**Key observables.** Concentratable Entanglement ($C(S)$), Generalized Order Parameters (GOPs, $O(\lambda)$), and the single-particle correlation matrix ($\Gamma_{ij}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Control parameters $\lambda$ governing the Hamiltonian; critical points where phase transitions occur.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a unique gapped ground state within each quantum phase, and for free-fermion systems, it relies on the analyticity of the Bogoliubov transformation with respect to Hamiltonian parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares normalized GOP ($\langle O angle$) and normalized CE ($\langle C angle$) across the TFIM and cluster-Ising models, demonstrating that CE accurately reproduces the phase boundaries identified by GOPs.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical theorems establishing shared analyticity (Theorem 1), demonstrates phase discrimination capabilities using CE (Theorem 2), and proves an exact correspondence for Gaussian ground states based on $\Gamma$ (Theorem 3). This is validated numerically on TFIM and cluster-Ising models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive an analytic correspondence between multipartite concentratable entanglement (CE) and quantum phase transitions in one-dimensional quantum spin systems. We prove that CE shares the same analyticity structure as generalized order parameters, identifies the same quantum phases, and, for Gaussian ground states, is completely determined by the same single-particle correlation matrix. Numerical validation on the transverse-field Ising and generalized cluster-Ising models confirms these analytical predictions for both symmetry-breaking and symmetry-protected topological quantum phase transitions. Since CE can be measured directly using a constant-depth parallelized SWAP-test circuit, our results establish CE as an experimentally accessible, model-independent probe of quantum phase transitions without requiring model-specific order parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates when generalized contextuality is necessary for optimal strategies in sequential quantum state discrimination. By comparing Unambiguous State Discrimination (USD) and Minimum Error State Discrimination (MESD), the authors show that the requirement for contextuality changes depending on the protocol and the initial distinguishability of the states. The findings offer deep insights into the fundamental limitations imposed by quantum mechanics on multi-stage measurement processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical tools (contextuality measures) applicable to quantum measurement theory, which underpins many open quantum system dynamics and information processing tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the role of generalized contextuality in sequential quantum state discrimination protocols, comparing unambiguous (USD) and minimum error (MESD) settings.

**Main result.** Contextuality's presence depends critically on the protocol used (USD vs. MESD) and the distinguishability of the input states; for instance, contextuality is predicted to be more likely in sequential USD when states have low confusability.

**Method.** The analysis employs formalisms like COPE matrices, consistency relations derived from completeness relations, and ontological modeling to test violations of noncontextuality across multiple measurement stages.

**Model / system.** The system involves discriminating between a set of unknown quantum states (qubits) sequentially among $n$ players. The protocols analyzed are Minimum Error State Discrimination (MESD) and Unambiguous State Discrimination (USD).

**Key observables.** Success probabilities for USD ($P_{Q}^{USD}(n)$) and MESD, confusability ($s^2$), and post-measurement confusability ($t^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of players $n$, the state confusability $s^2$, and the measurement parameters defining detection operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes specific structures for POVM elements in MESD analysis, and relies on Spekkens' assumption relating operational indistinguishability to ontological equivalence.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 visualizes the feasible region for optimal measurements in single-player USD for two pure qubit states, showing the optimum relative to the boundary defined by confusability.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the single-player case (USD) using COPE formalism, extends this analysis to sequential players ($n>1$) by deriving consistency relations, and then compares contextuality findings between USD and MESD protocols, culminating in detailed ontological checks for MESD.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generalized contextuality is known to be required in optimal strategies for quantum state discrimination protocols. More recently, sequential discrimination tasks have been studied; n players attempt to determine in which state a qubit was prepared, in such a way that they all have a finite probability of success. We consider the extent to which contextuality plays a role in sequential versions of both unambiguous and minimum error discrimination. In the standard nonsequential case where n = 1, we use the COPE formalism to demonstrate that the presence of contextuality is guaranteed not only for the optimal measurement, but for a specific set of nonoptimal measurements as well. In the sequential case n > 1, we show that the presence of contextuality depends on which states are prepared, and on the protocol (unambiguous or minimum error) selected.

</details>

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work rigorously calculates the two-particle-irreducible vertex for the 2D $\phi^4$ model across its Ising phase transition. By combining Monte Carlo data with advanced functional methods, the authors pinpoint that the A1 channel drives the instability and provide a highly accurate benchmark for theoretical approximations of critical dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous diagrammatic techniques used to calculate correlation functions and vertices are foundational tools applicable to understanding quantum critical points in many-body systems relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study reconstructs and analyzes the two-particle-irreducible (2PI) vertex of the 2D lattice $\phi^4$ model as it passes through the Ising phase transition.

**Main result.** The instability driving the transition is governed by the A1 (ferromagnetic) channel, while the fully irreducible vertex can be accurately determined using advanced diagrammatic techniques like parquet equations.

**Method.** The authors combine Monte Carlo measurements of two-particle correlators with theoretical frameworks such as Bethe-Salpeter and Parquet/Schwinger-Dyson equations to extract the vertex function $\Gamma$.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional single-component $\phi^4$ lattice field theory, which belongs to the Ising universality class. The analysis tracks its behavior across the critical point.

**Key observables.** The leading eigenvalue of the symmetrized Bethe-Salpeter kernel; the momentum and real-space dependence of the 2PI vertex $\Gamma(k, p; q)$

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature/Inverse Temperature ($\beta$); lattice size ($L$); momentum transfer ($q$)

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on inverting Monte Carlo data and employing approximations like the Dynamical Local-Vertex Approximation (D$\Gamma$A) and solving parquet equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how leading eigenvalues approach unity at criticality, show $\Gamma(q)$ evolving from short-range to power-law tails near $T_c$, and map out convergence walls for theoretical approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first relating the vertex to measured correlators, analyzing its symmetry decomposition ($C_{4v}$), tracking the instability across phases (disordered $\to$ critical $\to$ ordered), and finally benchmarking results against advanced functional methods like parquet equations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We reconstruct the 2PI vertex $Γ(k,p;q)$ from Monte Carlo measurements of the connected two-particle correlator for the two-dimensional single-component $φ^4$ lattice field theory and follow it across the Ising transition. Resolving the vertex in the irreducible representations of the point group $C_{4v}$, we find that the instability is driven by the $A_1$ (ferromagnetic) channel at zero transfer, whose leading eigenvalue of the symmetrized Bethe--Salpeter kernel approaches unity. Substantial $B_1$ (nematic) and $B_2$ (diagonal nematic) contributions cooperate with $A_1$ across all system sizes, highlighting that the soft sector is   multidimensional. In real space, the vertex is short-ranged away from criticality while it develops a power-law tail at the critical point. In the ordered phase, the $q=0$ eigenvalue collapses because the ferromagnetic weight has condensed into the (one-particle-reducible) order parameter (or collective coordinate for a finite system), although finite-momentum fluctuations persist. By stripping the crossed-channel ladders, we obtain the fully irreducible vertex, which is a local contact -- to a very good approximation. Inserted into the parquet and Schwinger--Dyson equations, this contact reproduces the Monte Carlo self-energy with an accuracy better than one-tenth of a percent. This provides a first-principles benchmark of the dynamical local-vertex approximation (D$Γ$A). Additionally, we demonstrate that in the critical region, the physical solution of the parquet equations behaves as a repulsive fixed point, driven initially by a single order-parameter mode.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses muon spin relaxation ($\mu$SR) under uniaxial pressure to probe the quantum spin liquid candidate $	ext{PbCuTe}_2	ext{O}_6$. The researchers found that applying stress modifies the local magnetic response and enhances quasi-static correlations without inducing conventional long-range magnetic order. This demonstrates a powerful, symmetry-selective method for controlling frustrated exchange interactions in QSL materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to tune quantum magnetic ground states using external strain is highly relevant for understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated electron systems, providing a clean experimental handle on frustrated interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if uniaxial pressure can control the frustrated exchange landscape of a 3D Quantum Spin Liquid (QSL) candidate, $	ext{PbCuTe}_2	ext{O}_6$, and potentially drive it toward magnetic order.

**Main result.** The system remains in a dynamically fluctuating state even under high stress, but compression provides a clean route to tune the frustrated exchange landscape by exploiting magnetoelastic coupling. No evidence for conventional static long-range magnetic order was observed.

**Method.** Muon spin relaxation/rotation ($\mu$SR) measurements were performed *in situ* while applying controlled uniaxial compression along the [110] direction.

**Model / system.** The study investigates $	ext{PbCuTe}_2	ext{O}_6$, a candidate three-dimensional (3D) $S=1/2$ QSL characterized by competing antiferromagnetic interactions forming a frustrated 3D hyper-hyperkagome network.

**Key observables.** Muon spin relaxation rates ($\lambda_{	ext{fast}}, \lambda_{	ext{slow}}$), internal-field distribution broadening, and the stress-strain curve.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial stress $\sigma_{[110]}$ up to $37.7\ 	ext{MPa}$; critical stress $\sigma_{	ext{cr}} \sim 10.8\ 	ext{MPa}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed changes are due to magnetoelastic coupling between lattice strain and magnetic correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show ZF-$\mu$SR spectra, force-displacement curves correlating stress with $\mu$SR response, and temperature dependence of relaxation rates under varying uniaxial stresses.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures $\mu$SR properties across a range of applied uniaxial stresses, identifying critical structural transitions ($\sigma_{	ext{cr}}$) that correlate with changes in the local magnetic environment and spin dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report muon spin relaxation/rotation ($μ$SR) measurements of the candidate three-dimensional (3D) quantum spin liquid (QSL) PbCuTe$_2$O$_6$, hosting $S=1/2$ moments, under controlled in situ [110] uniaxial compression up to $σ_{[110]}=37.7$~MPa. A small directional lattice perturbation significantly modifies the local magnetic response, while above $σ_{\rm cr}\sim10.8$\,MPa the relaxation rates are strongly enhanced and the internal-field distribution is substantially broadened. These changes occur along with the local crystalline symmetry breaking. While, no evidence for conventional static long-range magnetic order is observed, the compression drives the system towards a structurally modified and strongly correlated state in which enhanced quasi-static correlations coexist with persistent slow spin dynamics. This work demonstrates a clean and symmetry-selective route to control frustrated exchange landscape and access hidden magnetic instabilities in a 3D QSL candidate opening up the possibilities to tune other correlated systems where intrinsic coupling between magnetic and lattice degrees of freedom are relevant.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors developed and experimentally demonstrated a novel, resource-efficient method to measure non-Abelian braiding and fusion properties for topological quantum computing using only local measurements on trapped ions. By implementing this on Quantinuum's H2 system, they achieved high fidelities, proving that complex anyonic operations can be simulated without physically moving the anyons.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a major bottleneck in topological quantum computation—the difficulty of accessing fundamental anyonic data—by proposing an entirely measurement-based protocol applicable to trapped-ion hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to reconstruct the defining non-Abelian braiding and fusion data for topological quantum computation without needing physical transport of anyons.

**Main result.** They successfully reconstructed these primitives with high fidelities ($\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{R}=0.9988$ and $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{F}=0.9987$), demonstrating measurement-only protocols for building larger topological quantum processors.

**Method.** The protocol uses a measurement-only approach based on temporally ordered ribbon operations, adapted Hadamard tests for braiding phases, and post-selected measurements for fusion amplitudes.

**Model / system.** The study models the system using the quantum double model $D(S_3)$ and implements the reduced two-qutrit version of the protocol on Quantinuum's H2 trapped-ion processors.

**Key observables.** Reconstructed squared braiding phases ($\langle (R+)^2 angle_{	ext{exp}}$) and fusion amplitudes ($F_{	ext{exp}}$); output-state fidelities $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{R}$ and $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{F}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The protocol is reduced to a two-qutrit implementation from an initial four-qudit overlap, utilizing logical qutrits ($	ext{g1}, 	ext{g2} \in \mathbb{Z}_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction relies on reducing higher-dimensional overlaps to effective two-qutrit operations and using specific input states for the Hadamard test.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the plaquette diagram of the reduced protocol, the spacetime sequence for braiding measurement (Fig. 1b), hardware circuits for testing phases via adapted Hadamard tests (Fig. 2), and circuit diagrams for fusion amplitude reconstruction (Fig. 3a).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework for non-Abelian anyons, presents the measurement-only protocol using ribbon operations on trapped ions, describes the specific circuits for measuring braiding phases via Hadamard tests, and concludes with high-fidelity results confirming the feasibility of these building blocks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Abelian anyons offer a route to fault-tolerant and universal quantum computing, but experimental access to their defining braiding and fusion data remains limited by the resource overhead of implementing extended anyonic processes on quantum hardware. Here we introduce and experimentally realise a measurement-only protocol based on temporally ordered ribbon operations that reconstructs the non-Abelian braiding and fusion primitives of the quantum double model $D(S_3)$ without physical anyon transport. We implement a reduced two-qutrit version of the protocol on Quantinuum's H2 trapped-ion processors, realising ancilla-assisted ribbon operations and anyonic charge projections in a qubit encoding. We reconstruct the squared braiding phases and fusion amplitudes using an adapted Hadamard test and post-selected measurements, respectively. The associated braiding and fusion transformations reproduce their ideal actions with average normalised output-state fidelities of $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{R}=0.9988$ and $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{F}=0.9987$. Combining these primitives produces a non-Clifford braid and a non-stabilizer resource state, supporting measurement-only anyonic encodings as building blocks for larger topologically encoded quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration of an optical-fibre integrated buffer designed for packet-switched quantum networks. By utilizing a poled fibre phase modulator within a recirculating loop, the system can store and retrieve polarization-encoded qubits while simultaneously reading out classical packet headers. This achievement establishes a practical, telecom-compatible architecture crucial for deploying the quantum internet.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations in quantum networking by providing a robust, low-loss, fibre-integrated component necessary for scaling beyond simple point-to-point links.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a fully fibre-integrated buffer for packet-switched quantum networks that can delay qubit payloads while allowing real-time readout of classical packet headers.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a practical, all-fibre architecture capable of storing and retrieving polarization-encoded qubits with storage times up to 47 $\mu$s and an average QBER of 1.8%.

**Method.** The system uses a recirculating loop incorporating an ultra-low-loss poled fibre phase modulator (PFPM) within a Sagnac interferometer setup.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an optical-fibre integrated buffer utilizing a recirculating resonator circuit. The key component enabling the functionality is the PFPM, which provides fast, polarization-insensitive switching directly in the fiber.

**Key observables.** Storage time (up to 47 $\mu$s), average quantum bit error rate (1.8%), and successful simultaneous header/payload processing.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit encoding (polarization), operational stability over several hours, and the required phase shift ($\phi=0$ or $\pi$) controlled by an electric field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that using in-fibre components overcomes limitations associated with bulk optics and high insertion losses of previous approaches.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates quantum network packet switching requiring buffering. Figure 2 details the poled fibre modulator's cross-section, interference curves, and time domain response.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for integrated quantum buffers, presents the physical realization using a recirculating loop and PFPM, demonstrates the storage/retrieval process with polarization encoding, and concludes by establishing its viability for telecom integration.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Packet-switched quantum networks require buffers that can delay qubit payloads while routing information is read out in real time. Previous approaches have not provided this functionality in a fully fibre-integrated architecture compatible with telecom infrastructure. Here we demonstrate an optical-fibre-integrated buffer, based on a recirculating loop and a fibre storage line, in which the storage time of a polarisation-encoded qubit payload is determined by readout of an attached packet header. The key component behind this achievement is an ultra-low-loss poled fibre phase modulator, which provides fast, polarisation-insensitive switching directly in fibre and allows header and payload to be processed within the buffer. We demonstrate storage and retrieval of polarisation-encoded qubit payloads for storage times up to 47 $μ$s, with an average quantum bit error rate of 1.8% together with stable operation over several hours. These results establish a practical fibre-based architecture for packet-level quantum network buffering that can easily integrate into the current telecommunication infrastructure opening up new paths for deployment of the quantum internet.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents comprehensive experimental data on off-stoichiometric CeCuBi2, revealing it to be an anisotropic Kondo antiferromagnet. The research demonstrates that the interplay between magnetic anisotropy and Kondo correlations leads to complex, field-tunable spin phases, which manifest as large, highly anisotropic magnetotransport responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** The co-existence of Kondo physics, strong magnetic anisotropy, and field-induced glassy behavior in a single material platform makes it highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated quantum materials where external fields can tune ground states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between field-induced magnetic phases, strong magnetic anisotropy, and anisotropic magnetotransport in off-stoichiometric CeCuBi2.

**Main result.** The material exhibits coexistence of Kondo correlations, long-range antiferromagnetism, and field-tunable spin configurations, leading to large room-temperature magnetoresistance and evidence of glassy spin dynamics.

**Method.** A combination of structural characterization (XRD), magnetic measurements ($\chi$, $C_P$, $	ext{M}(H)$), and angle-dependent magnetotransport measurements were employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is off-stoichiometric CeCuBi2 single crystals, which are modeled as an anisotropic Kondo antiferromagnet where the electronic behavior is governed by the interplay of CEF effects, RKKY exchange, and Kondo interactions.

**Key observables.** Antiferromagnetic ordering ($T_N \sim 14	ext{ K}$), weak heavy-fermion signature ($\gamma = 102$), multiple metamagnetic transitions, large magnetoresistance ($\sim 22\%$), and anisotropic magnetoresistance ($	ext{AMR} \sim 10.9\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (especially $T_N$ and $\sim 47	ext{ K}$), magnetic field strength ($H$) up to $9	ext{ T}$, and crystal orientation relative to the applied field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The off-stoichiometry, particularly Cu vacancies, is assumed to tune the local electronic environment and mediate complex exchange interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures confirm single-crystallinity along the c-axis via XRD/Laue patterns; magnetic measurements show $	ext{ZFC}/	ext{FC}$ behavior confirming AFM ordering and field-induced metamagnetism; transport data quantify anisotropy in resistance and magnetoresistance.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first characterizing the crystal structure and stoichiometry, followed by detailed analysis of magnetic properties (susceptibility, specific heat, $	ext{M}(H)$) to establish AFM order and heavy-fermion nature. It then focuses on magnetotransport measurements to link the complex spin states to measurable electronic transport anomalies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a detailed study on the structural, angle-dependent magnetic and magnetotransport properties of highly anisotropic off-stoichiometric CeCuBi2 single crystals. Our results reveal CeCuBi2 as an anisotropic Kondo antiferromagnet exhibiting complex field-induced magnetic behavior and unusual magnetotransport properties. Magnetic susceptibility and specific heat measurements reveal antiferromagnetic (AFM) ordering below TN = 14 K with strong anisotropy and weak heavy-fermion behavior. Electrical transport measurements show highly anisotropic resistivity and a broad hump around 47 K, indicative of Kondo-driven heavy-fermion behavior. Magnetization measurements reveal multiple field-induced metamagnetic phases, while AC susceptibility measurements indicate slow spin dynamics and spin-glass-like behavior in intermediate field-induced magnetic states. Furthermore, we observe large and strongly anisotropic magneto transport responses, including room-temperature magnetoresistance of approximately 22% at 300 K and 9 T and butterfly-like anisotropic magnetoresistance with AMR values reaching approximately 10.9%. These results highlight a strong interplay among Kondo correlations, magnetic anisotropy, and field-tunable spin configurations, making CeCuBi2 a possible platform for exploring correlated and anisotropic quantum phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel motional refocusing protocol that eliminates heating during the release-and-recapture cycle inherent to Rydberg gates. By precisely controlling the trapping light intensity, the authors restore the atomic motion to its initial state, enabling deeper and more reliable quantum circuits in optical tweezers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, theoretically rigorous method (intensity modulation) to overcome fundamental decoherence/heating limits in neutral atom quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Rydberg entangling gates suffer from motional heating due to the release-and-recapture cycle when trapping light is switched off, limiting circuit depth.

**Main result.** A motional refocusing protocol using only programmable trap intensity switching can exactly remove this heating in the harmonic approximation and suppress leading anharmonic corrections.

**Method.** The authors developed a time-optimal control sequence by promoting classical phase-space refocusing conditions to an exact quantum statement, analyzed via the Ermakov equation.

**Model / system.** The system involves neutral atoms trapped in optical tweezers executing Rydberg blockade gates. The dynamics are modeled using a three-dimensional harmonic potential whose evolution is governed by programmable trap intensity switching.

**Key observables.** Motional populations and coherences, heating ($\Deltaar{n}_{	ext{sud}}$), recapture probability ($P_{1d}(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trap frequencies ($\omega_i$), catch depth ($\Lambda$), gate duration ($T$), anharmonicity strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analysis assumes the harmonic approximation; later extensions consider finite ramps and anharmonic corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase-space dynamics (echo protocol) and show 1D recapture probabilities comparing quantum vs. classical predictions at various temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first develops the refocusing protocol in the harmonic approximation, proving its time-optimality. It then extends this theory to multiple modes and incorporates anharmonic corrections by deriving composite sequences. Finally, wave-packet simulations validate the analytic results for realistic Gaussian tweezers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rydberg entangling gates in optical-tweezer arrays are commonly executed with the trapping light switched off, so every gate contains a release--and--recapture cycle that heats the atomic motion and can ultimately limit circuit depth. We develop a motional refocusing protocol that exactly removes this heating in the harmonic approximation using only programmable intensity switching of the trapping light. The protocol closes the release--and--recapture cycle for every matched harmonic mode, returning arbitrary motional populations and coherences exactly up to ordinary evolution under the static trap. We derive the recovery sequence in closed form for arbitrary catch depth and prove that, within the experimentally relevant regime, it is the unique globally time-optimal solution under bounded trap intensity. The harmonic theory is then extended in two directions. First, we construct exact common-intensity recovery sequences that simultaneously refocus several nondegenerate harmonic modes, including radial--axial and fully anisotropic three-dimensional traps. Second, we derive a composite sequence that suppresses the leading anharmonic correction of weakly anharmonic traps by canceling all first-order motional transitions induced by the quartic anharmonicity, changing the residual heating law from $U_0^{-2}$ to $U_0^{-4}$. Wave-packet simulations in realistic Gaussian tweezers validate the analytic theory and quantify the residual effects of anharmonicity, finite switching ramps, trap ellipticity, and control errors. Applied to representative cesium Rydberg gates, the protocol suppresses the dominant recapture heating to the anharmonic floor and prevents the associated motional Doppler contribution from increasing with circuit depth. The resulting framework provides a practical route toward heating-free trap-off neutral-atom gates using only trap-intensity modulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates enhancing rotation sensing sensitivity using strongly interacting bosons trapped in a quasi-1D ring. By employing advanced MCTDH calculations, the authors demonstrate that strong interactions beneficially increase the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) at zero rotation rate, significantly outperforming simpler mean-field approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how strong quantum correlations can be harnessed in ultracold atomic systems to surpass classical or simple mean-field limits in precision sensing applications like gyroscopes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the ultimate quantum limit for rotation sensing using strongly interacting bosonic systems.

**Main result.** Self-consistent many-body calculations show that strong interactions can enhance the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) at zero rotation rate, contrary to mean-field predictions.

**Method.** The authors employ a fully self-consistent approach utilizing the Multi-Configurational Time-Dependent Hartree (MCTDH) method to compute the QFI of the full many-body eigenstates.

**Model / system.** The system consists of strongly interacting bosons confined in a quasi-one-dimensional ring trap with two weak links. The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating interaction terms and an angular momentum term related to rotation.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), which sets the ultimate precision limit for estimating the rotation rate ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction coupling strength ($\lambda_{1D}$ or $\gamma$), particle number ($N$), and the ratio of interaction to kinetic energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing the full MCTDH solution against simpler approximations like the two-mode Bose-Hubbard model, which is shown to be insufficient for accurate metrology predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QFI results between the full MCTDH method and the simplified Bose-Hubbard model across varying interaction strengths and particle numbers. One figure shows the dependence of QFI on lattice depth.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical setup (interacting bosons in a ring trap) and the metrological goal (maximizing QFI). It then develops the theoretical framework using MCTDH, contrasting its results against simpler mean-field models to demonstrate the non-trivial enhancement provided by strong correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the ultimate quantum limit of rotation sensing with a few strongly interacting bosons confined in a quasi-one-dimensional ring trap with two weak links. It is demonstrated that a self-consistent many-body solution of the problem is required to correctly predict the ultimate sensitivity of this strongly correlated many-body gyroscope to rotation. For both small rotation velocities and small particle numbers, the many-body quantum Fisher information becomes maximal for large interaction couplings, showing the potential of strongly interacting miniaturized many-body sensors to precisely estimate slow rotations with high spatial resolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes a novel lower bound on total quantum correlation for two-qubit systems by summing classical correlations measured in complementary bases. It proves this relationship holds due to unique geometric properties of qubits (binary entropy curvature). This has direct operational consequences, providing a tomography-free way to estimate entanglement and quantify the capacity of quantum channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, experimentally accessible diagnostic tool ($S_{XZ}$) that bypasses the need for full state tomography to quantify fundamental resources like entanglement and mutual information in qubit systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is establishing a rigorous, state-independent lower bound for the total quantum correlation (Quantum Mutual Information) when experiments can only measure correlations from limited, complementary measurement settings.

**Main result.** For two qubits, the sum of classical mutual informations measured in two complementary local bases provides a universal lower bound on the total quantum correlation, $I(A:B) \ge I(X_A:X_B) + I(Z_A:Z_B)$.

**Method.** The proof relies on analyzing the geometry of the Bloch ball using binary-entropy curvature and applying advanced information theory tools like relative-entropy data processing bounds.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses exclusively on two-qubit quantum states ($ho_{AB}$) and their correlations measured via Pauli bases (X, Z). The result is highly dependent on the dimensionality being exactly two.

**Key observables.** Quantum Mutual Information ($I(A:B)$), Classical Mutual Information from measurements ($I(X_A:X_B)$, $I(Z_A:Z_B)$), and the derived lower bound $S_{XZ}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the system (must be qubits, $d=2$), and the specific choice of complementary bases (e.g., X and Z).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is strictly valid for two-qubit systems; in higher dimensions ($d \ge 3$), complementary measurements can lead to overcounting.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, derives the mathematical inequality using entropy curvature arguments on the Bloch ball, and then applies this bound to operational tasks like certifying entanglement or bounding quantum channel capacity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extracting total correlations from a quantum system usually requires reconstructing its state, whereas many experiments access only a few measurement settings. A possible shortcut is to add the mutual informations obtained from complementary measurements; in dimensions above two, however, this procedure can count the same classical correlation twice. We establish that qubits are protected from such overcounting. For every two-qubit state, the correlations observed in two complementary local bases are bounded by the premeasurement quantum mutual information. The proof traces this protection to binary-entropy curvature on the Bloch ball and combines a qubit information-exclusion tradeoff with data processing under local dephasing. Consequently, two correlation tables give a tomography-free lower bound on total correlation. A score above one bit also certifies a quantitative one-way entanglement-distillation rate; when applied to the Choi state of a qubit channel, the same data lower bound its quantum capacity. The theorem therefore identifies both an operational use of complementarity and the trusted two-dimensional setting in which its correlation accounting is valid.

</details>
