**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the sharp rate at which Petz and sandwiched Rényi conditional entropies change when the input quantum states are slightly perturbed in trace distance. By employing advanced functional analysis techniques, it establishes optimal continuity bounds that generalize known results for the standard von Neumann entropy.

**Why it may be interesting.** Understanding how quantum information measures change smoothly with small perturbations is crucial for quantifying noise resilience and defining robust physical observables in quantum computation and communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the sharp modulus of continuity for optimized Petz and sandwiched Rényi conditional entropies when comparing two bipartite quantum states that are close in trace distance.

**Main result.** A concrete, optimal bound is derived for the difference between these conditional entropies based on the trace distance $\delta$ and the effective dimension $D$. Taking the limit $\alpha 	o 1$ recovers known sharp bounds for the von Neumann conditional entropy.

**Method.** The proof involves linearizing the concave Rényi functional at a comparison point, utilizing concepts like Schmidt-rank domination, and applying noncommutative calibration estimates derived from trace-distance duality.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns general bipartite quantum states ($ho_{AB}, \sigma_{AB}$) in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. The core mathematical tools involve Rényi entropy functionals applied to these states.

**Key observables.** Petz and sandwiched Rényi conditional entropies ($H^\uparrow_\alpha(A|B)$ and $eH^\uparrow_\alpha(A|B)$), the trace distance $\delta$, and the effective dimension $D$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entropy order parameter $\alpha \in [1/2, 1)$, and the trace distance $\delta \in [0, 1]$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** All Hilbert spaces are finite dimensional. The derivation relies on advanced matrix analysis techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares isotropic equality models with arbitrary-anchor constructions; Figure 2 illustrates the linearization of the Rényi functional $Q_\alpha(ho)$ at a comparison point $	au$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes foundational properties (concavity, linearity) for the entropy functionals using supporting operators. It then applies these tools to derive sharp continuity bounds for both Petz and sandwiched entropies, culminating in explicit formulas for the optimal bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the sharp modulus of continuity, in trace distance, of the optimized Petz and sandwiched Rényi conditional entropies for every order $α\in[\frac12,1)$. If two bipartite states are within trace distance $δ$, then both conditional entropies differ by at most $\frac{1}{1-α}   \log[(1-\varepsilon)^α   +(D-1)^{1-α}\varepsilon^α]$, where $\varepsilon := \min\{δ,1-1/D\}$ and $D$ is the effective dimension, given by the dimension of the first subsystem times the largest possible Schmidt rank. For every distance constraint $δ\in[0,1]$, the bound is attained by an isotropic pair with a maximally entangled anchor. Taking $α\uparrow1$ recovers the recent sharp continuity bound of quantum conditional entropy by Berta et al. [arXiv:2607.24687].   The proof linearizes the relevant concave Rényi functional at a comparison point dictated by the isotropic equality family. Schmidt-rank domination extends the equality geometry to an arbitrary anchor state, after which trace-distance duality and a noncommutative calibration estimate control the perturbation and anchor term without weakening the sharp constant. The latter estimate requires matrix analysis and is assisted by ChatGPT 5.6 Sol.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a novel, scalable quantum interconnect using neutral atom ensembles and Rydberg interactions to generate remote entanglement. By exploiting strong dipole-dipole coupling between atomic states, it achieves high entanglement generation rates ($\gtrsim 3 	imes 10^5$ s$^{-1}$) without requiring an optical cavity. This presents a promising pathway for building distributed quantum networks based on neutral atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a core challenge in quantum networking—creating fast, scalable entanglement links—by proposing an all-atomic/photonic mechanism that bypasses cavity requirements, which is highly relevant for experimental AMO physicists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing fast and scalable quantum interconnects necessary for building distributed quantum networks.

**Main result.** The proposed method achieves entanglement generation rates exceeding $3 	imes 10^5$ s$^{-1}$ by leveraging strong dipole-dipole interactions in neutral atom ensembles, avoiding the need for optical cavities.

**Method.** The analysis involves numerical simulations of qubit-photon dynamics governed by optical propagation equations in frequency space to model state-dependent photon operations.

**Model / system.** The system uses neutral atom ensembles (e.g., Ytterbium) where a single qubit interacts with propagating photons via strong dipole-dipole interactions between Rydberg states, mediating entanglement generation.

**Key observables.** Entanglement generation rate ($\Gamma_e$), transmission spectrum ($T_0$), and conditional reflection spectrum ($R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dipole-dipole interaction potential $V(r)$, Rabi frequencies ($\Omega_\pm$), decay rates ($\gamma_{p\pm}, \gamma_{r\pm}$), and optical depth ($O_{Db}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol relies on the ability to implement state-dependent photon operations using collective light-matter coupling in small atomic ensembles.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows entanglement generation via dipolar Rydberg interactions between atoms and photons. Figure 2 details level diagrams illustrating the coupling mechanism for ensemble and qubit atoms.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the concept by first describing the physical setup, then deriving the necessary Hamiltonian/equations of motion (Eqs. 1-3), simulating the dynamics to calculate performance metrics ($\Gamma_e$), and finally analyzing scaling laws based on optical depth.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributing entanglement between distant qubits is a crucial element of scalable quantum computing. Here, we describe a scalable quantum interconnect that generates remote entanglement at rates approaching those compatible with two-qubit gates of current neutral-atom quantum processors. The proposed approach exploits the strong dipole-dipole interactions between atomic Rydberg states to generate entanglement between stationary qubits and propagating photons, without the need for an optical cavity. We provide a thorough description of the optimal conditions for the developed entanglement-generation protocol for realistic experimental parameters and demonstrate that entanglement-generation rates $\gtrsim 3\times 10^5$ s$^{-1}$ can be achieved using Rydberg states of ytterbium atoms. Given the inherent scalability and design flexibility of the proposed interconnect, our results suggest a promising approach towards distributed networks based on neutral-atom quantum architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proves that structural chirality can emerge purely from the specific arrangement (ordering) of two different anion species within an otherwise achiral crystal lattice. Using DFT and advanced Monte Carlo simulations, the authors predict a stable chiral phase transition temperature for $	ext{NbO}_2	ext{F}$. This establishes configurational ordering as a robust mechanism for designing new functional chiral materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state chemistry, the use of advanced statistical mechanics tools like Wang-Landau sampling and cluster expansions to map out complex multi-component ordering phases is highly relevant for understanding frustrated quantum systems or lattice models in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines if structural chirality can be induced solely by the configurational ordering of two anion species over an achiral parent structure, independent of atomic displacements.

**Main result.** Configurational ordering is established as a chemically realizable route to structural chirality, with simulations predicting a stable chiral phase up to a first-order transition at 494 K in $	ext{NbO}_2	ext{F}$.

**Method.** The research employs direct enumeration, Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, and advanced Monte Carlo simulations (Wang–Landau) on cluster-expansion models.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is ReO$_3$-type oxyfluorides with a 2:1 anion stoichiometry (e.g., $	ext{NbO}_2	ext{F}$), analyzed using a $3 	imes 3 	imes 3$ supercell framework.

**Key observables.** Chirality order parameter ($\chi$), inter-chain coherence, OOF amplitude, and the phase transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical temperature $T_c = 494 \pm 1$ K; the stoichiometry of 2:1 anion ratio.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that both cis coordination and period-three chain ordering rules are energetically favored in the material.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the achiral parent structure, the labeling rules for enumeration, DFT energy comparisons between random and ordered states, and size-dependent signatures of a first-order phase transition (bimodality in energy distribution).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the combinatorial proof that specific anion orderings are inherently chiral, to using DFT to identify the ground state, and finally employing Monte Carlo simulations with finite-size scaling to predict the thermodynamic stability and critical temperature of the resulting chiral phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Established routes to structural chirality in inorganic crystals depend on symmetry-lowering atomic displacements of an achiral parent structure. We show that chirality can instead be forced by the ordering of two anion species over the sites of an achiral parent, independent of atomic displacements. ReO$_3$-type oxyfluorides with a 2:1 anion stoichiometry, such as NbO$_2$F, exhibit two ordering patterns: cis octahedral coordination and period-three anion-chain ordering, both rooted in the off-centring of $d^0$ cations. By direct enumeration, we prove that every configuration combining both orderings on the smallest commensurate cell is chiral, belonging to a Sohncke space group. For the specific case of NbO$_2$F, density-functional theory calculations predict that the ground state is the maximally symmetric chiral configuration. Wang-Landau Monte Carlo simulations of a DFT-trained cluster-expansion model predict an equilibrium chiral phase up to a first-order transition at 494 K. These results establish configurational ordering as a chemically realisable route to structural chirality, and mark heteroanionic materials with analogous ordering chemistry as candidates for a new class of chiral functional materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how correlated noise from a shared bosonic bath modifies qubit dynamics. By using the TCL2 framework, the authors show that cross-spectral correlations couple otherwise independent dephasing and relaxation channels. This coupling allows environmental noise to actively redistribute decoherence loss, creating temporary windows of enhanced coherence or suppressed energy decay.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses noise engineering in open quantum systems, showing how structured environmental coupling can be harnessed to temporarily protect or enhance coherence, which is critical for quantum memory and computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how cross-spectral correlations between longitudinal (dephasing) and transverse (relaxation) noise affect the dynamics of a qubit.

**Main result.** These correlations generate non-monotonic population relaxation and can induce a transient revival of coherence, allowing for temporal redistribution of decoherence loss.

**Method.** The analysis employs the second-order time-convolutionless (TCL2) framework to derive closed, time-local equations for the qubit's Bloch vector components.

**Model / system.** The model is a single qubit coupled to a shared bosonic bath via an interaction Hamiltonian. The environmental noise is characterized by a matrix-valued spectral density encoding cross-correlations between different noise channels.

**Key observables.** Bloch vector components ($\langle\sigma_xangle, \langle\sigma_yangle, \langle\sigma_zangle$), coherence revival magnitude ($\Delta C(t)$), and population relaxation modulation ($\Delta \langle\sigma_zangle_t$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cross-correlation strength ($\gamma_0$), spectral bandwidth ($\omega_c$), relative time delay ($	au$), phase offset ($\phi$), and bath temperature ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the weak-coupling regime, and the dynamics are derived using the Born approximation within the TCL2 framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate how coherence enhancement and relaxation suppression vary by changing correlation strength, spectral bandwidth, relative time delay, phase offset, and bath temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: introducing the model, deriving the master equation using TCL2, specializing to the qubit dynamics, parameterizing correlations, validating numerically against known limits, analyzing the effects of cross-correlations, and concluding with discussions on control parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the reduced dynamics of a qubit subject to correlated longitudinal and transverse noise arising from its coupling to a shared bosonic bath. The environmental fluctuations are characterized by a positive-semidefinite matrix-valued spectral density, whose complex off-diagonal elements encode correlations between dephasing and relaxation channels in the frequency domain. Within the second-order time-convolutionless framework, we derive closed time-local equations for the Bloch-vector components of the reduced density matrix. The numerical implementation is validated against the exact pure-dephasing solution and the established behavior of the transverse-coupling spin-boson model. When both noise channels are present, the cross-spectral terms couple the otherwise distinct dephasing and relaxation sectors, producing dynamics that cannot be reproduced by adding independent noise contributions. In particular, the correlations generate non-monotonic population relaxation and a transient revival of coherence following its initial decay. The strength, bandwidth, delay, and phase of the cross spectrum provide control parameters for the magnitude and temporal structure of these effects. Our results demonstrate that correlated multi-axis noise can redistribute coherence loss and energy relaxation in time, thereby providing finite temporal windows of enhanced coherence or suppressed relaxation within the weak-coupling regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies sophisticated variational principles from statistical physics to analyze the theoretical performance limits of a continuous-spin perceptron trained on structured data. By deriving matching upper and lower bounds for the quenched pressure, it provides a unified framework to calculate key learning metrics like generalization error and ground-state energy. This connects concepts from machine learning theory directly into the rigorous mathematical tools used in condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on classical ML models, the use of advanced statistical mechanics tools (variational methods, quenched averages, replica-like structures) mirrors techniques used in analyzing quantum many-body systems near criticality or in disordered quantum Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives variational bounds for the quenched pressure associated with Perceptron learning from structured data in the thermodynamic limit.

**Main result.** Lower and upper minimax variational bounds are established for the limiting quenched pressure, which match when optimization parameters commute, identifying a solution via a single variational principle.

**Method.** The authors employ a variational approach using interpolation techniques (adapted from Guerra and Toninelli) to bound the free energy/quenched pressure.

**Model / system.** The model is a continuous-spin perceptron trained on data generated by a Gaussian mixture, described by a random Hamiltonian $H_N(\sigma)$ acting on spins $\sigma \in \mathbb{R}^N$.

**Key observables.** Quenched pressure (limit of the log partition function), training loss ($\epsilon_t$), generalization error ($\epsilon_g$), and ground-state energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mixing strength ($\lambda$), centroid ($	heta$), regularization parameter ($\kappa$), and system size ($N, M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method requires specific hypotheses on the utility functions (concavity) and relies on log-concavity estimates; matching of outer optimizations is a key point.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical model and Hamiltonian, to introducing variational bounds via interpolation techniques, deriving stationarity conditions from a single potential, and finally applying this framework to compute multiple thermodynamic quantities like loss and error.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a variational approach to a finite-temperature continuous-spin perceptron trained on a Gaussian mixture. The model allows for a broad class of concave utilities and log-concave separable prior measures on the spins. By combining the interpolation method with log-concavity and concentration estimates, we derive lower and upper minimax variational bounds for the limiting quenched pressure. Remarkably, the two bounds differ only in the order of optimization of two variational parameters, while all remaining extrema are controlled by the concave--convex structure of the variational potential. Whenever the two optimizations commute, the two bounds match and identify the solution of the model. The same potential yields the fixed-point equations as stationarity conditions and provides a unified route to the computation of the ground-state energy, training loss, and generalization error.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced theoretical tool, the ghost-RISB method, to tackle the challenging physics of correlated electron-phonon interactions in models like Hubbard-Holstein. It successfully reproduces key results from computationally intensive DMFT while offering superior efficiency for mapping out complex phase diagrams, particularly concerning superconductivity suppression.

**Why it may be interesting.** The method provides a computationally cheaper alternative to DMFT for strongly coupled electron-phonon systems, allowing rapid phase diagram exploration that is critical for understanding materials like high-Tc superconductors or polaronic conductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a theoretical framework to accurately treat the complex interplay between electron-electron correlation (Hubbard repulsion) and local electron-phonon coupling in quantum materials.

**Main result.** The generalized ghost-RISB method achieves quantitative agreement with DMFT for key observables like quasiparticle weights, while being significantly more computationally efficient. It also predicts a suppression of the superconducting order parameter in the bipolaronic regime.

**Method.** The authors generalize the ghost-rotationally-invariant slave-boson (ghost-RISB) method by incorporating local phonon modes into the variational framework. This involves mapping the problem onto an enlarged Fock space and solving saddle-point equations derived from a Lagrangian formulation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Hubbard-Holstein model, which describes fermions coupled to local optical phonon modes via terms for hopping ($t$), on-site repulsion ($U$), phonon energy ($\omega_0$), and electron-phonon coupling ($g$).

**Key observables.** Electron quasiparticle weights (Z), superconducting order parameter, and charge-resolved phonon distributions ($P_2(X)$ and $P_0(X)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths are characterized by dimensionless ratios like $\lambda = 2g^2/\omega_0$. The analysis covers regimes ranging from weak to strong coupling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on mean-field decoupling for both bosonic and fermionic fields, and the final results are obtained by imposing static saddle point conditions on the derived Lagrangian.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares DMFT results with ghost-RISB calculations for the quasi-particle weight Z under varying electron-electron coupling strengths (U).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Hamiltonian, develops the generalized ghost-RISB formalism by mapping to an enlarged space and deriving a Lagrangian ($\mathcal{L}_{	ext{gRISB}}$), solves the resulting saddle point equations for matrix fields, benchmarks against DMFT, and finally applies the framework to analyze superconducting pairing in the bipolaronic regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a generalization of the ghost-rotationally-invariant slave-boson (ghost-RISB) method that incorporates local phonon modes coupled to arbitrary on-site electronic degrees of freedom, enabling a nonperturbative treatment of electron-electron and electron-phonon interactions within an efficient variational framework. The method extends the ghost-orbital construction to capture dynamical self-energy effects and phonon-induced renormalizations beyond static slave-boson approaches. Benchmarking against dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) results for the Hubbard-Holstein model, we find excellent quantitative agreement for electron quasiparticle weights and phonon properties across a wide range of coupling strengths, and it captures accurately the competition between electron-electron and electron-phonon interactions. We show that the inclusion of the ghost orbitals is crucial to accurately describe the regime of low-frequency, strongly dynamical, phonons. The extended ghost-RISB achieves this accuracy at a fraction of the computational cost of DMFT, due to its self-consistency rooted in static observables instead of dynamical ones, enabling rapid exploration of correlated electron-phonon phase diagrams. We exploit this advantage to characterize the most demanding regime of strong coupling and adiabatic phonons. Our analysis shows a suppression of the superconducting order parameter, which is interpreted as a Franck-Condon-like reduction of the overlap between the phonon wavefunctions associated with empty and doubly-occupied sites in the bipolaronic regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the memory effects generated by the quantum SWITCH, a mechanism enabling indefinite causal order. By employing advanced diagnostics comparing standard and extended mutual information, the authors demonstrate that any observed non-Markovianity is not genuinely quantum. This forces a critical re-examination of which physical resources truly provide quantum advantages in such protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists working on open quantum systems because it provides a rigorous, advanced diagnostic tool (the $I(R; QF)$ test) to filter out spurious non-Markovian signatures that might otherwise lead to overestimation of quantum advantages in complex protocols like indefinite causal order.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine if memory effects (non-Markovianity) generated by the quantum SWITCH are genuinely quantum or merely artifacts arising from classical correlations.

**Main result.** The research concludes that the observed memory effects in the quantum SWITCH dynamics are not genuinely quantum non-Markovian, suggesting a re-evaluation of the physical resources underpinning indefinite causal order advantages.

**Method.** The authors analyze two scenarios (discrete and continuous time) using advanced diagnostics like CP-divisibility and comparing standard mutual information ($I(R; Q)$) against an extended environment mutual information ($I(R; QF)$).

**Model / system.** The core model is the quantum SWITCH, which implements indefinite causal order by coherently superposing different orders of quantum channels acting on a system coupled to a control qubit.

**Key observables.** Non-Markovian information backflow, Quantum Mutual Information $I(R; Q)$, and the extended mutual information $I(R; QF)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis distinguishes between genuine quantum non-Markovianity (requiring specific monotonicity in $I(R; QF)$) and classical sources of memory.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary diagnostic tool relies on the assumption that true quantum non-Markovianity must manifest as a monotonic decrease when the environment is extended with an inert system ($F$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between $I(R; Q)$ (showing non-monotonic revival) and $I(R; QF)$ (showing monotonic decay) for both discrete and continuous time settings, confirming the classical origin of apparent memory.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the quantum SWITCH in both discrete-time evolution and continuous-time dynamical maps. The core logic involves applying various non-Markovianity measures and ultimately using the extended environment diagnostic ($F$) to distinguish genuine quantum effects from classical ones.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Indefinite causal order extends quantum information processing beyond fixed causal structures, with the quantum SWITCH serving as its canonical realization. By coherently superposing different orders of quantum channels, the quantum SWITCH has been shown to provide operational advantages in communication, computation, metrology, and related tasks. Despite these advances, the physical resources responsible for these advantages remains unclear. Recent studies have further revealed that the quantum SWITCH can generate memory effects, manifested as non-Markovian information backflow. In this work, we examine the origin of such memory and determine whether they reflect genuine (quantum) non-Markovianity or instead arises from classical origin. To this end, we analyze two representative scenarios: one based on discrete-time evolution and another formulated through dynamical maps in open quantum systems. We show that the memory effects generated by the quantum SWITCH are not genuinely quantum non-Markovian, thereby prompting a re-examination of the source of quantum advantage in indefinite causal order frameworks.

</details>

**Authors:** Jacob A. Barandes

**Summary.** This paper develops two theorems connecting stochastic matrices to quantum probability theory. It shows that any entry in a stochastic matrix can be written as a trace involving generalized quantum measurement operators (POVMs and PVMs). Crucially, it establishes that these processes are fundamentally linked to unistochastic matrices, suggesting that unitary evolution dictates the structure of all discrete-time probabilistic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical bridge between classical Markovian dynamics and quantum unitary evolution by showing how general probabilistic processes are constrained by structures related to unitaries (unistochastic matrices).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish deep mathematical connections between stochastic matrices, which model classical probabilistic processes (Markov chains), and the probability rules derived from quantum mechanics.

**Main result.** The paper proves that any entry of a stochastic matrix can be expressed as a trace involving POVMs and PVMs (Born Representation Theorem), and further shows that these entries are fundamentally constrained by unistochastic matrices, suggesting a primacy of unitary evolution structures.

**Method.** Constructive proofs utilizing properties of Positive-Operator-Valued Measures (POVMs) and Projection-Valued Measures (PVMs) within the framework of trace formulas.

**Model / system.** The model centers on stochastic matrices $\Gamma$ describing conditional probabilities $p(i|j)$ in discrete-time Markov chains. These are related to quantum systems via PVMs $\{P_j\}$ and POVMs $\{E_i\}$, which generalize the standard Born rule.

**Key observables.** The entries of the stochastic matrix $\Gamma_{ij}$, representing conditional probabilities, and their representation as traces involving quantum measures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions $M 	imes N$ of the stochastic matrix; the structure of the underlying POVMs and PVMs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that classical probabilistic processes can be mapped onto structures derived from quantum measurements (POVM/PVM formalism).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Born Representation Theorem, showing $\Gamma_{ij} = 	ext{tr}(E_i P_j)$. It then proves a corollary relating this to unistochastic matrices. Finally, it presents the Unistochastic Theorem, demonstrating that any stochastic matrix can be derived from marginalizing a larger unistochastic structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper presents self-contained, constructive proofs of two new theorems about stochastic matrices, with direct relevance to quantum theory. The first theorem, herein called the Born Representation Theorem, shows that each entry of any stochastic matrix can be expressed as the trace of a pairwise product of matrices, where the first factor in the pairwise product belongs to a positive-operator-valued measure (POVM) and the second factor belongs to a projection-valued measure (PVM). As its name suggests, this theorem entails that the entries of any stochastic matrix can be expressed in terms of a generalized version of the quantum-theoretic Born rule. It follows as a corollary that if the POVM in this first theorem is a PVM, then the stochastic matrix is unistochastic, meaning that its entries are each the modulus square of the corresponding entry of a unitary matrix of the same size. The second theorem proved in this paper, called the Unistochastic Theorem, then shows that by dilating the underlying vector space by a bounded number of additional dimensions if necessary, each entry of any stochastic matrix can be expressed in terms of the trace of a pairwise product for which both factors belong to PVMs, and can thus be derived via marginalization from a larger unistochastic matrix. This second theorem therefore establishes a kind of primacy of unistochastic matrices over stochastic matrices, and hints at a close connection with unitary time evolution in quantum theory. The paper concludes with a brief discussion of potential applications to discrete-time deterministic processes and Markov chains.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a protocol called SAFE to protect superconducting cavities from dephasing caused by flux noise inherent in nonlinear Josephson elements. By applying a weak off-resonant microwave drive, the system can be tuned to a dynamical sweet spot where the cavity frequency becomes insensitive to low-frequency noise. This significantly enhances coherence times, improving fidelity for quantum control applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses decoherence mitigation in superconducting quantum circuits by engineering noise resilience through external driving fields, which is central to modern quantum information processing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is mitigating cavity dephasing caused by low-frequency flux noise originating from coupled nonlinear Josephson elements.

**Main result.** The proposed Stark-Assisted Flux-noise Evasion (SAFE) protocol successfully creates a dynamical sweet spot, extending the cavity dephasing time by over an order of magnitude.

**Method.** The authors use Floquet theory and master equation analysis to derive conditions for noise insensitivity, implementing operator transformations and secular approximations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a superconducting microwave cavity dispersively coupled to a flux-tunable transmon (FTT), analyzed under the influence of $1/f$ flux noise.

**Key observables.** Cavity dephasing time ($T_2^*$), susceptibility ($D_{n,m}$), and residual decoherence rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak off-resonant microwave drive strength ($\Omega_0$), detuning ($\Delta_{bc}$), and the noise spectral density ($S_{1/f}(\omega)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on weak driving approximations, dispersive coupling regimes, and truncating the system dynamics to a manageable subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the creation of dynamical sweet spots in parameter space (Fig. 2) and show how the drive compensates for noise-induced shifts.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the noise source and its effect on the Hamiltonian, to deriving the condition for a dynamical sweet spot using Floquet theory, and finally modeling the dynamics via an effective master equation incorporating the protective drive.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-coherence superconducting cavities offer a promising platform for quantum information, with long coherence times and negligible intrinsic dephasing. However, cavity control generally relies on nonlinear Josephson elements whose frequency fluctuations are inherited by the cavity as dephasing, potentially limiting control fidelities and eroding the noise bias used in error-correction protocols. Here, we introduce Stark-Assisted Flux-noise Evasion (SAFE), a hardware-efficient protocol that protects the cavity from inherited dephasing using only a weak off-resonant microwave drive applied to the nonlinear element. As a concrete setup, we analyze a 3D superconducting cavity dispersively coupled to a flux-tunable transmon (FTT) subject to $1/f$ flux noise. Analytical predictions are confirmed by Monte Carlo simulations with realistic parameters, which show that SAFE can extend the cavity dephasing time by more than an order of magnitude while keeping residual drive-induced decoherence subdominant.

</details>

**Authors:** Kai Shinbrough, Donovan J. Webb, Iver R. Øvergaard, Oana Băzăvan, Sebastian Saner, Christopher J. Ballance, Raghavendra Srinivas

**Summary.** This paper details how generalized squeezing interactions can be harnessed to realize several key applications in hybrid spin-oscillator quantum processors. The research focuses on developing fast and high-fidelity methods for both discrete and continuous variable quantum computation and sensing. By leveraging the geometric phase inherent in these interactions, it establishes a robust theoretical framework for next-generation quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses advanced control techniques (generalized squeezing) in hybrid systems, providing concrete protocols for implementing complex quantum gates beyond standard pairwise interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work proposes four novel applications for utilizing spin-dependent generalized squeezing interactions to achieve fast, high-fidelity quantum computation and sensing.

**Main result.** These applications demonstrate that the geometric phase induced by generalized squeezing provides a foundation for advanced discrete- and continuous-variable quantum processing in hybrid platforms.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving time evolution using the Magnus expansion applied to Hamiltonians describing spin-oscillator coupling, leading to engineered gates and interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid spin-oscillator platform, modeled by an interaction Hamiltonian involving collective spin operators ($\hat{J}_{	heta,\phi}$) and bosonic ladder operators ($\hat{a}, \hat{a}^\dagger$).

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity, gate speedup ($S(k)$), Wigner function trajectories, and the effective Rabi frequency for N-body interactions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction order $k$ of the generalized squeezing, the initial Fock state occupation ($n_i$), and detunings ($\delta_j$) are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations rely on truncating the Magnus expansion (e.g., to second order), and the set of applications discussed is noted as non-exhaustive.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates Wigner function trajectories comparing $k=1$ (displacement) and $k=2$ (squeezing) gates, alongside plots showing gate speedup versus initial Fock state occupation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates four applications: geometric phase gates for two spins/one oscillator; genuine N-body spin interactions; oscillator thermometry via spin readout; and high-fidelity quantum state preparation of the oscillator.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generalized squeezing interactions are foundational to quantum optics, and have recently come under experimental control in hybrid spin-oscillator quantum processors [O. Băzăvan, et al., Nat. Phys. 22, 757 (2026); S. Saner, et al., Phys. Rev. X 16, 021049 (2026)]. These interactions open the door for new applications in the processing of discrete- and continuous-variable quantum information, four of which we propose and investigate in this work: geometric phase gates mediated by spin-dependent generalized squeezing acting on two spins and a common oscillator; genuine N-body spin interactions mediated by individually addressed spin-dependent generalized squeezing; oscillator thermometry via spin readout; and the preparation of high-fidelity quantum states of the oscillator via spin-dependent generalized squeezing and mid-circuit measurement. A unifying feature of these applications is the geometric phase induced by generalized squeezing interactions, which is nonlinear in the Fock occupation of the oscillator and the interaction order of the generalized squeezing. This work provides a foundation for fast, high-fidelity discrete- and continuous-variable quantum computation and sensing in the hybrid spin-oscillator platform via generalized squeezing.

</details>

### [Quantum annealers as programmable thermal machines](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04564v1)

**Authors:** Jakub Pawłowski, Tomasz Śmierzchalski, Fengping Jin, Bartłomiej Gardas, Sebastian Deffner, Zakaria Mzaouali

**Summary.** The paper develops a thermodynamic framework to analyze quantum annealers by treating them as closed thermal cycles. It uses fluctuation theorems to calculate energy exchange quantities like work and entropy production from measured energy changes. This allows researchers to diagnose the underlying physical process—whether it is net heating, cooling, or controlled work extraction—providing deeper insight than performance metrics alone.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous connection between quantum computation performance metrics (like optimization success) and fundamental principles of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, offering new diagnostics for open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize programmable quantum annealers as closed thermodynamic cycles to assess performance beyond simple solution quality or runtime metrics.

**Main result.** By applying fluctuation theorems, the authors derive lower bounds on entropy production, work, and heat exchange, revealing operational regimes (heater, refrigerator, etc.) that quantify energy flow during computation.

**Method.** The methodology involves treating the annealer as a thermodynamic system undergoing reverse annealing cycles, using initial/final Ising energies and effective temperature fits to calculate these thermodynamic bounds.

**Model / system.** The primary physical platform is the D-Wave quantum annealer (and Advantage hardware), modeled via an Ising Hamiltonian. The analysis applies to 1D chains and higher connectivity instances.

**Key observables.** Lower bounds on entropy production ($\Sigma$), Work ($W$), Heat ($Q$), Power ($P$), and the effective environment temperature ($T_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial inverse temperature ($eta_1$), annealing parameter ($s_p$), cycle time ($	au$), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fluctuation identities (like TURs) to derive bounds from limited measurements, and the extracted $T_2$ is an effective temperature of sampled degrees of freedom, not a direct hardware measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental workflow using reverse annealing cycles; one shows finite size scaling of work/power bounds for 1D chains, another maps operating modes in the $(eta_1, s_p)$ plane, and others show thermodynamic scans varying initial temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the thermodynamic framework by defining the cycle, applies this to measure energy exchange on D-Wave hardware across different annealing paths, classifies the operational regime based on input parameters ($eta_1, s_p$), and concludes with device-agnostic diagnostic tools.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable quantum annealers are used for optimization, probabilistic sampling, and simulation, but their performance is commonly reported without the energy exchanged during computation. Here we characterize the D-Wave quantum annealer as a closed thermodynamic cycle. From initial and final Ising energies and an effective temperature fitted to the output distribution, we obtain lower bounds on entropy production, environment energy exchange, work, and power. By varying the prepared distribution and the reverse annealing turning point, we map heater-, accelerator-, refrigerator-, and engine-compatible regimes in one dimensional chains and higher connectivity instances, and apply the same analysis to Advantage and Advantage2 hardware. For an encoded optimization problem, the measured processor energy change states whether final candidates improve or worsen the programmed objective on average. For sampling, the fitted temperature provides an operational measure of how strongly probability is concentrated among low energy configurations. The thermodynamic mode therefore adds information absent from solution quality or runtime alone: it distinguishes driven refinement, net heating, and heat pumping while quantifying their energetic consequences. This framework connects quantum optimization, probabilistic computing, statistical physics simulation, hardware diagnostics, and energy-aware assessment without assuming that a thermodynamic label alone determines computational performance.

</details>

### [Quantum Bayes Correlated Equilibrium and the Comparison of Quantum Information Structures in Games](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04973v1)

**Authors:** Furkan Sezer  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.04973v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.04973_figures/2608.04973_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1. Maximum average welfare per player over QBCE(G, Qγ) along the de- polarizing chain of Proposition 10.1, each marker being the optimal value of a semi- definite program. Informativeness increases with γ, so the structures are ordered from least informative on the left to most informative on the right. The value is non-increasing, as Theorem 9.4 requires: more information admits fewer equilibrium outcomes and hence a lower best case. It equals 4/3, the maximum over BCE(G, S∅), for γ ≲0.33, where the obedience constraints are slack, and falls to 1 at γ = 1. The first-best value 14/9 is never attained, because joint investment in the bad state is not obedient</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work quantizes the comparison of information structures by defining Quantum Bayes Correlated Equilibrium (QBCE). It shows that more detailed information, modeled via a parameter $\gamma$, systematically shrinks the set of possible equilibrium outcomes and lowers the maximum achievable welfare. The methodology relies on translating complex quantum constraints into computable semidefinite programming problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO systems, the mathematical tools—such as using operator inequalities (Loewner order) to constrain quantum decision processes—are highly relevant for modeling open quantum systems or measurement back-action in quantum control theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper builds a quantum analogue of comparing information structures used in games of incomplete information, aiming to quantify how much more informative one structure is compared to another.

**Main result.** It establishes that under quantum individual sufficiency, increasing the available information (via the mediator's observation) shrinks the set of possible Quantum Bayes Correlated Equilibrium outcomes and reduces the maximum attainable welfare in every game.

**Method.** The authors characterize obedience using a Loewner domination condition between operators on a player's subsystem, allowing the equilibrium set to be computed as a compact spectrahedron via semidefinite programming (SDP).

**Model / system.** The model is an extension of classical Bayesian games into quantum settings, where private information is encoded in subsystems and observed by a mediator. The analysis uses general quantum information structures defined by families of density operators.

**Key observables.** The equilibrium set (a non-empty compact spectrahedron), the maximum average welfare achievable over the QBCE set, and various comparison relations ($\preceq_{	ext{QIS}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** A depolarizing parameter $\gamma \in [0, 1]$ is used to interpolate between different information structures, controlling the amount of noise or information retained.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining quantum individual sufficiency and comparing structures via Loewner domination; exact equivalence with classical constraints holds only under specific conditions (e.g., simultaneous diagonalizability).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the maximum average welfare per player as a non-increasing function of the depolarizing parameter $\gamma$, showing plateaus and subsequent drops.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the QBCE concept, proves the Loewner characterization for obedience, establishes that the equilibrium set is an SDP-computable spectrahedron, and finally proves the comparison theorem by analyzing how information structure parameters ($\gamma$) affect welfare maximization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bergemann and Morris (2016) show that one information structure is more informative than another exactly when it induces a smaller set of Bayes correlated equilibrium outcomes in every game. We build the quantum analogue. An information structure becomes a family of density operators indexed by the payoff state, which the mediator observes. We show that obedience is equivalent to a Loewner domination between operators on one player's subsystem. The equilibrium set is then a nonempty compact spectrahedron computable by semidefinite programming, classical structures embed exactly, and under quantum individual sufficiency more information shrinks the equilibrium set in every game.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models liquid drainage through porous media by comparing two theoretical frameworks: standard invasion percolation (IP) and pressure-controlled drainage modeled as bond percolation with trapping (BPT). The key finding is that BPT predicts a finite residual saturation in the infinite limit, unlike IP. The work uses scaling laws to characterize how these differences manifest across various dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on fluid dynamics in porous media, the rigorous application of percolation theory, fractal geometry, and finite-size scaling laws connects to critical phenomena studied extensively in statistical mechanics, which shares mathematical tools with quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares residual saturation under pressure-controlled drainage versus standard invasion percolation theory.

**Main result.** Pressure-controlled drainage leads to a finite, non-zero residual saturation in the infinite limit, contrasting with invasion percolation's prediction that the invaded phase saturation vanishes asymptotically.

**Method.** The process is modeled using bond percolation with trapping (BPT) on a pore-network graph and analyzed by comparing its results against standard invasion percolation (IP).

**Model / system.** The physical system is liquid invasion into porous media, modeled via a pore-network graph. The theory compares two drainage mechanisms: IP (local advancement) and BPT (pressure-controlled filling with trapping).

**Key observables.** Invasion-phase saturation ($S_I$), residual saturation ($S_r$), and finite-size scaling exponents ($\delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pore network size ($L$), dimensionality (2D vs 3D), and bond occupancy ($p$) related to capillary pressure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting power laws to determine asymptotic behavior, and pore-shape effects are noted as remaining important for quantitative comparison with experiments.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare BPT and IP saturation curves on lattices; one figure shows snapshots illustrating the difference in invaded regions; another details residual saturation scaling across different lattice sizes and dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by formulating drainage as bond percolation with trapping, derives key results contrasting it with invasion percolation (especially regarding finite-size scaling), and analyzes dimensionality dependence through simulations on various lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here, pressure-controlled drainage is formulated as bond percolation with trapping on the pore-network graph, establishing a direct connection between percolation theory and pressure--saturation relations. In two dimensions, the deviation of the residual saturation from its non-vanishing thermodynamic limit obeys a finite-size scaling law with exponent $δ\approx 0.25$, independent of microscopic details of the lattice. In three dimensions, finite-size corrections decay more rapidly ($δ\approx 0.75$), while the asymptotic residual saturation remains finite and depends on coordination number. This extends the standard invasion-percolation picture beyond the breakthrough state, where the invading cluster is fractal and the invaded-phase saturation vanishes in the infinite-size limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present an experimental platform built from electronic quadrature oscillators designed to model coupled oscillator systems governed by the Kuramoto model. By employing a novel non-diffusive coupling circuit, they successfully demonstrate that their hardware can accurately reproduce phase transitions and synchronization behaviors across both weak and strong coupling regimes. This offers a cost-effective tool for systematically validating complex theoretical models of collective dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a tangible, low-cost hardware testbed for complex collective dynamics models (like Kuramoto), allowing experimental validation of theoretical predictions in parameter regimes previously inaccessible due to weak-coupling assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Connecting analytical results from the Kuramoto model, derived via phase reduction, to experimental data is challenging because many physical platforms violate the weak-coupling assumptions.

**Main result.** The proposed electronic platform successfully reproduces Kuramoto model dynamics for both weak and strong coupling regimes across various network topologies.

**Method.** The authors designed an experimental circuit using electronic quadrature oscillators coupled via a novel non-diffusive architecture to map oscillator dynamics onto the required sinusoidal phase coupling.

**Model / system.** The system uses electronic quadrature oscillators whose phase dynamics are engineered to mimic the Kuramoto model ($\dot{\phi}_i = \omega_i + K \sum A_{ij} \sin(\phi_j - \phi_i)$). The coupling is achieved using external currents in a non-diffusive circuit.

**Key observables.** Phase coherence, order parameter (R), and observation of phase transitions (global and cluster synchronization).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($K$), network topology (e.g., ring, all-to-all, directed), frequency mismatch ($\Delta\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The platform aims to achieve equivalence without relying on conventional weak-coupling phase-reduction assumptions; however, the initial coupling implementation is noted as being inherently diffusive.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate validation of synchronization transitions for different topologies (symmetric, all-to-all, ring, directed) and illustrate the $(K, \gamma)$-phase diagram for cluster synchronization.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical challenge, details the quadrature oscillator platform and non-diffusive coupling circuit, demonstrates its ability to reproduce Kuramoto phase transitions across various regimes, and concludes by outlining future extensions like implementing signed networks or adaptive control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase reduction provides a principled relationship between high-dimensional oscillator dynamics and low-dimensional phase models such as the Kuramoto model. However, connecting analytical results derived from the Kuramoto framework to experimental data remains an open challenge, as many physical platforms violate the weak-coupling assumptions underlying standard phase reduction. Here, we present an experimental platform based on electronic quadrature oscillators whose phase dynamics are, under certain assumptions, equivalent to the Kuramoto model. Crucially, this equivalence is achieved for both weak and strong coupling through a non-diffusive coupling circuit, and the design approach supports arbitrary (weighted and directed) network topologies while preserving scalability and oscillation regularity. Across different coupling schemes, we demonstrate that the platform reliably reproduces phase transitions predicted by the Kuramoto model for both global and cluster synchronization. This cost-effective platform enables systematic experimental validation of analytical and numerical results for synchronization in complex networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies concepts from equilibrium and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics by showing that the Gibbs variational principles are mathematically equivalent to the Boltzmann irreversible theorems. Using stochastic evolution equations, it derives bounds on entropy increase and free energy decrease for both isolated and open quantum/classical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical link between equilibrium statistical mechanics (variational principles) and non-equilibrium dynamics (irreversibility theorems), which is fundamental for understanding relaxation processes in complex quantum or classical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes Gibbs variational principles for systems isolated or at constant temperature, aiming to connect these principles rigorously to the Boltzmann irreversible theorems.

**Main result.** It establishes that both the first (entropy increase) and second (free energy decrease) Boltzmann irreversible theorems are mathematically equivalent to the corresponding Gibbs variational inequalities ($S_m \ge S$ and $F_c \le F$).

**Method.** The analysis uses stochastic evolution equations, specifically a modified Boltzmann-Kolmogorov equation, alongside thermodynamic derivations involving free energy minimization.

**Model / system.** The primary model studied is the Axial-Next-Nearest-Neighbor Ising (ANNNI) model on a cubic lattice. The framework extends to general open systems interacting with thermal reservoirs.

**Key observables.** Entropy ($S$), Free Energy ($F = U - TS$), and the time evolution of these quantities ($\mathrm{d}S/\mathrm{d}t$ and $\mathrm{d}F/\mathrm{d}t$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction coupling constants ($J_1, J_2$) defining the ANNNI Hamiltonian; temperature $T$; and system volume/size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of irreversible theorems relies on assuming specific forms for transition rates (detailed balance) and utilizing inequalities like Jensen's inequality.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the analysis involves deriving equations of state and phase diagrams for the ANNNI model using variational methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining Gibbs principles for isolated/canonical systems, then introducing a generalized Boltzmann-Kolmogorov equation to describe time evolution. It subsequently derives the irreversible theorems ($\mathrm{d}S/\mathrm{d}t \ge 0$ and $\mathrm{d}F/\mathrm{d}t \le 0$) and finally proves their equivalence to the variational bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze the Gibbs variational principles associated with the probability distributions of (i) an isolated system and (ii) a system at constant temperature. We give an example of using the Gibbs inequality to obtain the free energy and analyze the phase diagram of an Ising model with competing interactions. We also review the Boltzmann irreversible theorem, and show how it is connected to the Gibbs variational principles. This connection is established by using the Kolmogorov equation for the evolution of the probability distribution, which predicts a monotonic increase of entropy for an isolated system, and a decrease of the free energy for a system in contact with a thermal reservoir.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how non-Gaussian photon states generated via subtraction, addition, or catalysis can enhance phase estimation in an SU(1,1) interferometer. While these operations show promise under certain conditions, the authors conclude that for practical resource constraints, optimized Gaussian inputs still outperform them. The work establishes a critical boundary between theoretical quantum enhancement and experimentally achievable precision.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative boundary condition for quantum metrology, showing that theoretical potential (high local QFI) does not always translate into achievable precision when realistic measurement limitations (like parity detection or fixed resources) are imposed.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the metrological advantage of using heralded non-Gaussian squeezed-state inputs (via photon subtraction, addition, or catalysis) versus standard Gaussian inputs in a parity-detection SU(1,1) interferometer for phase estimation.

**Main result.** While single-photon PS and PA improve conditional information at high transmissivity, under strict resource constraints, the optimized Gaussian benchmark remains superior to all tested non-Gaussian operations. Photon catalysis shows promise only in low-transmissivity regimes.

**Method.** The authors developed a unified finite-transmissivity map to calculate conditional moments and Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for these non-Gaussian inputs within the interferometer framework.

**Model / system.** The system is an SU(1,1) interferometer where one mode receives a coherent state $|\alphaangle$ and another receives a squeezed vacuum $|\xiangle$. Non-Gaussian states are heralded by projecting ancillary modes using photon subtraction (PS), addition (PA), or catalysis (PC).

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for phase estimation, conditional moments ($\langle \hat{O} angle^{(m)}_j$), and the dark-point parity observable.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivity $T$, squeezing parameter $r$, interferometer gain $g$, and the specific type of heralding operation (PS, PA, PC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conclusion is highly constrained by fixed preparation parameters, fixed gain, parity readout, and the exclusion of full joint optimization over all resource allocations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the SU(1,1) interferometer setup incorporating the heralded non-Gaussian state generation module using a beam-splitter and ancillary mode projection.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by deriving conditional operators for PS, PA, and PC. It then calculates the QFI based on these inputs, comparing them systematically against the Gaussian benchmark under various loss and resource constraints to draw conclusions about practical utility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Gaussian operations can reshape the photon statistics of continuous-variable probes, but their metrological advantage is meaningful only when heralding probability and photon-number resources are counted consistently. We compare photon subtraction, photon addition, and photon catalysis as input-side heralding operations in a balanced SU(1,1) interferometer with parity detection. A unified finite-transmissivity map supplies closed conditional moments and the corresponding quantum Fisher information at arbitrary operation order; internal loss is absorbed into a single effective parity observable whose lossless limit recovers the ideal pulled-back measurement. At fixed preparation parameters, single-photon subtraction and addition improve the conditional phase information over the Gaussian reference across most of the high-transmissivity regime, while multi-photon catalysis opens useful low-transmissivity windows. However, when the coherent--squeezed allocation is independently optimized at fixed conditional-probe energy and fixed interferometer gain, the success-weighted Fisher information of all three non-Gaussian operations remains below the optimized Gaussian benchmark. This conclusion is subject to the tested constraints: single-photon operations, a coherent-plus-squeezed-vacuum Gaussian family, fixed gain, and parity readout. Photon catalysis separately generates a conditional branch with high local quantum Fisher information that dark-point parity extracts poorly, identifying a measurement mismatch rather than a state-preparation failure. The result draws a sharp boundary between conditional non-Gaussian enhancement and practically available precision under explicitly stated resource constraints.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced quantum algorithms to efficiently simulate dissipative time evolution governed by $\exp(-T H^\alpha)$. It utilizes Poisson summation to transform the problem into a polynomial eigenvalue transformation within Quantum Signal Processing. The key breakthrough is showing that using a shifted signal encoding significantly improves the approximation error scaling for noninteger powers, leading to more resource-efficient simulation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete algorithmic improvements (better error scaling) for simulating complex open quantum system dynamics ($\exp(-T H^\alpha)$) by optimizing the underlying quantum circuit structure, which is highly relevant for quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantum implementation of calculating the contraction $\exp(-T H^\alpha)$ for a positive semi-definite Hamiltonian $H$, which models dissipative dynamics.

**Main result.** By employing Poisson summation to construct polynomial eigenvalue transformations, they improve the error scaling for noninteger powers from $\Theta(d^{-\alpha})$ to $\Theta(d^{-2\alpha})$ using a shifted signal encoding.

**Method.** The core method involves Quantum Signal Processing (QSP) combined with Poisson Summation techniques to map the required function approximation into a polynomial eigenvalue transformation suitable for quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The model is general dissipative dynamics governed by $u_T = e^{-T H^\alpha} u_0$, where $H$ is a Hermitian, positive semi-definite operator. Two access models are analyzed: the standard block encoding of $H/\|H\|$ and a unit-normalized shifted signal $2H/\|H\| - I$.

**Key observables.** The state preparation overhead ratio $u_r = \|u_0\| / \|u_T\|$ and the approximation error $\epsilon$ are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T$ (time), $\alpha$ (power exponent), and the polynomial degree $d$ dictate resource scaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes classical compilation of Fourier samples into Chebyshev polynomials, and that specific structural assumptions (like unit normalization for the shifted signal) are met to improve convergence rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the need for polynomial transformations via Poisson summation, comparing standard vs. shifted access models, deriving improved error bounds and degree requirements ($d$), and finally applying these techniques to controlled dissipative families using amplitude-phase separation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study quantum implementations of the contraction $\exp(-T H^α)$ for $H=H^\dagger\succeq0$ and $α>0$. Poisson summation provides an exact target--alias--tail decomposition whose Fourier samples are compiled classically into a single Chebyshev polynomial, so the quantum circuit uses polynomial eigenvalue transformation rather than a frequency linear combination of unitaries. We compare block encodings of $H/\norm{H}$ and of the shifted signal $2H/\norm{H}-I$. Under ordinary single-sequence QSVT, parity forces the former to use an even extension, which is entire only for even positive integers. An exact quadratic lift for the shifted signal makes every positive integer entire and improves the fixed-scale approximation error for noninteger powers from $Θ(d^{-α})$ to $Θ(d^{-2α})$ within the stated access and parity classes. We derive matching degree bounds in the large-scale fixed-error and fixed-scale high-precision limits, including the output-normalization overhead $u_r$. Nearest-neighbor Laplacians give a unit-normalized shifted signal. We further establish a noncommutative Weyl--Poisson identity compatible with LCHS quadrature, and use the same polynomial construction to implement controlled dissipative families in amplitude--phase separation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated theoretical framework to calculate coupled transport phenomena in confined fluids by analyzing space-time correlations derived from molecular dynamics. By formulating the problem using an extended Onsager response matrix, the authors successfully distinguish between slow, nonlocal hydrodynamic modes and fast, localized ionic relaxation mechanisms. This provides crucial microscopic insights into how continuum theories break down at the nanoscale.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the rigorous application of linear response theory to derive non-equilibrium transport coefficients from microscopic fluctuations mirrors techniques used in quantum many-body physics for calculating correlation functions and transport properties in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to resolve coupled transport (particle, solute, heat, charge) in nanoconfined fluids by developing a framework that moves beyond continuum laws parameterized by bulk coefficients.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that hydrodynamic momentum transport is a long-lived nonlocal mode, while charge transport relaxes rapidly via localized ionic friction. The full space-time response kernel provides a microscopic basis for history-dependent transport laws.

**Method.** The coupled transport is formulated as a space–time-resolved Onsager response matrix, extracted from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations using generalized Green-Kubo relations.

**Model / system.** The study uses a minimal model of a charged Lennard-Jones (LJ) fluid confined between two negatively-charged walls in a nanopore geometry. The analysis extends to coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport channels.

**Key observables.** Space–time response kernels ($R_{ab}(r, r', t)$), local–local responses ($G_{ab}$), off-diagonal electrokinetic couplings (Streaming Current, EOF), and the full 4x4 matrix of reduced response kernels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanoscale confinement dimensions ($\sigma$), temperature ($k_B T$), and the time/space separation in correlation functions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that physics can be described by a generalized Onsager framework, relying on linear response theory applied to equilibrium current correlations. The analysis also involves choosing appropriate flux bases (e.g., excess flux basis).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hierarchy of response functions from molecular dynamics simulations, showing snapshots of the charged fluid and detailed visualizations of the full 4x4 coupling matrix for different transport channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the generalized Onsager framework using equilibrium current correlations. It then analyzes specific couplings (hydrodynamic vs. charge) and explores how transforming to an 'excess flux basis' can suppress long-range hydrodynamic components, revealing underlying local relaxation mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transport in fluids is generally reduced to continuum laws parametrized by bulk coefficients and effective interfacial parameters, such as viscosities, diffusivities, slip lengths, and interfacial resistances. This description becomes incomplete at the nanoscale, where spatial heterogeneity, molecular structure, and finite relaxation times are inseparable from the transport process. Here we formulate coupled transport in nanoconfined fluids as a space--time-resolved Onsager response matrix and extract it from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations. Applied to a confined charged fluid, the framework resolves the nonlocal and transient pathways coupling particle, solute, heat, and charge transport. Momentum transport appears as a long-lived, nonlocal hydrodynamic mode, whereas charge transport relaxes rapidly through localized ionic friction. Off-diagonal responses reveal distinct projected dynamics, providing a microscopic basis for nonlocal, history-dependent transport laws.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** This experimental study used ARPES to investigate the nature of the Charge Density Wave transition in TiSe2. Contrary to expectations of an electronic gap opening, the researchers found that the bulk band gap remains robustly constant across the transition temperature. This strongly suggests the CDW is driven by a lattice reconstruction that folds existing bands rather than creating a new electronic gap.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, understanding how lattice instabilities (like CDWs) interact with electronic structure without necessarily opening a gap provides insights into symmetry breaking mechanisms relevant to correlated electron systems and charge ordering phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether the Charge Density Wave (CDW) transition in TiSe2 involves an electronic gap opening or if it is driven by lattice reconstruction.

**Main result.** The fundamental band gap size remains constant across the CDW transition temperature, indicating that the transition is governed by lattice symmetry breaking and band folding rather than a true electronic gap opening.

**Method.** High-resolution Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) was used to directly track the temperature evolution of the bulk band edges.

**Model / system.** The study investigates TiSe2, a narrow-gap semiconductor known for its CDW transition at TCDW = 200 K. The analysis compares electronic structure measurements across the normal and CDW phases.

**Key observables.** Temperature evolution of the bulk band gap size; positions of conduction and valence band edges; spectral weight redistribution signatures (e.g., Folded Valence Band).

**Important parameters / regimes.** CDW transition temperature ($T_{	ext{CDW}} \approx 200	ext{ K}$); Temperature range studied ($300	ext{ K}$ down to $160	ext{ K}$); Gap magnitude ($\sim 85-100	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors assume that their simultaneous measurement of both band edges in single EDCs eliminates methodological uncertainties present in previous gap measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures show ARPES intensity maps and Fermi surface contours remaining nearly identical across the CDW transition, while other figures plot the measured bulk gap magnitude showing invariance with temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scientific debate surrounding the CDW mechanism. It then presents high-resolution ARPES data tracking band edges across $T_{	ext{CDW}}$, demonstrating constant gap size despite clear evidence of lattice-induced band folding signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mechanism driving the charge density wave (CDW) transition in TiSe2 has been debated for decades, with proposals ranging from an excitonic insulator to a lattice-driven instability. A central question remains whether the transition involves an opening or enhancement of the bulk band gap. Using high-resolution angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy, we directly track the temperature evolution of the bulk band edges across the CDW transition at TCDW = 200 K. Contrary to the expectation of a gap-opening transition, we find that the size of the fundamental band gap remains constant from the high-temperature normal phase down to 160 K. While the CDW induces clear band-folding signatures and spectral weight redistribution, the underlying band extrema are unperturbed. These results demonstrate that TiSe2 does not undergo a temperature-driven electronic gap opening. Instead, they support a scenario where the transition is governed by a lattice symmetry-breaking reconstruction that folds, but does not gap, the electronic structure of a pre-existing band insulator.

</details>
