**Summary.** This paper proposes the 'quantum filter' as a novel, resource-efficient method to protect quantum states from Amplitude Damping noise. By analyzing circuit structures using post-selection, the authors demonstrate that state protection fidelity depends critically on the input state's Hamming weight. The findings suggest that fixed-weight states can be perfectly protected, offering an improvement over existing methods without requiring full QEC overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-efficient alternative to full Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes for mitigating common noise channels like AD noise, which is highly relevant for superconducting and trapped-ion quantum computing platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is protecting fragile quantum states from irreversible energy relaxation caused by environmental noise, specifically Amplitude Damping (AD) noise.

**Main result.** The authors show that using a 'quantum filter' circuit structure allows for resource-efficient state protection; states with fixed quantum Hamming weights can be perfectly protected against AD noise.

**Method.** They introduce and systematically analyze the quantum filter, which utilizes post-selection on control qubit measurement outcomes to suppress noise effects in multi-qubit systems.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on general multi-qubit quantum information processing systems modeled by the Amplitude Damping (AD) channel. The protection scheme involves circuits with single- and multi-control qubits acting on target qubits.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F$), success probability, and the quantum Hamming weight ($s$) of the input state are key diagnostic tools.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The damping rate ($\gamma$) is crucial; performance depends heavily on $s$ relative to the total number of qubits $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that states within a fixed Hamming weight subspace $\mathcal{H}_{s,N}$ share the same achievable fidelity. The method avoids full quantum error correction overhead.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit sketches comparing filtration to CSWAP performance, and plots show average fidelity vs. the number of control qubits ($m$) for various state types (e.g., bipartite superposition states).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from single-qubit analysis demonstrating filter superiority over standard gates, to general multi-body state analysis using Hamming weight classification, culminating in showing that increasing control qubits improves average fidelity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

All realistic quantum systems are inevitably in contact with the environment. Suppressing theimpact of environmental noise is a critical challenge in cutting-edge quantum technologies. In thiswork, we introduce and systematically analyze a scheme for the protection of quantum states againstamplitude-damping (AD) noise based on the circuit structure called the quantum filter. Filtrationcircuits employing single- and multi-control qubits are examined, and their capability to enhance stateprotection fidelity while preserving a high success probability is discussed. Moreover, for many-bodyqubit states, those with a fixed quantum Hamming weight can be perfectly protected against ADnoise, whereas states with the largest Hamming weight difference set a lower bound on the achievableprotection fidelity. Our work provides a resource-efficient route for quantum state protection withoutrequiring full quantum error correction.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces novel 'routing' and 'multi-routing' schemes to implement universal linear transformations in multiport interferometers. These methods drastically reduce the required physical components compared to established designs like Reck or Clements meshes. Furthermore, the authors demonstrate that these resource-efficient routing schemes maintain high fidelity even when subjected to realistic losses and phase noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses photonic quantum computation by providing a highly efficient, resource-optimized blueprint for implementing complex unitary gates using fundamental optical elements (MZIs).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is implementing universal linear unitary transformations on $N$ photonic modes using minimal physical components.

**Main result.** Routing schemes can achieve universal linear manipulation with significantly fewer components (e.g., $N-1$ MZIs) compared to standard universal designs ($\mathcal{O}(N^2)$).

**Method.** The authors propose and analyze 'routing' and 'multi-routing' schemes that utilize projective measurements over time to realize the required unitary transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is a multiport interferometer with $N$ ports, modeled by unitary transformations ($U$) acting on classical coherent light or single photons. The fundamental building block is the Mach-Zehnder Interferometer (MZI).

**Key observables.** Fidelity and Trace Distance are used to quantify performance degradation due to losses; resource overheads (number of MZIs/detectors) are key comparative metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of ports ($N$), the number of photons ($m$), loss rates, and phase noise levels are critical parameters analyzed for robustness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares schemes based on ideal unitary realization versus practical resource constraints; some sections assume measurement occurs after unitary manipulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare resource requirements (Reck vs. Linear/Tree meshes) and analyze performance degradation under random MZI loss or output coupling losses, showing routing schemes are more robust.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of universal transformation implementation, contrasts standard $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ schemes with proposed linear/tree routing schemes ($\mathcal{O}(N)$), and finally tests the robustness of these resource-efficient schemes against realistic noise sources like loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiport interferometers with $N$ ports are basic devices in both classical and quantum photonics. Ideally, they implement a linear unitary transformation between the input and output electric field vectors with $N$ components, each associated with a spatial mode of classical coherent light or a single photon. Standard designs for a fully reconfigurable universal multiport interferometer are given by the Reck or the Clements schemes. In this work, we introduce routing schemes to implement a generic unitary transformation on classical coherent light or single photons using linear or tree geometries via multiple projective measurements on a single detector with the minimum number of components. Then, we generalize this result to the case of any multi-photon state for scattershot boson sampling experiments with multi-routing schemes. Finally, we test the robustness of routing schemes compared to universal schemes with respect to losses and phase noise.

</details>

**Authors:** Qing-Ao Xiang, Yan Liu, Xin-Yuan Yang, Ya-Ju Song

Orange dots mark alternation points at which the residual ωJopt N,R(ω) −1 alternately attains ±E3(102), where E3(102) = 0.02856, certifying minimax...</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously proves that for modeling $1/|\omega|$ spectra using a finite number of auxiliary bath modes, introducing coherent coupling between these modes provides no improvement in accuracy over simply using independent (uncoupled) damped modes. This establishes a fundamental lower bound on the minimum number of modes required to achieve a desired spectral fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical constraints on how complex quantum dissipation structures (like those found in cavity QED or open quantum dynamics) can model specific spectral features like $1/|\omega|$, showing that complexity does not grant spectral power beyond the fundamental limitations set by mode count.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if adding coherent intermode coupling between auxiliary bath modes can reduce the spectral approximation error when modeling a $1/|\omega|$ spectrum using a finite number of Markovian Lindblad modes.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for passive, number-conserving Gaussian Lindblad networks approximating $1/|\omega|$, coherent intermode coupling offers no advantage over uncoupled diagonal structures. Consequently, the minimum required mode count is fixed by the spectral approximation theory, regardless of complex coupling terms.

**Method.** The analysis relies on mapping the spectral approximation problem to a classical Zolotarev rational function approximation problem for the sign function ($	ext{sgn}(\omega)$). The optimal error bounds are derived using techniques from approximation theory and elliptic functions.

**Model / system.** The system involves an open quantum system coupled to a passive, number-conserving Gaussian Lindblad auxiliary bath. The dynamics are characterized by the spectral density $J(\omega)$, which is approximated by a rational function whose poles correspond to the auxiliary modes.

**Key observables.** Spectral approximation error ($E$), minimum required mode count ($N_{\min}$), and maximum achievable dynamic range ($D_{\max}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mode budget ($N$), spectral bandwidth/range ($R$), and target tolerance ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result holds for passive, number-conserving Gaussian Lindblad auxiliary networks operating in a stationary vacuum state coupled via a single Hermitian bath operator.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the structure of spectra from general coupled vs. uncoupled classes and illustrate the equioscillation property associated with the Zolotarev minimax optimum.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the spectral approximation problem, relates it to the Zolotarev theory for sign function approximation, proves the equivalence of error bounds between coupled and uncoupled systems, and finally derives scaling laws relating mode count to achievable dynamic range.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Representing continuous environments by finitely many Markovian auxiliary modes is fundamental in non-Markovian open quantum systems, yet a critical question remains: at a fixed mode budget, can coherent intermode coupling reduce the spectral approximation error? We prove that intermode coupling offers no advantage when passive, number-conserving Gaussian Lindblad auxiliary networks approximate a $1/|ω|$ spectrum over a finite two-sided frequency band. For any mode budget $N$, the general coupled class and its uncoupled diagonal subclass share the same optimal error, which is exactly the degree-$2N$ Zolotarev error for sign approximation. This optimum is attainable by $N$ independent damped auxiliary modes at zero detuning. The result holds when the auxiliary network is in a stationary vacuum state, the system couples to it via a single Hermitian bath operator, and no white-noise feedthrough term is present. Consequently, although a general coupled network has $O(N^{2})$ real parameters, coherent intermode coupling, collective dissipation, and nonnormal structure cannot reduce the number of auxiliary modes required to reach a prescribed tolerance. This exact relation yields both the minimum mode count for a prescribed positive-frequency dynamic range and tolerance, and the maximum dynamic range attainable for a prescribed mode budget and tolerance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper critically compares several theoretical measures proposed for describing photon localization probability density. By analyzing these measures using both idealized Gaussian wave packets and a model detector based on ion traps, the authors argue that while multiple proposals exist, the Energy Wave Function and Hawton Density are the most physically consistent descriptions of local detection rates.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the fundamental ambiguity in defining quantum observables like position for photons, which is crucial for developing realistic models for quantum optical devices and detectors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the theoretically correct measure for defining the probability density of a single photon's position, especially when considering detection at subwavelength spatial resolution.

**Main result.** While multiple measures (Landau-Peierls, Energy Wave Function, Hawton Density) are proposed, they all show consistency with local detection rates derived from ion traps. The energy wave function and Hawton density are highlighted as the most promising candidates for describing photon localization.

**Method.** The authors compare several theoretical proposals for photon localization probability densities by analyzing their time evolution using a two-dimensional Gaussian single-photon wave packet in momentum space, alongside calculations based on scattering matrix elements from an ion trap detector model.

**Model / system.** The system models the propagation and detection of single-photon wave packets. The analysis uses theoretical constructs like the Landau-Peierls function and compares them to physical measurements simulated using a model detector consisting of ions in ion traps.

**Key observables.** Photon localization probability density ($ho$), $\langle r angle - ct$ offset, scattering matrix elements ($S_{fi}$), and local detection probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time evolution $t$, momentum-space width ($\Delta k$), and the geometry/coupling of the ion trap detector.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing theoretical proposals against operational definitions derived from scattering matrix elements, which involves approximations like the dipole approximation and high-frequency limits in the detection model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of five different localization measures for a Gaussian packet, showing their coalescence towards classical wavefronts at late times. They also plot the deviation $\langle r angle - ct$ over time for comparison.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the operational problem using detector models (ion traps), linking detection rates to specific theoretical measures. It then performs a detailed comparative analysis of multiple localization proposals ($\varphi, \Psi, ho_H$, etc.) using Gaussian wave packets in momentum space to track their time evolution and convergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compare different measures for photon localization in terms of two-dimensional Gaussian wave packets. We find that all measures start to coalesce if the wave packet has evolved for times which are larger than a few times the inverse momentum-space width of the package. However, the Landau-Peierls wave function yields the largest positive offset <r>-ct>0 while the scalar Fourier transform of the wave packet yields the smallest offset and converges towards <r>-ct=0 fastest. We also discuss local detection of photons through a model detector consisting of ions in ion traps. The position-dependent detection probabilities are inferred from the scattering matrix. We find that the local detection probability for photons can be expressed in terms of three of the proposed localization measures, viz. the Landau-Peierls wave function, the energy wave function, and the Hawton density. Those three porposals also remain close throughout the time evolution of the single-photon wave packet.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 4.</b> Figure 4: Second-order critical point, Eq. (6), N = 10 (type II). Every eigenstate carries an exact O(5) seniority: maxψ Var ˆC2[O(5)] = 2.7 × 10−11 over the whole spectrum (left). The s|d entanglement is graded monotonically by τ (right), ⟨S⟩= 0.71 at τ = 0 falling to 0.00 at τ = N: the entanglement lives in the low-seniority sector and high-seniority states are near-product. This seniority-graded ladder differs qualitatively from the first-order solvable-subset picture of Fig. 3.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.04486_figures/2608.04486_fig5.jpg" alt="Figure 5">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 5: The purity/coherence bridge on Kremer et al.’s own states, N = 12. The O(6) block purity Trρ2 σ (left) and the bipartite s|d entanglement entropy (centre) are independent axes: the ground state becomes O(6)-pure (Trρ2 σ = 0.81–0.88) precisely where its s|d entanglement is maximal (right). The algebraic “purity” of the quasi-dynamical-symmetry literature and bipartite entanglement are logically independent QI quantities. Our O(6) machinery reproduces Kremer’s σ-fluctuation ∆σgs = 2.473 at the U(5) limit (their value 2.47) and the exactly sharp O(6) point.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work introduces the 'label variance' ($	ext{Var}\,\C[G]$) as a novel and robust quantum information diagnostic to detect Partial Dynamical Symmetry (PDS) in the Interacting Boson Model. It demonstrates that this structural measure successfully distinguishes between solvable and mixed eigenstates, unlike standard entanglement measures. The authors validate this method by encoding the model onto qubits, paving the way for its direct evaluation on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of label variance as a measurable, structure-revealing quantum invariant provides a powerful tool for classifying complex many-body systems whose symmetries are only partially preserved in nature or theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining if Partial Dynamical Symmetry (PDS) possesses a purely structural signature within quantum eigenstates, independent of entanglement magnitude.

**Main result.** The label variance ($	ext{Var}\,\C[G]$) serves as this robust, fit-independent diagnostic, successfully separating solvable states (zero variance) from mixed states ($\mathcal{O}(N^2)$).

**Method.** The authors compare structural diagnostics like label variance against entanglement measures and test the method's feasibility by encoding the Hamiltonian onto a qubit register for variational state preparation.

**Model / system.** The analysis is based on the Interacting Boson Model (IBM) applied to even-even nuclei, specifically anchored in $^{168}	ext{Er}$. The model Hamiltonian supports various symmetry limits, including those exhibiting PDS.

**Key observables.** Label variance ($	ext{Var}\,\C[G]$), block-coherence entropy ($S_G$), and block impurity ($P_G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The total boson number $N$, and the Hamiltonian parameters defining critical points (e.g., $eta_0$ for first/second order transitions).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the label variance correctly captures the structural distinction between solvable and mixed spectral subsets, even when entanglement measures fail to do so.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how label variance distinguishes solvable (rigid) from mixed states, and later show the successful application of VQE/VQD protocols on a qubit register to prepare these distinct eigenstates.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical equivalence between vanishing variance, zero entropy, and unit purity for PDS. It then compares this structural diagnostic against entanglement measures using the IBM spectrum. Finally, it details the implementation by encoding the model onto qubits and preparing states variationally on a quantum device.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Partial dynamical symmetry (PDS) is an algebraic structure in which a prescribed symmetry is neither exact nor completely broken: a subset of eigenstates keeps good quantum numbers and remains solvable while the rest of the spectrum mixes. PDS is currently identified from spectroscopic data, band-head energies, level systematics, and $B(E2)$ ratios. We ask whether it also has a purely structural signature in the eigenstates, and find that it does, though not in the magnitude of entanglement. The natural diagnostic is the variance of a symmetry Casimir, the label variance $\Var\,\C[G]$, which we show coincides with a block-coherence entropy and a block impurity: all three vanish exactly when a state carries a single irreducible-representation label. Resolved state by state, this quantity is zero on the solvable subset and of order $N^2$ on the mixed states, at stable symmetry points and at Leviatan's first- and second-order critical points, where it takes two distinct forms set by the order of the transition. The magnitude of bipartite entanglement, by contrast, does not separate solvable from mixed states and drifts even where the labels are exact. We anchor the analysis in $^{168}$Er, connect the block purity to the ``purity/coherence'' language of the quasi-dynamical-symmetry literature, and show the label variance is uncorrelated with multipartite entanglement and with magic. Finally we encode the model on a qubit register and prepare its solvable and mixed eigenstates variationally, as a step toward evaluating the diagnostic on a quantum device.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive thermodynamic framework for Non-Hermitian Dirac fermions in magnetic fields. It demonstrates that the system's thermodynamics can be simplified by mapping its spectrum onto an effective Hermitian model via spectral compression. This yields universal scaling relations for key responses like heat capacity and magnetization, providing a powerful tool for analyzing non-Hermitian quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of using spectral mapping derived from quasi-Hermiticity to simplify complex thermodynamic calculations is highly relevant for open quantum systems, as it provides a rigorous way to connect non-Hermitian physics results back to established Hermitian statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study establishes scaling relations governing the equilibrium thermodynamics, specifically caloric and magnetic responses, for non-Hermitian Dirac fermions subjected to a magnetic field.

**Main result.** The core finding is that spectral compression allows complex Non-Hermitian thermodynamic quantities (like chemical potential and heat capacity) to be mapped onto rescaled forms of their Hermitian counterparts using an effective magnetic field.

**Method.** The analysis employs similarity transformations to relate the quasi-Hermitian Hamiltonian to an effective Hermitian model, allowing the use of standard statistical mechanics techniques on the transformed spectrum.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by real-spectrum Non-Hermitian (NH) Dirac fermions, often represented using graphene as a platform, and analyzed in the presence of a magnetic field. The Hamiltonian is quasi-Hermitian but maps to an effective Hermitian model with reduced velocity.

**Key observables.** Chemical potential ($\mu$), entropy ($S$), heat capacities, orbital magnetic response, and the grand partition function ($\Xi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-Hermiticity parameter $eta$ (where $|eta| < 1$), temperature ($T$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and applied magnetic field ($B$). Scaling factors like $\lambda = 1 / (1 - eta^2)$ are crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Equilibrium is defined by thermalization with respect to the quasi-Hermitian Hamiltonian, and the analysis assumes that single-particle eigenvalues determine equilibrium quantities, which match those of the Hermitian model.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the thermodynamic framework by first establishing the spectral mapping identity ($D_eta(E; B) = D_0(E; 	ext{Beff})$), then applying this to derive scaling relations for various observables under fixed projected filling factor (PFF) and fixed chemical potential ($\mu$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish scaling relations governing the equilibrium thermodynamics of real-spectrum non-Hermitian Dirac fermions in a magnetic field. Assuming thermalization with respect to the quasi-Hermitian Hamiltonian, a similarity transformation maps the system at the same applied field onto a Hermitian Dirac model with reduced velocity, while the Landau-level spectrum also admits a representation in terms of a reduced effective magnetic field. This structure yields scaling relations for the chemical potential, entropy, heat capacities, and orbital magnetic response in different thermodynamic ensembles. At fixed projected filling factor (PFF), the self-consistent chemical potential follows the compressed ladder of Landau levels, and the canonical thermodynamic functions are rescaled Hermitian responses. At fixed chemical potential, Landau-level crossings generate oscillatory caloric and magnetic responses governed by the same spectral compression. Quasistatic non-Hermitian deformation at fixed PFF further yields adiabatic temperature scaling. More broadly, these results establish a thermodynamic framework for real-spectrum non-Hermitian quantum matter and provide a starting point for incorporating the effects of interactions and disorder within the same formalism.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a 'Toda–Arnoldi correspondence' showing that the evolution of non-Hermitian Hamiltonians, tracked via Arnoldi reduction, is governed by the mathematics of the two-dimensional Toda lattice. It provides deep insights into how geometric quantities such as the Berry curvature and Fubini–Study metric are encoded in the system's spectral coefficients.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects non-Hermitian quantum dynamics (relevant to open systems) directly to integrable systems theory (Toda lattice), providing powerful mathematical tools to analyze spectral flow and geometric invariants like the Chern number.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a deep mathematical correspondence between the dynamics generated by non-Hermitian Hamiltonians via Arnoldi reduction and the structure of the two-dimensional Toda lattice.

**Main result.** It proves that Krylov Gram determinants satisfy the $	au$-function relations of the 2D Toda lattice, linking geometric quantities like the Fubini–Study metric and Berry curvature to these integrable systems.

**Method.** The analysis combines concepts from numerical linear algebra (Arnoldi reduction), complex geometry (holomorphic deformations, Grassmannians), and soliton theory (Toda lattice $	au$-functions).

**Model / system.** The study concerns a fixed, finite-dimensional, generally non-Hermitian Hamiltonian $H$ whose evolution is tracked using holomorphic deformation of a cyclic state vector. The mathematical framework involves the Krylov subspace generated by this system.

**Key observables.** Krylov Gram determinants ($	au_n$), Arnoldi coefficients ($\alpha_n, eta_n$), Fubini–Study metric components ($b_n^2$), and Berry curvature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complex deformation parameter $z$ and the dimensionality of the Hamiltonian/subspace. The correspondence is restricted to cyclic states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a cyclic seed state vector and relies on the validity of the Arnoldi method, noting breakdowns when the Krylov dimension drops.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the connection between holomorphic evolution $ightarrow$ Krylov data $\leftrightarrow$ 2D Toda variables; they also show Stokes-Gauss relations linking area integrals (related to metric) to boundary integrals (related to coefficients).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the dynamics via holomorphic deformation and Krylov methods, establishing the correspondence with the 2D Toda lattice. It then explores geometric consequences like the Fubini-Study metric and Berry curvature, culminating in an electrostatic analogy for understanding these structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although Arnoldi reduction of a generally non-Hermitian Hamiltonian yields an upper Hessenberg matrix rather than the tridiagonal form of Hermitian Lanczos theory, we show that a closed Toda sector survives in its diagonal and subdiagonal coefficients. For a fixed finite-dimensional Hamiltonian and a cyclic state vector deformed holomorphically, the Krylov Gram determinants are $τ$ functions of the finite two-dimensional Toda lattice, whose Flaschka variables coincide exactly with these Arnoldi coefficients. The Toda dynamics therefore closes on this sector without determining the remaining upper Hessenberg entries. The subdiagonal part of the same sector also has a direct geometric meaning: the squared subdiagonal coefficients determine both the Fubini--Study metric and the Berry curvature of holomorphic Krylov subspaces, whereas the geometric quantities associated with subspaces lost at Arnoldi breakdown cease to be defined. Along a smooth real path in the cyclic region, the Arnoldi-frame connection further provides a Hermitian tridiagonal generator of exact isospectral transport. When added to the Arnoldi matrix, this generator cancels transitions between instantaneous eigenspaces and realizes counterdiabatic driving whenever the matrix is diagonalizable with a nondegenerate spectrum.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how quantum walkers behave on lattices with fractal geometry, specifically the Sierpiński carpet. It shows that compared to uniform lattices where particles spread ballistically, the fractal structure causes strong, self-similar trapping near the starting point. This highlights how geometric constraints alone can suppress quantum transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean theoretical demonstration of how geometric constraints, even without explicit disorder potentials, can fundamentally alter quantum transport properties by creating hierarchical barriers that suppress long-range coherence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares quantum particle transport dynamics on a fractal lattice (Sierpiński carpet) against its counterpart on a uniform lattice of the same size.

**Main result.** While particles move ballistically on the uniform lattice, they become increasingly trapped near their starting point on the Sierpiński carpet as the fractal order increases.

**Method.** The dynamics are simulated by solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation using numerical methods like Runge-Kutta, focusing on zone-integrated probabilities for comparison.

**Model / system.** A single quantum particle is modeled moving via nearest-neighbor tunneling on a square lattice structure that is iteratively modified into a Sierpiński carpet fractal. The dynamics are compared against the same system on a standard uniform lattice.

**Key observables.** Zone-integrated probabilities for finding the particle in the initial zone ($S(t)$) versus the opposite corner zone ($F(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fractal order ($R$), lattice size, and time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes continuous-time dynamics governed by nearest-neighbor tunneling only to sites belonging to the defined lattice structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $S(t)$ and $F(t)$ for different fractal orders, showing confinement on the Sierpiński carpet versus ballistic spread on the uniform lattice. Other figures analyze the scaling of characteristic crossing times across various zone sizes ($K$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model by defining the dynamics on both lattices, presents key observables (zone-integrated probabilities), and then systematically compares the time evolution results to demonstrate the qualitative difference between ballistic transport and fractal trapping.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the dynamics of a single quantum particle on a finite-size square lattice with a fractal structure resembling the Sierpiński carpet, and compare it to the dynamics on a uniform lattice of the same size. For a particle initially localized at a corner of the lattice, we monitor the probability of finding it near the initial and opposite corners using zone-integrated probabilities, allowing a consistent comparison across fractal orders. While on the uniform lattice the particle reaches the opposite corner ballistically, in a time proportional to the lattice size, on the Sierpiński lattice it becomes increasingly confined to the vicinity of its initial position. We show that this trapping builds up self-similarly across the whole hierarchy of corner zones of the lattice.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes structural properties of Random Recursive Trees by defining 'rank' and 'layer' distributions. Using advanced mathematical techniques like recursive relations and traveling wave analysis, the authors derive precise asymptotic formulas showing that these distributions decay factorially (for rank) and geometrically (for layer). The work provides a powerful framework for analyzing network structure in complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on graph theory, the mathematical machinery—solving complex recursions, analyzing wavefront propagation via PDEs, and deriving precise asymptotic scaling laws—is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics problems involving pattern formation or relaxation dynamics in physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving exact recursive expressions and asymptotic distributions for two structural measures, 'rank' and 'layer', within Random Recursive Trees (RRTs).

**Main result.** The rank distribution decays factorially, while the layer distribution exhibits a geometric decay characterized by complex exponential forms involving constants like $1/e$.

**Method.** The authors employ recursive methods, tree-splitting techniques, and advanced asymptotic analysis, including solving differential equations using a traveling wave ansatz.

**Model / system.** The model is the Random Recursive Tree (RRT), a growing network structure where nodes are added sequentially by connecting to an existing node chosen uniformly at random. The analysis focuses on structural properties of this graph ensemble.

**Key observables.** Rank ($r_k$): distance to the closest leaf; Layer ($\ell_k$): number of leaf-stripping rounds required for a node to become a leaf. Also analyzed are their respective fractions and variances.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the tree, $n$, is taken to infinity ($n 	o \infty$) for asymptotic results. The index $k$ (rank/layer) is also studied in the large-$k$ limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the validity of the traveling wave ansatz and the assertion that $\ell_k \sim ar{\ell}_k$, which cannot be rigorously derived from the main asymptotic equations provided.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concepts of rank and layer on small trees, compare computed vs. simulated distributions for $r_k$ and $\ell_k$, and show convergence rates related to variances and wavefront growth.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving exact recursive expressions (Theorems 1 & 2) to establishing self-averaging properties (Theorem 5). It then tackles the asymptotic limits, using advanced differential equation techniques (Traveling Wave Ansatz) to derive the final exponential decay laws for $ar{\ell}_k$ and conjectures for $\ell_k$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The distribution of node depths in a network is crucial for analyzing network structure. Two measures, rank and layer, quantify how deep inside a network a node is. The rank is the node's distance to the closest leaf, a node of degree 1. The layer is the number of times all leaves must be stripped off for the node to become a leaf. We derive exact recursive expressions for the rank and layer distributions in random recursive trees. We show that the rank and layer distributions decay factorially and geometrically, respectively, and prove the self-averaging of the numbers of nodes with fixed rank or layer. Unlike previous studies, our approach does not depend on labels or a root node, providing a more versatile framework for analyzing rank and layer distributions in complex networks.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a scalable method to simulate exotic Fractional Quantum Hall states on quantum processors. It demonstrates that non-Abelian clustered FQH states can be prepared with constant circuit depth, contrasting sharply with the linear depth required for simpler Abelian states. This establishes a powerful new route for studying topological matter beyond current hardware limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete quantum circuit blueprints for simulating highly complex, strongly correlated electron phenomena (FQH physics) that are otherwise inaccessible due to exponential resource scaling in traditional methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a scalable method to prepare exotic Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) states, particularly those exhibiting non-Abelian anyon statistics, on programmable quantum hardware.

**Main result.** Clustered non-Abelian FQH states can be prepared using parallel quantum circuits with a two-qubit depth independent of system size (constant depth), unlike the Abelian Laughlin state which requires linearly growing circuit depth.

**Method.** The authors use a systematic framework to catalog FQH states by constructing parent Hamiltonians from pattern-of-zeros data, translating these into quantum circuits for implementation on hardware like IBM Heron processors.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on FQH matter, modeling various states including the Abelian Laughlin state and non-Abelian Read–Rezayi $\mathbb{Z}_3$ and $\mathbb{Z}_4$ states. These are analyzed using thin-torus circuits derived from pseudopotential parent Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Fractional quasihole charges, non-Abelian braiding data (measured interferometrically), and circuit depth scaling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (up to 156 qubits), two-qubit gate depth, and the clustering property of the state wavefunction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation at large scales is identified as per-qubit readout error rather than the gate count itself; constant depth relies on junction squeezes forming disjoint blocks.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare circuit depth scaling, illustrating the dichotomy between linear depth (Laughlin) and constant depth (clustered non-Abelian states) for various FQH models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing a systematic framework to cataloging states, demonstrating the circuit construction pipeline, comparing depth scalings based on clustering properties, and finally validating results by measuring topological observables like fractional charge and braiding data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Abelian anyons arise as exotic excitations in fractional quantum Hall (FQH) matter and have proved very elusive to realize in conventional platforms. In this work, we show that on a programmable quantum hardware platform, the more exotic FQH excitations are the less costly ones to prepare: clustered non-Abelian FQH states admit parallel quantum preparation circuits whose two-qubit depth is independent of system size, while constructing the more common Abelian Laughlin state requires a sequential circuit chain with linear depth. The centerpiece of this work is our new systematic framework for cataloging possible FQH states and preparing them on quantum circuits at unprecedented scale and variety. Our prepared parafermionic Read--Rezayi $\mathbb{Z}_3$ state holds depth 3 from 8 to 118 qubits, and full root sampling extends to a 154-qubit, 104-electron Read--Rezayi $\mathbb{Z}_4$ state. In all, our demonstrated 18-family catalog of prepared FQH states extends to all 156 qubits of an IBM Heron processor, limited only by existing hardware scale. Measurements on the prepared states recover the expected fractional quasihole charges, with the charge estimator exact in every symmetry-selected shot for the clustered states, and braiding data of the non-Abelian $e/4$ quasihole measured via interferometric extensions. Our work establishes a scalable route to studying FQH physics on quantum processors and opens new avenues for preparing and probing non-Abelian topological matter far beyond the reach of conventional platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors study symmetry-projected Gibbs states in a 3D cluster model to characterize the resulting phase diagram. They introduce a quantized topological invariant, the flux-twisted membrane index, capable of distinguishing between topologically ordered, paramagnetically ordered, and disordered phases. The methodology combines theoretical analysis with advanced Quantum Monte Carlo simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete numerical evidence for how imposing global symmetries (via projection) can stabilize exotic quantum phases at non-zero temperatures, which is a key topic in modern condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how projecting Gibbs states onto symmetric sectors stabilizes topological phases at finite temperatures, aiming to determine if this projection induces a phase transition where none existed before.

**Main result.** A flux-twisted membrane index distinguishes three distinct phases (SPT, projected-paramagnetic, and disordered) with quantized values of $-1, +1,$ and $0$. The phase diagram exhibits continuous transitions and a self-dual first-order line.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical derivations involving partition function ratios and symmetry projectors with extensive Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations on 3D tori.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a three-dimensional cluster-model interpolation Hamiltonian $H(s)$ that smoothly transitions between a Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) state and a trivial paramagnet. The analysis focuses on the projected Gibbs states defined by specific symmetry projectors ($\mathcal{P}_{loc}$ or $\mathcal{P}_+$).

**Key observables.** Flux-twisted membrane index ($I$), free-energy splittings ($F_\Sigma(s, T)$), and tension diagnostics (spatial-sheet, winding-line, interface tensions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interpolation parameter $s \in [0, 1]$, temperature $T$, and lattice size $L$. Critical values include $T_{I} \approx 1.3133$ for the endpoint mapping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Quantization away from specific limits requires positive spatial-sheet, winding-line, and interface tensions; theoretical derivations rely on assumptions about sector asymptotics (Hypothesis H).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the finite-regulator estimate of the projected phase diagram showing three distinct regions separated by continuous and first-order lines. Other figures detail the geometry involving noncontractible cycles.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context via symmetry projection, derives a topological index ($I$), calculates this index using QMC on finite lattices, and maps out the resulting phase diagram structure across varying parameters $s$ and $T$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Gibbs states projected onto the symmetric sector of contractible one-form symmetries. In a three-dimensional cluster-model interpolation, this projection stabilizes symmetry-protected topological and projected-paramagnetic phases, both sharply distinct from thermal disorder. A flux-twisted membrane invariant distinguishes these three phases by the values $-1,+1,0$, respectively. These values are exact on the endpoint, self-dual, zero-temperature, and infinite-temperature lines; elsewhere their quantization requires positive spatial-sheet, winding-line, and interface tensions. Quantum Monte Carlo supports the quantization through tension diagnostics and direct finite-size estimates.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose a novel method to solve linear ordinary differential equations by reformulating the problem as a continuous-variable optimization task suitable for bosonic quantum annealing. By mapping this objective function onto a specific Hamiltonian, they demonstrate that their approach successfully reproduces known analytical solutions in numerical tests. This work offers a promising route to solving complex physical problems without resorting to variable discretization overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how quantum computation can tackle continuous physical problems (like solving differential equations) using hardware platforms naturally suited for continuous variables (bosonic systems), bypassing major limitations of current qubit architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a quantum annealing approach capable of solving linear ordinary differential equations (ODEs) without the computational overhead associated with discretizing variables for qubit-based systems.

**Main result.** Numerical simulations successfully demonstrated that the proposed continuous-variable formulation can reproduce known analytical solutions for second-order linear ODEs, establishing a viable pathway for this application.

**Method.** The ODE solution is recast as a continuous-variable optimization problem whose objective function is then mapped onto a bosonic Hamiltonian suitable for continuous-variable quantum annealing (CVQA).

**Model / system.** The model utilizes bosonic systems, such as cavity modes, where each optimization variable is directly encoded in a bosonic mode. The formulation relies on approximating derivatives using central finite differences.

**Key observables.** The expectation value of the problem Hamiltonian, $\langle\langle \hat{H}_P angleangle_M$, which must approach zero for the solution to be accurate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of divisions ($M$), step size ($\Delta	au$), and annealing time ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the cost function derived from finite differences can be accurately represented in a quadratic form, $L(	heta) = 	heta^T Q	heta + r^T 	heta$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QA solutions against analytical solutions for specific ODEs and show that the expectation value of $\hat{H}_P$ decreases towards zero as the annealing time $T$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the motivation by contrasting CVQA with qubit-based methods. It then details the mathematical formulation, mapping the discretized ODE into a quadratic cost function and subsequently deriving the bosonic Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_P$. Finally, it presents numerical simulations on specific second-order ODEs to validate the approach.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Most existing quantum annealing approaches are formulated for qubit-based architectures. Consequently, applying them to continuous-variable optimization problems requires discretizing the variables, which can incur substantial qubit overhead. Continuous-variable quantum annealing based on bosonic systems has recently been proposed as an alternative framework, in which each optimization variable is directly encoded in a bosonic mode, such as a cavity mode. In this work, we develop a continuous-variable quantum annealing formulation for solving linear differential equations. By recasting the determination of the solution as a continuous-variable optimization problem, the differential equation can be mapped onto an objective function compatible with bosonic quantum annealing. Numerical simulations of second-order linear differential equations demonstrate that, under the conditions considered, the proposed formulation reproduces the corresponding analytical solutions. These results establish a potential route toward solving differential equations without the discretization overhead inherent in qubit-based implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that applying a strong magnetic field can drive dimensional reduction in a 2D quantum antiferromagnet model. By analyzing the system's spin dynamics via QMC simulations, the authors show that interchain correlations are suppressed, causing the low-energy spectrum to map onto that of an isolated 1D Heisenberg chain.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clear example of tuning dimensionality in quantum magnets using external fields, offering measurable spectroscopic signatures (inelastic neutron scattering) for emergent low-dimensional physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether an external magnetic field can induce a dimensional reduction in a quasi-two-dimensional quantum antiferromagnet.

**Main result.** Beyond a critical magnetic field, the system's low-energy excitations are predicted to reduce to those of a one-dimensional AFM Heisenberg chain, exhibiting gapless incommensurate spinon fluctuations.

**Method.** The research employs Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations using Abrikosov fermions and compares results with analytical treatments like spin-wave theory and Bethe Ansatz analysis for limits.

**Model / system.** The model is a quasi-2D quantum magnet based on the mineral botallackite, featuring alternating ferromagnetic (FM) and antiferromagnetic (AFM) spin-1/2 Heisenberg chains coupled together. The Hamiltonian includes interchain couplings that are tuned by an external magnetic field.

**Key observables.** Total magnetization ($M$), dynamical structure factor ($S(\mathbf{Q}, \omega)$), static structure factor, and the evolution of spectral weight in these quantities as a function of $B$ and $T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical magnetic field ($B_c \approx 16	ext{ T}$), temperature ($T$), and the relative strengths of intra-chain vs. inter-chain couplings ($J_2 > J_1 > J_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model simplifies by omitting easy-axis anisotropy and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, focusing on SU(2)-symmetric exchange terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetization curve vs. field (Fig. 1b), and show how the dynamical structure factor evolves from a 2D pattern to one characteristic of decoupled 1D chains at high fields (Figs. 2, 3, 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model and calculating magnetization curves; it then analyzes the spectral weight evolution using QMC simulations across varying magnetic fields, demonstrating the crossover to 1D behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low dimensionality enhances quantum fluctuations, triggering novel states of quantum matter to emerge. In real materials, low dimensionality usually arises from spatially strongly anisotropic couplings. Here, we demonstrate a different mechanism: in two-dimensional systems with coupled alternating ferromagnetic (FM) and antiferromagnetic (AFM) spin-$1/2$ chains, an applied magnetic field may drive a dimensional reduction. Under magnetic field, the FM chains polarize and stiffen, suppressing the propagation of transverse AFM fluctuations from one chain to another, and effectively induce one-dimensional behavior at low energies. For a model describing botallackite, Cu$_2$(OH)$_3$Br, quantum Monte Carlo dynamics show that beyond a critical magnetic field, the low-energy spectrum reduces to that of a one-dimensional AFM Heisenberg spin-$1/2$ chain with field-dependent incommensurate two-spinon fluctuations, providing clear signatures for inelastic neutron scattering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of compiling quantum circuits for large Neutral Atom quantum computers. The authors developed NAViz, a visualization tool, to analyze atom rearrangement strategies. By revealing hidden detours in relaxed movement protocols, they proposed and validated a hybrid compilation strategy that significantly improves performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical engineering challenges of scaling quantum hardware by providing diagnostic tools that reveal subtle physical inefficiencies in compilation paths, which is crucial for theoretical modeling of complex quantum architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of automated compilation tools is necessary for large-scale Neutral Atom (NA) quantum computers due to complex hardware trade-offs.

**Main result.** A novel hybrid routing strategy was derived that outperforms both strictly constrained and fully relaxed compilation methods by intelligently merging steps when overhead reduction is possible.

**Method.** The authors introduced NAViz, a real-time visualization tool, to empirically test and diagnose inefficiencies in existing quantum computation compilation strategies for NA devices.

**Model / system.** The system involves Neutral Atom (NA) quantum computers utilizing optical tweezers for qubit placement and rearrangement. Compilation requires managing physical constraints on atom movement (placement and routing).

**Key observables.** Rearrangement time (T) for various quantum benchmarks like graphstate or QFT.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The trade-off between strict vs. relaxed routing constraints, which govern relative order maintenance during atomic rearrangement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initially assumed that relaxing movement constraints would improve performance; visualization proved this assumption false by revealing physical detours.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows NAViz visualizing computation steps (static/rearranging atoms). Figure 2 illustrates an inefficient detour path during relaxed routing. Table I compares rearrangement times for Strict, Relaxed, and Hybrid strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the need for compilation tools, introduces NAViz to visualize NA computations, analyzes different routing constraints (strict vs. relaxed), diagnoses performance bottlenecks using visualization, and concludes by proposing and validating a superior hybrid strategy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The scale of Neutral Atom (NA) quantum computers requires automated compilation tools. Designing the required heuristic methods demands a deep understanding of complex hardware trade-offs, for which visualizations can provide crucial insights. This work introduces NAViz, the first publicly available app to visualize quantum computations on NA devices in real-time. A case study demonstrates how NAViz was instrumental in identifying and resolving inefficiencies in an existing compilation strategy, leading to a new, more performant one. The tool is available as part of the Munich Quantum Toolkit (MQT) at https://github.com/munich-quantum-toolkit/naviz.

</details>

**Authors:** E. Zambrini Cruzeiro  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05092v1>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes 'perfect games' in dimension-bounded quantum communication settings. It establishes that the ability to perfectly distinguish between quantum and classical strategies depends on comparing graph invariants like chromatic number ($\chi$) against a quantum measure ($\xi_C$). The work provides concrete examples showing genuine quantum advantage where $\xi_C < \chi$, connecting quantum physics directly to extremal combinatorics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides deep connections between fundamental concepts in quantum information theory (contextuality, state exclusion) and classical combinatorial optimization problems (graph coloring), which is highly relevant for understanding resource limitations in quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates perfect prepare-and-measure (PM) games to establish structural relationships between quantum advantage, classical communication complexity, and graph theory invariants.

**Main result.** It provides a complete characterization for binary-output support games, showing that the existence of a perfect quantum strategy is equivalent to a condition involving the complex orthogonal rank ($\xi_C$), which can be strictly weaker than the chromatic number ($\chi$) required for classical realization.

**Method.** The analysis relies on translating physical constraints (perfect winning sets) into graph-theoretic problems, comparing bounds derived from quantum information theory (e.g., orthogonal representations) against classical combinatorial measures (like graph coloring).

**Model / system.** The model is the prepare-and-measure experiment involving Alice encoding an input $x$ into a system of dimension $d$, sending it to Bob for measurement based on $y$. The core mathematical structure analyzed is the conflict graph $\Gamma_G$ derived from the game's winning constraints.

**Key observables.** Chromatic number ($\chi$), complex orthogonal rank ($\xi_C$), classical upper bound ($SG,T$), and quantum values ($Q_d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the quantum system (e.g., qubit $d=2$, qutrit $d=3$), and graph invariants like edge biclique-cover number.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses specifically on 'perfect' winning constraints, implying zero-error information theory requirements for both classical and quantum strategies.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the notes reference tables summarizing perfect PM games with calculated values ($	ext{C}_3, 	ext{Q}_3, S$) and explicit colorings for graph deletions.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the framework of support games and establishing necessary conditions (Proposition 1). It then presents the main characterization theorems linking quantum/classical existence to graph parameters ($\chi$ vs. $\xi_C$). Finally, it applies these results to complex examples like the 13-ray qutrit graph and nonbinary tasks like Torpedo loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Perfect prepare-and-measure games exhibit an all-or-nothing quantum advantage: a quantum system of dimension $d$ satisfies every prescribed winning constraint, whereas a classical $d$-level message cannot. We establish two structural results for such forbidden-output support constraints. First, every binary-output support game reduces exactly to a conflict graph: perfect classical realization with a $d$-level message is equivalent to $d$-colorability, perfect $d$-dimensional quantum realization is equivalent to a $d$-dimensional orthogonal representation, and the minimum number of Bob inputs realizing a fixed conflict graph is its edge biclique-cover number. Second, for an arbitrary finite output alphabet, every perfect qubit strategy admits a perfect classical-bit realization. As a flagship application, the $13$-ray qutrit graph yields a compressed game $(X,Y,B)=(13,8,2)$ with $C_3=39<Q_3=S=40$, and eight Bob inputs are minimal among all binary-output realizations of that graph. Graph extensions demonstrate the mechanism in every dimension, while Torpedo and antidistinguishability games illustrate the genuinely nonbinary regime. These results connect exact communication, graph coloring, contextuality, state exclusion, and zero-error information theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates how 2-group global symmetries constrain line operators (defects) within Quantum Field Theories. The core finding is that these charged lines must explicitly break the system's primary symmetry, a constraint formalized by a 'family anomaly.' The authors use advanced mathematical tools like generalized anomaly inflow to prove this result across various types of symmetry groups.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the concepts of higher symmetries, topological defects, and anomaly matching are fundamental to understanding emergent order and stability in strongly interacting quantum systems, which has parallels with correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes how global symmetries described by 2-groups constrain line operators (defects) charged under the 1-form subgroup of these symmetries.

**Main result.** It shows that such line operators generically break the underlying 0-form symmetry, leading to a family of defects whose existence is protected by a 'family anomaly' invariant under RG flow.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced tools including the Wess-Zumino consistency condition, generalized anomaly inflow formalisms for defects, and differential cohomology techniques depending on whether the 2-group is continuous abelian, nonabelian, or discrete.

**Model / system.** The study applies to Quantum Field Theories (QFTs) possessing 2-group global symmetries, examining constraints in both continuous and discrete settings. The focus is on the interplay between bulk symmetry ($G(0)$) and defect structure ($A(1)$).

**Key observables.** Family anomaly (topological obstruction), tilt operator (linear response to broken symmetry), and constrained correlation functions (e.g., 3-point functions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of the 2-group (abelian, nonabelian, discrete) dictates which mathematical tools are required for the consistency checks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The argument for symmetry breaking is presented as being 'completely kinematic,' meaning it holds regardless of whether the bulk theory is a CFT or if the symmetry is spontaneously broken.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops its argument by first establishing the general constraints using anomaly inflow, then detailing specific mathematical machinery for continuous abelian/nonabelian groups (differential cohomology), and finally addressing the discrete case via defect associativity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

2-group global symmetries intertwine 0-form and 1-form symmetries in an interesting way. We analyze universal constraints on lines which are charged under the 1-form subgroup of a 2-group, and show that they generically break the 0-form symmetry explicitly. This gives rise to a family of line defects parameterized by the broken symmetry generators, whose existence is invariant under the renormalization group flow of the bulk-defect system. The symmetry breaking is enforced by a `family anomaly,' which is a topological obstruction to a symmetric defect. Our main tools are the Wess-Zumino consistency condition and a generalized anomaly inflow formalism for defects and boundaries. For nonabelian continuous 2-groups the family anomaly stems from a higher Berry connection on the moduli space of defects, and constrains response functions that probe the local action of the broken symmetry. In the continuous abelian case we apply differential cohomology to the 2-group background fields to uncover a subtle generalization of the Wess-Zumino condition, while in the discrete case we recast it in terms of the associativity of symmetry defects. We give numerous illustrative examples, computing when possible explicit forms of the tilt operator (which probes the linear response of the defect to the broken symmetry) and its higher analogs, which are crucial for matching the family anomaly. We also discuss universal features such as how symmetry violation by charged line defects is related to symmetry breaking hierarchies in the bulk, and highlight a number of subtle points including the distinction between simple and non-simple lines, and Postnikov class resolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes the theoretical speed limit for performing Quantum Error Correction codes under strict U(1) charge conservation. By analyzing error metrics using advanced mathematical techniques, the authors prove that diffusion processes impose a quadratic ($\Omega(n^2)$) lower bound on how fast these codes can be prepared. This identifies diffusion itself as the fundamental operational bottleneck in symmetry-constrained quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep connection between fundamental physical constraints (like charge conservation/U(1) symmetry) and computational resource limits (encoding time), which is crucial for designing scalable quantum memory architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research determines the fundamental diffusive speed limits governing U(1)-covariant Quantum Error Correction (QEC) when subjected to flagged erasures.

**Main result.** Diffusion of the logical charge enforces an $\Omega(n^2)$ lower bound on the encoding time for local number-conserving circuits, establishing diffusion as a key operational limit in symmetry-constrained quantum coding.

**Method.** The analysis combines exact complementary-channel geometry, charge-sector Haar analysis, and gate-resolved connected-moment expansions to derive rigorous bounds on error metrics.

**Model / system.** The system models one-dimensional arrays of nearest-neighbor coupled cells encoding a logical qubit under strict U(1) covariance. The study focuses on Charge-Haar codes and local brickwork ensembles.

**Key observables.** Decoupling Error ($DE(V)$), classical mean ($M_t$), centered two-copy fluctuation ($\Gamma$), and the total variation distance ($	ext{dTV}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling laws are characterized by $n^{-1/2}$ for equilibrium error bounds, $\Omega(n^2)$ for time lower bounds, and $O(n^3)$ for classical mixing bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the strict adherence to U(1) covariance and the analysis being restricted initially to single-distinguished-particle classes or specific local ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the information-theoretic optimum for charge-Haar codes, then deriving dynamical bounds using moment expansions, identifying diffusion as the bottleneck, and finally relating this to mixing times in random lattice processes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast preparation of quantum error-correcting codes is essential for scalable quantum memories, but geometric locality and $U(1)$ charge conservation impose an unavoidable transport constraint. We combine exact complementary-channel geometry, charge-sector Haar analysis, and a gate-resolved connected-moment expansion to study one-dimensional covariant encoders under flagged erasure. Charge-Haar codes attain the universal adjacent-charge lower bound up to exponentially small corrections, yielding an exact $n^{-1/2}$ extensive-erasure law and a sharp half-erasure transition. For local number-conserving brickwork circuits, diffusion of the logical charge enforces an $Ω(n^2)$ encoding-time lower bound; we also prove an $O(n^3)$ mixing bound for the classical component and reduce the remaining full-channel upper bound to a source-restricted low-support operator-spreading problem. These results identify diffusion as an operational limit on symmetry-constrained quantum coding and establish a route to its exact formation time.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced algebraic formalism to characterize topological defects, such as domain walls, in 2D gapped quantum systems. It generalizes the concept of 'tube algebras' by showing that these defects are governed by comodule algebras rather than simple Hopf structures. This provides a powerful mathematical tool for classifying and understanding exotic excitations beyond simple point defects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework connecting abstract algebraic structures (comodule/multicomodule algebras) directly to physical concepts in condensed matter theory like topological order and defect physics, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work generalizes the tube algebra framework from characterizing bulk topological excitations to describing codimension-2 defects, such as domain walls, in (2+1)D gapped phases.

**Main result.** These defects are characterized by a 'defect tube algebra,' which naturally possesses the structure of a comodule algebra over the weak Hopf tube algebra associated with the bulk topological order.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced algebraic techniques, specifically utilizing comodule algebras and multicomodule algebras to classify defect structures arising from Turaev-Viro-Barrett-Westbury TQFTs.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to topological phases in (2+1)D, particularly those modeled by Levin-Wen string-net models. The focus is on the algebraic description of defects connecting multiple bulk phases.

**Key observables.** The representations of the defect tube algebras are used to characterize the boundary and domain wall defects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Codimension of the defect (2 for domain walls), number of adjacent phases ($N$), and the underlying unitary modular tensor category ($\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that defects are not fusion-closed, necessitating the use of a comodule structure rather than a simple coproduct.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how a point defect connects two domain walls described by bimodules $\mathcal{M}$ and $\mathcal{N}$, showing the surrounding tube regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon the known bulk theory (tube algebra as weak Hopf algebra) and extends it systematically to defects, defining the comodule structure for codimension-2 defects, and generalizing this to $N$-tuple and multicomodule algebras for higher defects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The tube algebra, which carries the structure of a $C^*$ weak Hopf algebra, is a fundamental tool for characterizing topological excitations in topological phases. In this work, we generalize the tube algebra framework to codimension-2 defects in $(2+1)$D gapped phases described by Turaev--Viro--Barrett--Westbury TQFTs, with particular emphasis on Levin--Wen string-net models. We show that such codimension-2 defects are described by a \emph{defect tube algebra}, which naturally carries the structure of a comodule algebra over the weak Hopf tube algebra associated with the topological excitations. Boundary and domain wall defects are then characterized by the representations of the corresponding boundary and domain wall defect tube algebras. In particular, a domain wall defect tube algebra can be regarded as a generalized Drinfeld double of the tube algebras associated with the two adjacent boundary defects. More generally, for a domain wall joining $N$ bulk phases, the associated domain wall defect tube algebra can be viewed as an $N$-tuple algebra. This perspective extends naturally to general $k$-defects, namely codimension-2 defects joining $k$ codimension-1 defects, for which the resulting generalized tube algebra carries the structure of a multicomodule algebra.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proves that incorporating shared classical randomness into shallow quantum generative models provides a provable and scalable advantage over purely unitary models. By generating long-range correlations measurable through covariance, the stochastic channel model can represent distributions unattainable by local unitaries at fixed depths. This has significant implications for understanding the limits of near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, quantifiable demonstration of how non-unitary classical control (shared randomness) can enhance the expressive power of quantum circuits beyond what is achievable by purely unitary evolution at limited depths, which has implications for near-term hardware constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if shared classical randomness can establish a strict, scalable representational separation between quantum generative models based on shallow local circuits and those augmented with this randomness.

**Main result.** Shared classical randomness is sufficient to establish a strict representational separation at shallow depth, allowing the generation of long-range correlations unattainable by purely unitary shallow-depth models.

**Method.** The authors compare the output distributions (via covariance measurements) generated by stochastic channel models versus their corresponding purely unitary counterparts. They use total variation distance bounds derived from these observables to prove the separation.

**Model / system.** The model uses Quantum Generative Models (QCBMs) based on shallow, bounded-connectivity quantum circuits acting on $N$ qubits. The key modification is augmenting the circuit with spatially separated local Pauli operations controlled by a single shared classical random bit.

**Key observables.** Covariance between measurements on distant qubits ($	ext{Cov}(\hat{Z}_1, \hat{Z}_6)$) and the Total Variation distance between the stochastic channel output and the unitary model output.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Shallow circuit depth (fixed), system size $N$, and the probability/control mechanism of the shared classical random bit ($s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes bounded connectivity for the local circuits and relies on specific structural properties of the quantum state evolution under stochastic Pauli operations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates that adding shared randomness allows generating non-zero long-range correlations ($	ext{Cov} 
eq 0$) in shallow circuits, which purely unitary models cannot achieve. Figure 7 shows this realization using Measurement-Based Quantum Computation (MBQC).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting limitations of shallow unitaries; it then introduces shared classical randomness as a resource to augment the model; the core is the theoretical proof showing separation via correlation measurements ($	ext{Cov} 
eq 0$); finally, it demonstrates feasibility using MBQC and learning frameworks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Near-term quantum hardware limits circuit depth and often imposes geometrically local connectivity for quantum generative models, restricting the output distributions accessible to shallow unitary Born models. Introducing stochasticity into a unitary quantum Born model can improve the empirical generative performance of the resulting channel model and, for a restricted small-scale architecture, has been proven to represent a strictly larger family of distributions than its unitary counterpart. However, whether such randomness provides a provable separation at fixed shallow depth for arbitrarily large systems has remained open. Here, we show that shared classical randomness, a comparatively weak resource from entanglement theory, is sufficient to establish such a strict scalable representational separation over the corresponding shallow unitary Born model. More specifically, we augment bounded-connectivity shallow unitary circuits, followed by computational-basis measurements, with spatially separated local Pauli operations, whose joint application is controlled by a single classically sampled random bit. The resulting shallow-depth channel model generates long-range correlations in the classical output distribution that no purely unitary shallow-depth model with bounded connectivity can reproduce. For one-dimensional nearest-neighbour architectures, reproducing such distributions with a purely unitary model can require depth $Ω(N)$ in the worst case. We further show that measurement-based quantum computation (MBQC) provides a natural implementation of the required shared classical randomness through suitable adaptation of the random measurement outcomes. Numerical experiments on MBQC-based generative models support the analytical results.

</details>

**Authors:** Ryutaroh Matsumoto

</figure>
