**Summary.** This paper introduces a refined concept called 'information locality' for Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs) to improve upon existing overestimations of required resources for erasure correction. Using advanced linear algebra on code subspaces, the authors derive constructive methods and prove that their proposed measure yields tighter bounds than previous quantum locality definitions. This work is vital for designing efficient and resource-aware fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It advances the theoretical understanding of fault-tolerance in quantum computation by refining the metrics used to quantify local error correction capabilities, which is crucial for building scalable quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a quantum counterpart of 'information locality' for Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs) to provide a more accurate measure of the necessary codeword symbols required for erasure correction than existing methods.

**Main result.** The proposed information locality definition is shown to be superior to previous definitions by providing tighter bounds, although limitations exist when applying it to general QECCs beyond stabilizer codes.

**Method.** The authors use linear algebraic procedures involving punctured and shortened code constructions ($\pi_J(C)$ and $\sigma_J(C^{\perp} \otimes h)$) to derive and compare different locality parameters for quantum stabilizer codes.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs), specifically stabilizer codes $Q(C)$, which are complex subspaces of the tensor product Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}_{\mathbb{C}_q}$. The analysis involves local measurements and recovery procedures.

**Key observables.** Locality parameters $(r, \delta)$; dimension of punctured/shortened codes ($\dim \pi_J(C)$); minimum Hamming weights ($w_H$); quantum local distance ($\delta_Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code length $n$, code dimension $k$; field size $q$; locality parameters $(r, \delta)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the structure of stabilizer codes and specific linear algebraic decompositions; limitations are noted when assuming general QECCs or when comparing classical vs. quantum settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews classical locality definitions, proposes a quantum information locality based on punctured code dimensions, develops constructive proofs (Propositions 1 and 7) to compute the required repair group size, compares this new measure against symbol locality, and finally discusses limitations using counterexamples.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A classical linear code $C$ of length $n$ is said to have symbol locality $(r, δ)$ if for any index $j$ there exists a repair group $J_j \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}$ with $j\in J_j$ and $|J_j| \leq r+δ-1$ such that any $δ-1$ or fewer erasures in $J_j$ can be corrected by using codeword symbols only in $J_j$. Later it turned out that this way of defining $r$ overestimates the number of necessary codeword symbols for multiple-erasure correction, and information locality was proposed to define $r$ as the dimension of the punctured code of $C$ onto $J_j$. Recently locality $(r,δ)$ was proposed for quantum error-correcting codes by following the original definition of symbol locality $(r, δ)$. We propose a quantum counterpart of the information locality for quantum stabilizer codes constructed by Hermitian orthogonality, and a linear algebraic procedure computing a smaller repair group predicted by the proposed information locality and simultaneously reducing the number of measured observables in decoding to its minimum possible value. Then we demonstrate that the previously proposed definition of quantum locality $(r,δ)$ has the same drawback of overestimating the number of necessary codeword symbols for erasure correction by providing an explicit example of a quantum stabilizer code. Finally, we will give another example of a quantum stabilizer code constructed by Euclidean orthogonality and two different linear codes, with which a natural translation of the classical information locality into the quantum setting underestimates the number of necessary codeword symbols for erasure correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel quantum-assisted protocol for transmitting symbols by encoding them across multiple temporal modes using LM05 operations. It rigorously analyzes the security against collective eavesdropping attacks and quantifies performance limits based on physical constraints like fiber loss, timing jitter, and detector dead time. The work advances the theory of quantum direct communication but notes that practical implementation faces severe engineering hurdles.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed theoretical framework for quantum communication protocols that leverage multiplexing in temporal modes, extending security analysis beyond simple single-channel assumptions to tackle collective attacks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum-assisted classical communication protocol that encodes symbols from a finite alphabet onto temporal modes using the LM05 operation as a building block.

**Main result.** It derives theoretical upper bounds on the success probability for complete recovery and establishes an asymptotic collective-attack bound, showing the tensor product structure allows attacks beyond standard proofs.

**Method.** Security analysis involves deriving information leakage bounds using entropic uncertainty relations (EUR) applied to the full N-photon block, alongside analyzing classical ordering costs.

**Model / system.** The model uses temporal-mode multiplexing across N parallel channels, where each channel implements an LM05 operation. The message is split into an unordered quantum symbol set and a classical ordering permutation.

**Key observables.** Theoretical upper bound on success probability ($P_{	ext{success}}$), secret key rate ($r$), information leakage $I(PM; E)$, and timing misidentification probability ($P_{	ext{misID}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Alphabet size (N=53), Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER), Transmission efficiency ($\eta_T$), Fiber distance ($d$), and detector dead time ($	au_{	ext{dead}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes perfect loss identification, uniform message distribution, ideal detectors, and that the classical ordering information must be encrypted for true QSDC.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the theoretical upper bound on $P_{	ext{success}}$ vs. fiber distance for N=53; other figures/tables relate to rate calculations based on jitter or detector dead time.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the protocol's structure (quantum symbol set + classical permutation), derives security bounds against collective attacks, compares resource costs (QKD vs. classical keying), and finally assesses practical limitations imposed by timing jitter and detector dead time.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce and analyze a quantum-assisted classical communication protocol that encodes symbols from a finite alphabet onto temporal modes using the LM05 operation as a building block. The protocol applies a bit-flip gate independently to temporal slots corresponding to message symbols, yielding a tensor product of LM05 operations on parallel channels. This tensor product structure enables collective attacks not covered by standard LM05 security proofs. We derive a theoretical upper bound on the success probability for complete recovery under random guessing, accounting for the receiver's 50\% guessing ability on lost photons, and emphasize that this bound assumes perfect loss identification. We characterize the intended transmission, derive an asymptotic collective-attack bound for the symbol-set mode, and identify open challenges for composable security. The protocol is not a standalone Quantum Secure Direct Communication scheme, as the classical ordering information requires encryption. For a 53-symbol alphabet, the optimistic 1\% success bound occurs at about 0.8 kilometers under zero QBER and reduces to about 0.6 kilometers for QBER equals 0.01; practical constraints severely limit performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This perspective argues that quantum computing's progress requires equally developing both foundational algorithms (bottom-up) and direct links to industrial decision problems (top-down). The key challenge is building explicit interfaces that translate abstract quantum outputs into actionable, domain-relevant observables. This co-design approach is necessary for realizing transformative commercial value.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level, strategic framework relevant to all theoretical quantum physicists by framing the necessary intersection between fundamental physics research and applied computational utility.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is closing the 'Application Gap' in quantum computing, moving from theoretical algorithmic speedups to demonstrable industrial impact driven by real-world needs.

**Main result.** Sustained progress requires treating bottom-up development (physics/hardware/algorithms) and top-down development (industrial needs) as equally primary and continuously coupled.

**Method.** The paper uses conceptual argument, analogy drawing from historical technological shifts (like classical computing), and analysis of required interfaces to bridge the gap.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the computational ecosystem surrounding quantum computation rather than a specific physical Hamiltonian. It discusses algorithmic paradigms applicable to various industrial problems.

**Key observables.** Industrial impact; the ability of a technology to measurably influence real decisions (e.g., go/no-go choices, design trade-offs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Algorithmic tractability; the coupling between technical capabilities and domain context.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The success of quantum computing hinges on establishing organizational structures and interfaces to connect abstract quantum outputs to concrete industrial decision points.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but notes reference figures illustrating algorithm speedup spectra and simplified views of the Quantum Phase Estimation process.

**Paper structure.** The paper argues for a dual-viewpoint approach: coupling bottom-up algorithmic development with top-down industrial needs. It analyzes historical parallels to show how this coupling must be built explicitly through interfaces.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing is a deep technology whose progress cannot be driven effectively from one direction alone. While the field has developed a growing catalogue of mathematically grounded algorithmic speedups, industrial impact will depend just as much on starting from real industrial decision contexts and working downward to what must be computed, validated and integrated. In this Perspective, I argue that sustained progress requires treating these two directions: bottom-up development from physics, hardware and algorithms, and top-down development from industrial needs and constraints. Equally primary and continuously coupled. This dual-viewpoint is not a matter of balance for its own sake. Quantum computers cannot solve arbitrary problems, so engagement with industry must remain anchored in algorithmic tractability. Yet tractable computations are rarely valuable unless they connect to decision points in established workflows such as candidate selection in drug discovery or the design of a new aircraft shape with improved aerodynamics. I analyse how historical narratives and structural separations of expertise slowed the formation of this coupling and outline what it takes to build it: explicit interfaces between technical teams and domain context and intermediate layers that translate quantum outputs into decision-relevant observables without suffocating foundational innovation. Framed this way, quantum computing's opportunity is clearest where deep physical modelling meets high-value decisions. Provided the field co-designs both sides from the outset.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes that imaginary coherences between computational basis states are a necessary resource for training parameters in QAOA. By deriving rigorous bounds using adjoint channels and testing them against Max-Cut simulations under various noise models, the authors quantify how quantum state structure dictates algorithmic trainability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical insights into the practical limitations of variational quantum algorithms like QAOA, linking optimization convergence directly to fundamental quantum coherence properties (imaginarity).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the necessary quantum resources required for a non-zero gradient when training parameters in the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA). It specifically investigates the role of imaginary coherences between candidate solutions.

**Main result.** Imaginarity, quantified by specific coherence measures, is shown to be a necessary but not sufficient condition for obtaining a non-zero gradient during QAOA optimization. The derived bounds successfully constrain the magnitude of the gradient across various noise models.

**Method.** The authors derive analytical upper bounds on the gradient using quantum calculus and the adjoint channel formalism, comparing these theoretical limits against numerical simulations in Max-Cut instances.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on QAOA applied to combinatorial optimization problems like Max-Cut. The system involves a cost Hamiltonian ($H_C$) and a transverse-field mixer ($H_B$), analyzed under various noise channels (phase-flip, amplitude damping, depolarizing).

**Key observables.** Imaginarity ($	ext{Im}(ho_{zz'})$), restricted $\ell_1$ coherence ($\mathcal{C}^{(1)}$), and the gradient magnitude $|\partial_eta F_\lambda|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The QAOA parameters $(\gamma, eta)$, noise strength $\lambda$, and graph structure (e.g., 3-regular graphs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on specific Hamiltonian structures (diagonal $H_C$) and the validity of adjoint channel bounds for different noise types.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the calculated gradient magnitude against the theoretical adjoint-channel bound, showing agreement. Other figures track Hamming-one imaginarity ($I^{(1)}$) and coherence ($\mathcal{C}^{(1)}$) across varying noise strengths and graph structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the general necessary condition (imaginarity) in the noiseless case to deriving bounds for terminal noise. It then extends this analysis by examining how different physical noise channels affect these bounds, culminating in numerical comparisons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) tackles combinatorial problems by tuning a quantum circuit in a classical loop, often guided by gradients. We show that the gradient used to tune the circuit's final parameter is bounded by imaginarity, which weights phase relationships between candidate solutions by how strongly the circuit connects them and how differently the problem scores them. Imaginarity is necessary but not sufficient for a nonzero gradient. We extend the bound to three common noise models and compare it numerically with the gradient in Max-Cut simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an automated logging framework inspired by MLflow to track the entire provenance of quantum circuit compilation within Qiskit. By automatically capturing details from every transpilation pass and backend characteristic, it significantly improves compiler observability. This allows researchers to reproduce results and pinpoint exactly which optimization steps affect final circuit performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on software tools, understanding the transformation steps (passes) applied to quantum circuits is crucial for optimizing algorithms intended for near-term hardware, which has implications for resource estimation in AMO/QIP.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Understanding quantum compilation effectiveness requires visibility into the entire transpilation process, not just final circuit metrics.

**Main result.** The proposed autologging framework successfully captures comprehensive provenance data automatically, enabling reproducible evaluation of complex quantum compilation workflows.

**Method.** It implements an MLflow-inspired autologging framework for Qiskit that transparently instruments the transpilation and execution workflow to capture detailed compiler provenance.

**Model / system.** The focus is on abstract quantum circuits executed via Qiskit, targeting simulated or abstract backends (e.g., IQMFakeAphrodite) rather than specific physical hardware details.

**Key observables.** Transpilation stages, pass-level execution data (C7 Passes), backend characteristics, compiler configuration, circuit metric evolution, and overall provenance metadata (Q1-E7).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimization levels used during compilation (e.g., level 2 vs. level 3) are key parameters for comparison.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes compatibility with the existing QProv provenance model and is designed to be extensible to other quantum SDKs like Qrisp and PennyLane.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system architecture (Developer $ightarrow$ Qiskit $ightarrow$ MLflow), the extended quantum provenance taxonomy, sample code usage, a transpilation pass timeline, and experiment comparison views in the MLflow UI.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of compilation observability, details the MLflow-inspired autologging framework extending QProv, demonstrates its use with Qiskit, and shows how the collected data facilitates comparative analysis of different compilation runs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the effectiveness of quantum compilation techniques requires visibility into the entire transpilation process, not just the final circuit metrics. This demonstration presents an MLflow-inspired autologging framework for Qiskit that automatically captures compiler provenance, including transpilation stages, pass-level execution data, backend characteristics, compiler configuration, and execution results. The framework extends the QProv provenance model with compiler-specific information and stores the collected data in an MLflow Tracking Server for analysis and visualization. By eliminating manual instrumentation, the proposed approach improves compiler observability and supports reproducible evaluation of quantum compilation workflows.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical challenge of accurately estimating resources for fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. The authors propose a novel programming language and a cross-layer framework that allows programmers to explicitly manage error correction while enabling joint analysis of resource trade-offs. This results in significantly more accurate and detailed resource estimates compared to existing black-box methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation theory, the systematic approach to resource accounting across multiple abstraction layers is highly relevant for developing robust simulation tools or understanding hardware overheads in any complex physical system modeled by layered control structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Resource estimation for fault-tolerant quantum computation is difficult because existing tools either force programmers to handle low-level hardware details or abstract away error correction schemes, leading to inaccurate resource utilization estimates.

**Main result.** The proposed framework enables substantial resource savings and provides detailed, fine-grained, and accurate resource estimates by jointly analyzing the logical program and its associated error-correction schemes.

**Method.** The authors introduce a specialized quantum programming language and a cross-layer resource-estimation framework that allows programmers to define QEC schemes explicitly while enabling joint analysis across different abstraction layers.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computation (FTQC), where logical qubits are encoded using physical qubits via Quantum Error-Correction Schemes ($\langle	ext{QEC}angle$). The framework models the compilation and execution flow from high-level logic down to hardware configuration.

**Key observables.** Space cost (# total physical qubits), Time cost (total runtime / syndrome-extraction cycles), and error rates (e.g., activate-operation vs. idle memory error).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distances ($d_{data}, d_{anc}$), the underlying physical error rate ($p_{phy} = 3 	imes 10^{-3}$), and mean decoding latency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires a joint, cross-layer analysis of the program and QEC specifications, which is more complex than independent estimation methods. It also assumes that decoding latency can be utilized by inserting intermediate statements.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the overall architecture of the resource estimation framework across multiple layers (Logical $ightarrow$ QEC Specification $ightarrow$ Hardware). Other figures show code examples for logical gates like $T$-gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem limitations, presents the novel programming language and cross-layer framework, details the joint analysis methodology, evaluates it on practical large-scale algorithms, and concludes with resource savings demonstrations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computation enables the deployment of practical quantum algorithms but incurs substantial overhead from error correction, making resource estimation a central concern. Beyond case-by-case analyses, existing quantum programming languages either require programmers to manipulate low-level hardware details, rendering fault-tolerant implementations cumbersome, or abstract away the underlying error-correction schemes, reducing the effectiveness of resource utilization and estimation.   To address these limitations while preserving programmability, we present a quantum programming language that enables efficient resource utilization, together with a resource-estimation framework for comprehensive resource analysis. Our framework features programmer-visible abstractions of error-correction schemes and cross-layer program-hardware analysis, allowing systematic exploration of resource trade-offs. We evaluate our approach on detailed fault-tolerant implementations of practical large-scale quantum algorithms, including components typically treated as black boxes in existing frameworks. The results demonstrate that our framework enables substantial resource savings while delivering detailed, fine-grained, and accurate resource estimates for fault-tolerant quantum programs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops the zero displacement ratio ($\zeta$) as a quantitative diagnostic tool to map out phase transitions in Fractional Quantum Hall systems by analyzing the geometry of wave function zeros. By comparing results from Laughlin models against realistic Coulomb interactions, the authors show that the zero structure collapses or shifts predictably when topological order is lost. The findings strongly support Composite Fermion descriptions over simpler crystal approximations for describing these correlated electron liquids.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of geometric properties (zero clustering) to diagnose topological order is a powerful, non-local probe that complements standard energy gap or correlation function measurements. This methodology could be adapted to study other exotic quantum phases where local observables are insufficient.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify how deviations from ideal model wave functions affect the topological properties of Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) systems, particularly during phase transitions.

**Main result.** The zero displacement ratio ($\zeta$) serves as a diagnostic tool: its statistical behavior reveals the transition from Laughlin liquid order to charge-ordered or crystalline phases. Furthermore, Composite Fermion Fluid descriptions provide an improved description of $
u=1/5$ states compared to simple crystal embeddings.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating and analyzing the geometric distribution (density maps) of zeros in the ground state wave functions using a zero displacement ratio ($\zeta$) derived from nearest neighbor zeros. Overlap amplitudes are also used to compare different theoretical approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is strongly interacting electrons confined to the Lowest Landau Level, modeled at fractional fillings like $
u=1/3$ and $
u=1/5$. The Hamiltonian is varied by mixing terms between simple pseudopotentials ($V_1$) and full Coulomb interactions ($H_C$).

**Key observables.** Zero displacement ratio ($\zeta$), density distribution $P(\phi)$ of the zero argument, Kurtosis ($K$) of this distribution, Overlap Amplitude ($O$), and Pair Correlation Function ($g(r, R)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions $
u=1/3$ and $
u=1/5$; mixing parameters ($\lambda$ or $\eta$); the ratio defining $\zeta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on studying specific small-N systems ($N=3$) to extract general scaling laws, and approximations are made when constructing Composite Fermion states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of zero patterns (density maps) as interaction strength changes, showing shifts in $\zeta$ peaks. Other figures track order parameters like $P(\phi)$ and overlap amplitudes ($O$) versus tuning parameters ($\lambda$ or $\eta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the geometry of zeros for different FQH states ($
u=1/3$, $
u=1/5$), introducing the zero displacement ratio as a diagnostic. It then compares results from idealized Laughlin models against realistic Coulomb interactions, culminating in the development and testing of Composite Fermion Fluid descriptions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The clustering pattern of zeros in the ground state of a fractional quantum Hall system is a defining feature of its topological properties. We analyze the geometrical fluctuations of the zeros around individual electrons and propose to use the displacement ratio of the zeros to visualize and measure the distance of a realistic state to a model wave function. The distribution of the zero displacement ratio behaves like an order parameter in the transition from a Laughlin phase to a topologically trivial one. The statistical comparison between quantum Hall states belonging to different Jain sequences leads to a composite fermion fluid description of the $ν= 1/5$ ground state with long-range Coulomb interaction that agrees almost perfectly for as few as $3$-$5$ electrons, overcoming the long-standing difficulties of accommodating the competing liquid and crystal orders at short distances.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the loss landscape of QAOA for MIS, finding that established theory based on Dynamical Lie Algebras fails in shallow circuits. Instead of barren plateaus, the landscapes exhibit 'cragged terrains' with polynomially increasing variance. The findings emphasize that empirical, data-driven models are necessary to characterize VQA hardness accurately.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a critical, empirically grounded challenge to highly abstract theoretical frameworks (like DLA) used to predict VQA performance, suggesting that practical limitations (shallow depth) invalidate deep mathematical assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if theoretical predictions based on Dynamical Lie Algebras (DLA) accurately describe the loss landscape scaling of shallow Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs), specifically for QAOA applied to MIS.

**Main result.** The DLA theory fails in the shallow-circuit regime, as empirical studies show that 'cragged terrains' (polynomial variance increase) are common, contradicting the predicted exponential vanishing variances associated with 2-design assumptions.

**Method.** A combination of large-scale numerical simulations across thousands of graph instances and training Empirical Hardness Models (EHMs) to predict instance-wise landscape metrics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on VQAs, particularly QAOA for the Maximum Independent Set (MIS) problem, using parametrized quantum circuits acting on an N-qubit Hilbert space. The theoretical framework involves analyzing the circuit ensemble's adherence to a 2-design property derived from DLA theory.

**Key observables.** Loss function variance ($	ext{Var}_{	heta}[\partial_\mu C(	heta)]$), Barren Plateau (BP) vs. Cragged Terrain scaling, and model prediction accuracy ($R^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth (shallow regime), system size ($N$), graph structure (random vs. vertex-transitive).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DLA theory assumes the circuit ensemble forms an approximate 2-design, which is shown to be insufficient for shallow circuits; EHMs are trained on finite subsets of data.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate GCNN predictions for gradient variance across different scaling regimes (BP vs. cragged terrains) using metrics like von Neumann entropy and edge density.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts the theoretical DLA prediction (based on 2-design theory) with empirical results from QAOA-MIS, demonstrating where the theory breaks down in shallow circuits. It concludes by validating the utility of data-driven EHMs over asymptotic unitary design theories for this regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The dynamical Lie algebraic (DLA) theory of variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) predicts commonplace exponentially vanishing loss and gradient variances for sufficiently deep parametrized circuits. In this work, we show that these predictions fail dramatically in the shallow-circuit (and particularly constant-depth) regime for the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) applied to the maximum independent set (MIS) problem. In a large-scale numerical study across $\sim$23,000 problem instances, we find that barren plateaus are rare, while landscapes whose variances polynomially increase with system size---which we term "cragged terrains"---are common across graph families. This aggregate polynomial growth persists both for generic, low-symmetry random graphs and for highly symmetric vertex-transitive graphs, indicating that DLA-based variance predictions do not describe landscape scaling in this regime. As a stopgap alternative to the theory, we train empirical hardness models to predict instance-wise hardness metrics for QAOA-MIS. While these models generalize poorly, they nonetheless recover the correct landscape scaling class (barren plateau vs. cragged terrain) with high fidelity. Taken together, our results identify shallow QAOA for MIS as a prototypical setting in which asymptotic, unitary-design-centric predictions may be fundamentally insufficient to describe shallow variational quantum algorithms more broadly, emphasizing the need for more empirically-informed models of VQA loss landscapes.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.04627v1>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the resource trade-offs (depth vs. ancillas) for computing fundamental functions like Hamming weight in quantum circuits. By comparing standard and dynamic models under different connectivity constraints, they show how constant depth can be achieved using a polynomial number of ancillary qubits. This work is vital for understanding the practical limits of implementing complex quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract quantum circuits, achieving constant depth with polynomial ancillary overhead is crucial for designing scalable quantum algorithms that can run efficiently on near-term hardware architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the optimal trade-offs between circuit depth and the number of ancillary qubits required to compute Hamming weight and general symmetric Boolean functions.

**Main result.** They establish that in dynamic quantum circuits, constant depth can be achieved for these computations, although this requires an ancillary qubit count scaling as $O(n^{1+\epsilon} 	ext{polylog } n)$.

**Method.** The authors employ circuit construction techniques by reducing the problem to weighted counting and applying analysis across different connectivity models (all-to-all vs. 2D) and circuit types (standard vs. dynamic).

**Model / system.** The study is purely theoretical, analyzing quantum circuits designed for specific computational tasks on abstract qubit architectures constrained by connectivity (all-to-all or 2D nearest-neighbor grid).

**Key observables.** Circuit Depth ($D$) and Ancilla Count ($	ext{Ancilla}$), relative to the input size $n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Connectivity model (All-to-all, 2D); Circuit Model (Standard vs. Dynamic); Input Size ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between output register space and ancillary workspace; dynamic models rely on measurement-based fan-out and cat-state recovery.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the high-level construction: a reduction to weighted counting, the dynamic weighted-counting primitive, and its implementation within a two-dimensional patch geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the depth-ancilla tradeoffs for Hamming weight computation first, then generalizes these results to arbitrary symmetric Boolean functions across four settings (standard/dynamic $	imes$ all-to-all/2D).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hamming weight computation maps an $n$-bit input to the number of ones it contains. It is a basic subroutine in quantum computing, and the core building block for symmetric Boolean functions, whose value depends only on the Hamming weight of the input. Moreover, symmetric Boolean functions are among the most common primitives in quantum computing. Efficient circuits for both problems are therefore important for the efficiency of many quantum algorithms. We study the depth-ancilla tradeoffs of Hamming weight computation under two qubit connectivity models, all-to-all and two-dimensional nearest-neighbor square grid (2D), in both the standard and dynamic circuit models. In the standard all-to-all model, we obtain depth $O(\log n)$ with a sublinear number of ancillas. In the standard 2D model, we give a circuit of depth $O(\sqrt n)$ with $O(\log^2 n)$ ancillas, and a matching lower bound showing that $Θ(\sqrt n)$ is optimal. In both dynamic models, we obtain constant-depth circuits with $O(n^{1+\varepsilon}\operatorname{polylog}\,n)$ ancillary qubits for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$. All constructions give a smooth depth-ancilla tradeoff, and they also extend to arbitrary symmetric Boolean functions.

</details>

**Summary.** This study benchmarks Hamiltonian simulation resources for hydrogen chains using different molecular orbital bases and truncation methods. It finds that employing localized orbitals (LMOs) with locality-based truncation yields a dramatically better scaling of quantum gate requirements—polylogarithmic versus polynomial—compared to canonical molecular orbitals (CMOs). This provides critical guidance for designing efficient quantum algorithms for large-scale quantum chemistry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource estimation in quantum chemistry simulations, providing concrete guidelines on how basis set choice (localization) can fundamentally change the complexity scaling from polynomial to polylogarithmic, which is crucial for feasibility studies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the choice of molecular orbital basis set (CMO vs. LMO) and Hamiltonian truncation strategy impacts the required quantum resources for simulating electronic structure via Trotter decomposition.

**Main result.** The analysis shows an exponential advantage for using localized orbitals (LMOs) combined with locality-based Hamiltonian truncation, leading to a polylogarithmic growth in gate count compared to the polynomial growth seen with canonical molecular orbitals (CMOs).

**Method.** The authors compare quantum resource estimation (quantum gate counts) derived from simulating hydrogen chains using first-order Trotter decomposition across different basis sets and truncation methods.

**Model / system.** The model is one-dimensional hydrogen chain systems ($	ext{H}_n$), analyzed by constructing a qubit Hamiltonian via the Jordan–Wigner transformation. The core comparison involves two orbital bases: Hartree–Fock canonical molecular orbitals (CMOs) and Pipek–Mezey localized molecular orbitals (LMOs).

**Key observables.** Number of quantum gates ($\#	ext{Clifford}(1q)$, $\#	ext{CNOT}$, $\#	ext{Rz}$), ground-state energy, and fidelity ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (up to $	ext{H}_{100}$), truncation thresholds ($	ext{c}_{	ext{thre}}$ or $k_{\max}$), and the bond dimension ($D$) for related DMRG convergence checks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on first-order Trotter decomposition, and comparisons are made between truncated and full Hamiltonians to estimate resource scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the procedure of orbital localization (for $	ext{H}_{10}$) and show the distribution of rotational angles ($	ext{Rz}(	heta)$ gates) for larger chains ($	ext{H}_{50}$), comparing original vs. truncated Hamiltonians.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares computational costs by first analyzing short chains (ground-state energy/fidelity) to determine optimal truncation thresholds, and then extrapolating these findings to estimate the scaling of quantum gate resources for very long chains ($	ext{H}_{100}$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how molecular orbitals used as the basis of wave function expansion and how operator coefficient-based and locality-based Hamiltonian truncation affects the computational cost of Trotter decomposition-based Hamiltonian simulation in one-dimensional hydrogen chain systems. The analysis is performed using both Hartree--Fock canonical molecular orbitals (CMOs) and Pipek--Mezey-based localized molecular orbitals (LMOs). For short hydrogen chains, we evaluate the ground-state energy and fidelity and find that, in the CMO-based wave function expansion, introducing a threshold on Hamiltonian coefficients is effective in reducing the gate cost while maintaining computational accuracy. In contrast, in the LMO-based wave function expansion, operator locality-based Hamiltonian truncation is found to be more effective. By fitting the relationship between the truncation threshold and the ground-state energies and fidelities with empirical formulas, we estimate the threshold values required to achieve high fidelity ($F \ge 0.99$) in the ground-state wave function. Using the estimated thresholds, we then perform quantum gate resource estimation for longer hydrogen chains up to H$_{100}$. The results suggest an exponential advantage of the LMO-based wave function expansion with Hamiltonian truncation: the number of quantum gates required for Hamiltonian simulation grows polynomially when the CMO-based wave function expansion with operator coefficient-based Hamiltonian truncation is adopted, whereas it grows polylogarithmically when the LMO-based wave function expansion is combined with operator locality-based Hamiltonian truncation. These results provide useful guidelines for choosing orbital representations and Hamiltonian truncation strategies in large-scale quantum chemical simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that the Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida (RKKY) interaction can act as a universal probe for characterizing exotic, odd-parity magnets generated in antiferromagnets using Floquet engineering. By analyzing distinct magnetic signatures within the RKKY coupling—such as specific sign changes and symmetry constraints on the DM term—the authors provide a clear theoretical pathway to experimentally verify these complex quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, symmetry-based theoretical diagnostic (RKKY) that links abstract concepts like odd-parity spin polarization and Floquet engineering to measurable magnetic responses accessible via techniques like STM spectroscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a method to detect both spin splitting and confirm the odd-parity nature of exotic magnets predicted to emerge in collinear antiferromagnets via Floquet engineering.

**Main result.** The RKKY interaction serves as a unified magnetic probe, yielding distinct signatures like magnetism reversals in Heisenberg/Ising terms and specific symmetries in the DM term that allow clear discrimination of these novel odd-parity magnets.

**Method.** The study utilizes theoretical calculations based on the Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida (RKKY) interaction, which is derived using Green's functions applied to effective Floquet Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The model focuses on collinear $f$-wave magnets realized in 2D collinear antiferromagnets on a honeycomb lattice, compared against baseline systems like graphene and standard AFMs. The physics is governed by an effective low-energy Floquet Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Distinct magnetic signals from the RKKY interaction: magnetism reversal in Heisenberg/Ising terms, sign alternation of the DM term, and confirmation of odd-parity symmetry ($\mathcal{J}^{	ext{DM}}(\mathbf{R}) = -\mathcal{J}^{	ext{DM}}(C_2^q\mathbf{R})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed in the high-frequency off-resonant regime of Floquet theory, and the results are shown to be general for $p$-wave magnets ($q=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the RKKY interaction can effectively probe the underlying spin polarization features generated by the Floquet driving.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates band dispersions and Fermi surfaces for various magnetic systems (graphene, AFM, f-wave magnet). Figure 2 compares the calculated RKKY components ($J^H, J^I, J^{DM}$) versus impurity distance $R$ for different material types.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using Floquet engineering to generate odd-parity magnets. It then introduces and applies the RKKY interaction as a diagnostic tool, systematically comparing its calculated components across various magnetic models to isolate the unique signatures of the target f-wave magnet.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Odd-parity magnets were recently proposed to emerge in collinear antiferromagnets (AFMs) via Floquet engineering, with valley-dependent spin splitting underlying the odd-parity spin polarization. This proposal brings about two key challenges: detecting the spin splitting to verify the generation mechanism of these magnets, and identifying such polarization to confirm the odd-parity nature. Here, we show that the Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida (RKKY) interaction provides a unified magnetic probe for both tasks. Taking collinear $f$-wave magnets as a representative example, we find that the RKKY interaction yields distinct magnetic signals of the spin splitting---including a magnetism reversal in the Heisenberg/Ising terms and a sign alternation of the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) term---that enable clear discrimination of collinear $f$-wave magnets from other related AFMs. Moreover, the DM term exhibits an $f$-wave shape with odd-parity symmetry, satisfying $J^{αβ}_{DM}(\mathbf{R}) = -J^{αβ}_{DM}(C_{2q}\mathbf{R})$ ($q=3$), which directly reflects the odd-parity spin polarization $S_z(\mathbf{k}) = -S_z(C_{2q}\mathbf{k})$ in momentum space. This behavior persists in $p$-wave magnets ($q=1$), demonstrating the generality of our approach. Our work establishes the RKKY interaction as a versatile probe for detecting band features of collinear odd-parity magnets, with predictions accessible to existing experimental techniques such as spin-polarized scanning tunneling spectroscopy.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces two robust theoretical methods to calculate the Chern number of Bloch bands by analyzing phase singularities directly within the eigenstates, rather than integrating the Berry curvature. These methods are successfully applied to a photonic lattice experiment, confirming that the resulting topological invariant is quantized and independent of gauge choice.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimentally accessible route to topological invariants in condensed matter systems using only measurements of wavefunction phases/zeros, which is highly relevant for quantum optics and photonics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes novel methods to directly calculate topological invariants, specifically Chern numbers, of Bloch bands by analyzing phase singularities (vorticities) in the eigenstates, bypassing traditional Berry curvature integration.

**Main result.** Two distinct methods—the 0-section method and the tautological bundle method—were successfully applied to demonstrate that the calculated Chern number is robustly quantized and independent of gauge choice, even when using experimental data from photonic lattices.

**Method.** The core methodology involves identifying zeros (singularities) in continuous sections of complex line bundles formed by Bloch eigenvectors. This leads to calculating topological indices or winding numbers at these zero points across the Brillouin zone torus.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to two-dimensional insulators, demonstrated using a photonic lattice experiment simulating a quantum walk model governed by Floquet operators. The analysis uses concepts from Bloch band theory and complex geometry.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($C_n$), phase vorticities in eigenstates structure, local indices ($	ext{Ind}_{ar{k}_j}$), and winding numbers ($W_j[\sigma]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is characterized by parameters governing the photonic lattice or the Floquet Hamiltonian, leading to topological phases (e.g., $C^+ = -2$ vs. $C^+=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** For the 0-section method, it is crucial that the eigenvector does not vanish on the plaquette when using raw experimental data; for classical wave systems, non-normalization of eigenvectors is exploited.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate reconstructed eigenvectors ($	ilde{\alpha}^+, 	ilde{eta}^+$) in both topologically trivial ($C=0$) and topological ($C^+=-2$) regimes. They show the relationship between measured data, analytical reconstructions, and the resulting Chern number calculation via index summation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops two theoretical methods (0-section and tautological bundle) for calculating $C_n$ from eigenstate zeros. It then applies these methods to a photonic lattice model derived from Floquet theory, demonstrating gauge invariance by comparing results across different gauges and calculation techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose two methods to extract the Chern numbers of the Bloch bands of a two dimensional insulator, and apply them to a photonic lattice experiment. These two methods require the knowledge of the complex-valued components of the Bloch eigenvectors, or their ratios. Unlike other tomography methods relying on the approximate reconstruction of the Berry curvature and its integration over the Brillouin zone, our methods boil down to the observation of phase vorticities in the eigensates structure. Those measurements, robust to experimental noise, yield exactly quantized values. One of the two methods exploits the non-normalization of the eigenmodes, making it particularly relevant for classical wave systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes wave damping in weakly viscous deep-water waves by extending the Dysthe theory using multiple-scales analysis. The authors compare the results from this rigorous fluid dynamics framework against an independent phase-lag model for mean-flow interaction. They conclude that while higher orders reveal specific coupling terms, the predicted frequency-dependent damping correction arises naturally only in the simplified time-adjustment model, suggesting a gap between the two theoretical approaches.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on fluid dynamics, the mathematical techniques—such as multi-scale analysis, deriving higher-order nonlinear evolution equations, and modeling delayed feedback via relaxation kernels—are highly relevant to open quantum systems where memory effects or non-Markovian damping are crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors investigate extending weakly viscous Dysthe wave theory by determining if the standard weak-viscosity framework can systematically generate a complex multiplier associated with nonlinear mean-flow damping.

**Main result.** They show that while higher-order analysis reveals specific direct viscous contributions at sixth order, an independent finite-adjustment-time model predicts a phase-lag correction entering at fifth order, suggesting the systematic origin of this effect is not guaranteed by the standard weak-viscosity reduction.

**Method.** The work employs multiple-scales perturbation analysis on the Navier-Stokes equations, extending the Dias-Dyachenko-Zakharov (DDZ) framework, and independently utilizes a finite-adjustment-time model for mean-flow response.

**Model / system.** The system is two-dimensional free-surface gravity waves in an incompressible, weakly viscous fluid of infinite depth. The analysis builds upon the DDZ weak-viscosity reduction of the full Navier-Stokes equations.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal mean-flow interaction terms ($\mathcal{A}c_M$), the complex multiplier $(1 + ieta)$, and the effective frequency-dependent damping coefficient $eta_{	ext{eff}}(\Omega) = \Omega	au$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak-viscosity scaling ($ar{
u} = \epsilon^2
u$) and the small parameter $\epsilon$ (wave steepness). The adjustment time $	au$ is a key parameter in the phase-lag model.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DDZ reduction assumes potential flow supplemented by boundary condition modifications; the phase-lag model treats mean-flow relaxation as an independent, reduced dynamics problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical system and the leading Dysthe theory (DDZ reduction) to performing high-order perturbation analysis (fifth and sixth order solvability conditions), culminating in a comparison between these results and an independent, exact phase-lag model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We extend the Carter-Govan multiple-scales analysis of weakly viscous, narrowband deep-water wave packets beyond Dysthe order within the potential-flow reduction of Dias, Dyachenko, and Zakharov (DDZ). We seek to determine whether this framework generates the complex multiplier $(1 + iβ)$ used phenomenologically to modify the nonlocal Dysthe mean-flow interaction. Although order counting places a direct viscous carrier-mean interaction at sixth order, it does not exclude an indirect fifth-order contribution arising from viscosity dependent lower-order harmonics and nonlinear interactions. We therefore derive the first correction to the induced mean flow and the complete fifth-order first-harmonic solvability condition. The resulting nonlocal terms are derivative--dependent and contain no explicit viscosity, excluding the proposed indirect mechanism within the DDZ framework. At sixth order, a restricted calculation of the nonlinear viscous block isolates a direct carrier-mean contribution with the same operator structure as the imaginary component of the prescribed mean-flow correction. Independently, a finite-adjustment-time model yields an exact, frequency dependent mean-flow response. Its low-frequency expansion produces the multiplier $ 1 + iβ_{\mathrm{eff}}(Ω)$, with $β_{\mathrm{eff}}(Ω) =Ωτ.$ When $Ωτ= \mathcal O(ε)$, the resulting phase-lag correction enters at fifth order, one order beyond the leading Dysthe mean-flow interaction.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Figure 2 shows the width dependence of Bs at ﬁnite temperatures. Since ξD is temperature independent, the dimensionless width w = W/ξD of a given strip is ﬁxed. Both crossover widths increase with temperature because the characteristic length scale of the superconducting re- sponse grows as T approaches Tc. Near Tc, this increase can be attributed to the divergence of ξGL(T ). The inset</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops a microscopic theory to accurately predict when a vortex-free superconducting thin film becomes unstable due to an applied magnetic field. By formulating the stability problem directly, it resolves ambiguities present in older models and maps out three distinct physical regimes based on the strip's width relative to characteristic lengths. The work is significant for understanding superconductivity in confined geometries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the rigorous stability analysis of a macroscopic quantum state (the superconducting order parameter) under external fields provides valuable techniques for analyzing phase transitions in complex many-body systems relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To microscopically determine the field-induced instability of the vortex-free state in superconducting thin-film strips, overcoming ambiguities found in older theories.

**Main result.** The theory establishes three distinct width regimes for instability: 1D instability, edge-selective 2D mode instability, and localized modes. It shows that for narrow strips, the classical Pearl–London picture fails qualitatively.

**Method.** Stability is analyzed by examining the quadratic variation of the Gibbs free energy functional using Usadel theory formalism, considering both 1D and 2D perturbations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a homogeneous dirty superconducting thin-film strip subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field. The analysis uses theoretical tools derived from superconductivity theory (Usadel equations).

**Key observables.** Instability field ($B_s$), critical wave number ($k^*$), and the order parameter amplitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strip width ($W$), temperature ($T$), and magnetic field strength ($B$). The analysis identifies crossover widths $W_1(T)$ and $W_2(T)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume a homogeneous strip, negligible self-field effects, and no applied transport current.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the width dependence of $B_s$ at $T=0$, marking the three instability regimes. Figure 2 shows how these critical fields change with temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the stability analysis by first considering 1D perturbations, then extending to 2D modes using Fourier decomposition for longitudinal wave numbers ($k$). The results are synthesized to define $B_s$ as the minimum instability field across all considered modes and regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the Pearl--London theory, the edge-barrier-disappearance field of a superconducting thin-film strip depends on an arbitrary short-distance core cutoff because the vortex is treated as a point object. The theory does not determine the cutoff or how it depends on temperature $T$, and therefore cannot determine the $T$ dependence of the instability field. Here we formulate the microscopic stability problem directly for the vortex-free superconducting state. This removes the core-cutoff ambiguity and determines the instability field $B_s$ over the full temperature range and across all width regimes considered here. For a homogeneous dirty strip with negligible self-field, three width regimes occur. For $W<W_1(T)$, superconductivity disappears continuously into the normal state through a one-dimensional (1D) instability. For $W_1(T)<W<W_2(T)$, an edge-selective two-dimensional (2D) long-wavelength mode becomes unstable. For $W>W_2(T)$, the critical wave number is finite and the unstable mode is localized near an edge. In the wide-strip limit, $B_s\propto1/W$, recovering the Pearl--London scaling. In sufficiently narrow strips, however, the Pearl--London edge-barrier picture fails qualitatively.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines the effect of a magnetic field on an excitonic insulator phase using a four-Fermi model. It finds that the magnetic field enhances the excitonic order parameter, demonstrating a form of magnetic catalysis. Furthermore, it predicts characteristic changes in the Hall conductivity that serve as complementary signatures for detecting this type of electronic ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter theory, the study of emergent collective phases like excitonic insulators under external fields provides insights into many-body ground states that are relevant to strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how a perpendicular magnetic field affects the excitonic insulator (EI) phase and its associated Hall conductivity in planar fermionic systems.

**Main result.** The magnetic field induces an 'excitonic realization of magnetic catalysis' by enhancing the EI condensate, while the Hall conductivity exhibits characteristic changes accompanying this ordering transition.

**Method.** The analysis employs the large-$N$ approximation within a mean-field framework to derive and minimize the thermodynamic potential for coupled scalar and excitonic condensates.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using an extended planar four-Fermi model (extended Gross-Neveu model) describing electron-hole coherence interactions in the semiconductor regime. The Hamiltonian incorporates minimal coupling to a perpendicular magnetic field.

**Key observables.** EI condensate ($\eta_c$), critical temperature ($T_c$), Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), and phase structure changes (e.g., tricritical point).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perpendicular magnetic field ($B$), finite temperature ($T$), and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-$N$ approximation and treats the system using a homogeneous saddle-point mean-field approach, neglecting collective phase fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the text describes analyzing field dependence of condensates and Hall conductivity near transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the model (Section II), derives the thermodynamic potential in zero field, establishes gap equations for equilibrium condensates, and then extends this framework to incorporate a perpendicular magnetic field (Section III).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the effects of a perpendicular magnetic field on the excitonic insulator (EI) phase in the semiconductor regime using an extended planar four-Fermi model. Within the large-$N$ approximation, we determine the coupled scalar and excitonic condensates at finite temperature, chemical potential, and magnetic field. The field enhances the EI condensate and raises its critical temperature, providing an excitonic realization of magnetic catalysis, while the scalar condensate remains constant throughout the EI phase. By contrast, the critical chemical potential depends nonmonotonically on the field because of the successive occupation of Landau levels. The magnetic field also shifts the mean-field tricritical point and enlarges the first-order region of the temperature--chemical-potential phase diagram. We further analyze the Hall conductivity and find that increasing the field reduces the number of plateaus and modifies the threshold for a finite Hall response. For the parameters considered, the emergence of the EI condensate is accompanied by a characteristic change in the Hall conductivity, including a field-dependent change of slope near a continuous transition. These results show that the combined phase structure and Hall response can provide complementary signatures of excitonic ordering in planar fermionic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the nonlinear Hall effect in strained moiré watermill lattices containing high-pseudospin fermions. By introducing strain, the authors activate a Berry curvature dipole that drives a large, tunable nonlinear response. This work demonstrates how geometric properties of the lattice can be engineered to yield enhanced topological transport signatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the concept of engineering geometric responses (like the BCD) through external fields (strain) mirrors control mechanisms used in quantum optics or AMO physics to tune system Hamiltonians and induce novel collective states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the linear and nonlinear Hall response, particularly focusing on how strain engineering can activate a finite Berry curvature dipole in moiré structures hosting high-pseudospin fermions.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a large nonlinear Hall effect whose magnitude substantially exceeds that of symmetry-broken transition metal dichalcogenides. The sign reversal of the Berry curvature dipole across topological phase boundaries creates characteristic 'butterfly like features'.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using continuum models (Bistritzer-MacDonald formalism) to map out phase diagrams based on mass strength and twist angle, followed by incorporating strain effects.

**Model / system.** The model is a moiré watermill lattice (WM), treated as a four-band manifold hosting high-pseudospin fermions. The Hamiltonian incorporates intralayer hopping terms and interlayer coupling, which are then modified by an inversion symmetry breaking mass term ($\Delta$) and uniaxial strain ($\epsilon_p$).

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Hall response (NLHE), Chern numbers ($C$), Berry curvature dipole (BCD).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), onsite mass strength ($\Delta$), and applied strain ($\epsilon_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes only nearest-neighbor hopping processes are dominant, and the system is analyzed in the context of a four-band model.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the WM lattice structure with its various hopping bonds. The paper analyzes phase diagrams showing Chern number evolution and BCD dependence on strain/mass.

**Paper structure.** The work proceeds by first establishing the low-energy physics of the moiré watermill lattice, mapping out topological phases via Chern numbers using mass terms. Subsequently, uniaxial strain is introduced to break residual symmetries, activating a finite Berry curvature dipole responsible for the nonlinear Hall response.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the linear and nonlinear Hall response of a moiré \emph{watermill lattice}, in which stacking and twisting generate a four-band manifold near the Fermi level with suppressed group velocities at discrete magic angles. Including an inversion symmetry breaking onsite mass breaks the interlayer symmetry, opening a gap in this manifold and driving the system into a non-trivial bulk topological phase. We map the resulting phase diagram as a function of the strength of the mass and twist angle $θ$, revealing several sectors with high Chern numbers. We then introduce strain to break the residual $C_3$ symmetry of the lattice which activates a finite Berry curvature dipole and correspondingly, a nonlinear Hall response. The dipole reverses sign sharply across topological phase boundaries, producing butterfly like features when plotted against the relevant system parameters. Its magnitude substantially exceeds that reported for symmetry-broken transition metal dichalcogenides, consistent with the elevated Wilson-loop winding and enhanced quantum geometry associated with the lattice's pseudospin-$3/2$ character. We conclude by incorporating thermal effects on the Berry curvature dipole, asserting that it is an important tool for discerning topology at low temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced analytical techniques to quantify nonlinear phase noise in high-speed optical communication links. By expanding the noise field using Gauss-Hermite functions up to arbitrary degrees of freedom, it provides a more accurate model than previous works. The results yield an optimum launch power that minimizes overall signal degradation due to both linear and nonlinear effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical communication systems, the mathematical framework—using orthogonal function expansions (Gauss-Hermite) to model complex noise accumulation in a nonlinear wave equation—shares conceptual parallels with analyzing quantum noise or decoherence channels in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes nonlinear phase noise accumulation in coherent fiber-optic communication systems utilizing Phase Shift Keying (PSK). It aims to provide accurate analytical expressions for the phase variance by accounting for higher-order noise modes.

**Main result.** The inclusion of arbitrary degrees of freedom (DOFs) significantly improves the accuracy over previous two-DOF models, and an optimum launch power can be derived by balancing linear and nonlinear noise contributions.

**Method.** The analysis uses a perturbation expansion approach on the Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, representing the noise field using Gauss-Hermite functions as an orthogonal basis set.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an amplified optical link governed by the NLS equation, where noise arises from Amplified Spontaneous Emission (ASE) at discrete amplifier locations. The analysis extends beyond simple two-DOF approximations to arbitrary DOFs.

**Key observables.** Phase variance ($\langle\delta\phi^2angle$), optimum launch power ($P_{opt}$), and Bit Error Rate (BER).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dispersion profile ($eta_2(z)$), nonlinear coefficient ($\gamma$), number of amplifiers ($N_a$), and the number of degrees of freedom (DOFs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a pseudo-linear regime where nonlinearity is treated as a first-order perturbation, and it assumes signal power is much greater than mean ASE power.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of phase variance on the number of DOFs (matched vs. Gaussian filters) and show how variance changes with total length or launch power for specific dispersion parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops analytical expressions by expanding the noise field using Gauss-Hermite functions, deriving formulas for the phase variance considering arbitrary DOFs, and finally determining the optimum launch power that minimizes this accumulated noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Analytical expressions for the phase variance in a nonlinear fiber optic system based on phase-shift keying are developed. The Gauss-Hermite functions are used as the orthogonal basis to represent the noise field. Number of degrees of freedom (DOF) to accurately model the phase variance is estimated. The amplifier noise excites higher order Gauss-Hermite noise modes and the nonlinear mixing of a signal pulse and higher order Gauss-Hermite noise mode leads to new noise fields which enhance the nonlinear phase noise. The higher order noise modes propagate linearly and enhance the linear phase noise if the matched filter is not used at the receiver. Analytical expression for the optimum launch power is developed taking into account the linear and nonlinear phase noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates using $	ext{Ta}_2	ext{O}_5$ memristor circuits as compact dynamic reservoirs for high-speed time-series prediction. By exploiting the voltage-dependent switching speed, the system achieves tunable forgetting dynamics and can operate across frequencies from kHz to MHz. This shows that memristors are excellent candidates for next-generation neuromorphic hardware capable of analyzing fast temporal signals.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical signal processing, the underlying physics involves non-linear device dynamics (memristors) whose state evolution is governed by external electrical fields, touching upon concepts relevant to open quantum systems modeling or complex memory elements in quantum computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses high-speed time-series prediction by utilizing compact memristor circuits that exhibit adjustable dynamic behavior.

**Main result.** The configurable forgetting dynamics of the memristors enable high prediction accuracy using a small number of channels, and the operating frequency can be tuned across many orders of magnitude (kHz to MHz).

**Method.** Time-series prediction is benchmarked against complex dynamical systems (NARX, NARMA2, DYN3) by encoding inputs with positive pulses and controlling memory decay via adjustable negative offset voltages.

**Model / system.** The physical platform consists of Ta2O5 nonvolatile memristors configured as dynamic reservoir computing layers. The key mechanism exploited is the strongly voltage-dependent switching speed, which allows for tunable programming and forgetting times.

**Key observables.** Normalized Mean Squared Error (NMSE) used to quantify prediction accuracy; operating frequency ($	ext{kHz}$ to $	ext{MHz}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of memristive channels ($N$), the negative offset voltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{offset}}$), and the input/readout voltages.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on the 'time-voltage dilemma' inherent to memristors for achieving tunable forgetting dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept of using memristors as dynamic reservoirs, detailing the pulsing scheme (programming vs. forgetting) and comparing prediction performance across three benchmark dynamical systems (NARX, NARMA2, DYN3).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the use of memristors for reservoir computing, details the physical mechanism enabling tunable dynamics via voltage pulses, benchmarks the system's performance on increasingly complex time-series tasks, and concludes by demonstrating high accuracy across wide frequency ranges.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ta$_2$O$_5$ nonvolatile memristors are used as compact, traceable, and well-controllable dynamic reservoir computing layers to perform time-series prediction tasks. The strongly voltage-dependent switching speed is utilized for information processing. It enables the configuration of tailorable programming and forgetting times in response to the positive and negative driving voltage pulses. Benchmarking this framework on time-series prediction problems reveals that the configurable forgetting dynamics enables a high prediction accuracy using a rather small number of memristive input channels. The training is based either on optimizing the output layer using linear regression with fixed forgetting times, or on optimizing the forgetting times as well. In the first case, six memristive channels, while in the second, only two memristive channels are used to demonstrate excellent prediction accuracy for the benchmark tasks. This scheme allows for the tunability of the operating frequency over many orders of magnitude: by adjusting the input voltage levels, the information processing speed of the same memristive dynamic layer can be increased from the kHz to the MHz range while maintaining excellent prediction accuracy. These findings demonstrate the merits of memristor based dynamic networks in the analysis, prediction and recovery of fast temporal signals, approaching telecommunication data rates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reveals that magnetic fields profoundly reorganize electronic states in moiré bilayer graphene by inducing phenomena like magnetic breakdown and Lifshitz transitions. The evolution is highly dependent on the valley index, offering a unified picture of how modest fields drive the system from simple semiclassical behavior toward complex Hofstadter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the study of field-induced topological changes (like Berry curvature hotspots driving MB) provides a rich example of how external fields can fundamentally alter quantum band topology, which has parallels in understanding engineered quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how applying a magnetic field reorganizes the electronic states within moiré bilayer graphene, tracing the system's evolution from semiclassical transport toward the quantum Hofstadter regime.

**Main result.** Magnetic fields induce rich, valley-contrasting changes in orbital networks, manifesting as magnetic breakdown and Lifshitz transitions. Furthermore, interpocket scattering can produce persistent resistance oscillations even when conventional quantum oscillations are thermally washed out.

**Method.** The research combines transport measurements (measuring magnetoresistance and Hall density) with theoretical analysis of semiclassical orbits influenced by moiré potentials and Berry curvature effects.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a moiré bilayer graphene/hBN heterostructure. The electronic structure is governed by the interplay between the underlying band structure, magnetic field quantization (leading to orbital magnetic moments), and moiré superlattice effects.

**Key observables.** Quantum oscillation frequencies, Hall density ($	ext{nH}$), longitudinal resistance ($R_{xx}$), and signatures of magnetic breakdown/Lifshitz transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength (1 T to 2 T), carrier density range, temperature, and the moiré filling factor ($
u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that observed quantum oscillations can be decomposed into contributions from different Fermi pockets (electron vs. hole) and that magnetic breakdown/Lifshitz transitions are key mechanisms governing state reorganization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of transport properties ($R_{xx}$ and $R_{xy}$) across varying magnetic fields and carrier densities, detailing signatures of magnetic breakdown in K and K' valleys and mapping out low-field quantum oscillations from different pocket populations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the system's response at low fields, then detailing how moderate fields induce distinct valley-dependent transitions (MB/Lifshitz), and finally presenting evidence for interpocket scattering effects persisting to higher temperatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic fields are widely used to diagnose quantum phases in two-dimensional systems through quantum oscillations or by tuning spin and valley polarizations, but magnetic fields can also reshape the underlying electronic structure. Here, using a bilayer graphene/hBN moiré system, we reveal a rich magnetic field induced evolution of the semiclassical orbit network, encompassing Lifshitz transitions, magnetic breakdown, and scattering between coexisting electron and hole pockets. At magnetic fields of 1 T to 2 T, quantum oscillation frequencies and the Hall density change markedly over a broad carrier density range, signaling magnetic breakdown and magnetic Lifshitz transitions. This evolution is valley contrasting: Berry curvature hot spots near the breakdown junctions enhance magnetic breakdown in the K valley while suppressing it in the K$^\prime$ valley, whereas valley-antisymmetric orbital magnetic moments split the corresponding Lifshitz transitions. The resulting valley-selective trajectories manifest at higher fields as valley-symmetry-breaking Hofstadter gaps. At elevated temperatures and low magnetic fields, scattering between coexisting electron and hole pockets produces nearly density-independent resistance oscillations whose frequency tracks the sum of their Fermi surface areas, persisting after conventional Onsager oscillations are thermally washed out. Our results provide a unified picture of how modest magnetic fields reorganize moiré electronic states as the system evolves from semiclassical transport toward the Hofstadter regime.

</details>
