**Summary.** This work uses molecular dynamics simulations to map out how methane and water structure and move when squeezed into nanopores. The key finding is that structural organization doesn't simply increase or decrease with pore size; instead, a competition between local ordering and the inability to form full 3D structures defines critical length scales. This has implications for understanding gas hydrate stability in porous media.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on liquid-state physics, the concept of confinement-induced dimensional reduction, frustrated ordering, and identifying critical length scales where multiple physical behaviors peak is highly relevant for understanding quantum confinement effects in nanoscale materials or engineered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural ordering and dynamic behavior of methane-water mixtures are altered when confined within nanopores.

**Main result.** Confinement leads to a non-monotonic competition between local structuring (maximizing at intermediate pores) and the suppression of extended 3D correlations, defining a characteristic length scale for maximum heterogeneity.

**Method.** Molecular dynamics simulations were employed using all-atom force fields to track structural metrics and transport properties across varying pore widths.

**Model / system.** The system models methane ($	ext{CH}_4$) and water ($	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$) confined within planar silica nanopores, simulating conditions relevant to hydrate formation.

**Key observables.** Radial distribution functions (RDFs), local order parameters ($F_{3}, F_{4}$), parallel and perpendicular diffusion coefficients ($D_{\parallel}, D_{\perp}$), and anomalous diffusion exponents ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pore widths ranging from 1 to 5 nm, temperatures between 250 K and 295 K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation conditions are assumed to fall within the methane hydrate stability zone for the force field used.

**Figures summary.** Figures show density profiles of water/methane across pore sizes; RDFs illustrate local structure; plots detail non-monotonic changes in diffusion coefficients and anomalous exponents ($\alpha$) with pore size.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes structural evolution (using order parameters and RDFs) followed by a detailed investigation of anisotropic transport properties, correlating dynamical decoupling ($\Delta\alpha$) directly to the observed structural frustration regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the structural and dynamical behavior of methane water systems under nanoconfinement using molecular dynamics simulations across pore widths from 1 to 5 nm. Structural analysis reveals a strong and nonmonotonic dependence on confinement: while tetrahedral ordering partially recovers as confinement is reduced, cubic like order associated with clathrate precursors is maximized at intermediate pore sizes. Radial distribution functions show that three dimensional correlations are suppressed under strong confinement, whereas lateral ordering persists, indicating a reduction in the effective dimensionality of structural organization. Transport properties reflect the same structural competition. Parallel diffusion is nonmonotonic with pore size, while perpendicular motion is subdiffusive due to confinement induced trapping and heterogeneity. Methane exhibits stronger subdiffusion and remains dynamically coupled to the water matrix. A characteristic confinement length scale emerges at which structural ordering, dynamical heterogeneity, and solvent solute decoupling are simultaneously maximized. At strong confinement, three dimensional correlations are suppressed, leading to dimensional reduction, frustrated ordering, and inhibited nucleation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces CheMLFlow, an open-source platform designed to solve the reproducibility crisis in scientific machine learning. It allows researchers to build end-to-end, automated pipelines for tasks like predicting material properties or bioactivity using molecular data. By standardizing workflows and supporting agentic experimentation, it significantly reduces manual orchestration overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not focused on fundamental quantum physics, the platform's emphasis on structured, reproducible pipelines and agentic workflows is highly relevant for automating complex simulations or data analysis involving quantum chemistry calculations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem addressed is the bottleneck in scientific machine learning development, where researchers struggle to assemble reproducible, end-to-end pipelines for complex tasks.

**Main result.** CheMLFlow provides an open-source, modular platform that standardizes and automates the entire ML workflow—from data acquisition to final reporting—for cheminformatics and materials informatics applications.

**Method.** The authors introduce CheMLFlow, a configuration-driven framework using YAML/JSON files to orchestrate sequential or parallel computational nodes (e.g., featurization, training, validation).

**Model / system.** The platform is designed for molecular and material systems, enabling high-throughput screening and benchmarking across various ML models (e.g., Random Forest, Deep Learning) applied to tasks like bioactivity prediction.

**Key observables.** Model performance metrics ($	ext{R}^2$, $	ext{RMSE}$, $	ext{AUROC}$), standardized run artifacts, SHAP explanations, and structured reports.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The platform supports varying parameters through a Design of Experiments (DOE) layer, allowing systematic testing of different representations or model settings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that standardizing the workflow orchestration process will significantly reduce overhead and improve reproducibility across diverse scientific domains.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the three layers of CheMLFlow (Core, DOE, Agentic). Figure 2 illustrates the YAML/JSON configuration structure. Figure 3 maps a typical bioactivity prediction workflow flowchart.

**Paper structure.** The paper describes the system architecture by detailing its modular components: data handling, feature engineering nodes, model backends, and the overarching control flow managed by the run configuration file, culminating in benchmarking results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

CheMLFlow is an open-source platform for building and executing end-to-end, high-throughput, and agentic workflows for scientific and technological applications. CheMLFlow targets a common bottleneck in scientific machine learning development, where researchers often need to assemble data acquisition, curation, representation, model training, validation, screening, interpretation, and reporting into a reproducible pipeline, even when their primary research contribution concerns only one stage. CheMLFlow provides modular workflow components, ready-to-run reference pipelines, standardized artifacts, and evaluation outputs that reduce orchestration overhead and support benchmarking across methods and datasets. The platform is designed to be extensible, reproducible, and automation friendly, with pluggable representations and models, deterministic splits, explicit run artifacts, batch execution, and report generation. As scientific software increasingly moves toward agent assisted experimentation, CheMLFlow's configuration driven workflows and structured outputs also provide a practical interface for coding agents to help users construct experiments, inspect results, and summarize findings under human supervision. This article describes the system architecture, core workflows, and benchmarks that reach literature performance for quantum mechanical, physicochemical and bioactivity property prediction, and use cases involving time series datasets demonstrating applications beyond molecular chemistry datasets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically screens potential superconducting materials by calculating their fundamental mechanical properties using first-principles DFT. By developing quantitative indicators based on elastic constants and stacking fault energies, the authors successfully pinpointed several new compounds that are predicted to be both highly superconducting and mechanically ductile.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter mechanics, the rigorous application of first-principles methods to link electronic/superconducting properties ($T_c$) with macroscopic mechanical stability (ductility) represents a powerful multi-physics approach relevant to designing novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To assess the practical viability of novel superconducting materials by correlating their high critical temperatures ($T_c$) with their mechanical properties, specifically ductility.

**Main result.** The study identified several new candidates ($	ext{HfPd}_2	ext{Al}$, $	ext{TiRuSb}$, and $	ext{ZrNi}_2	ext{Ga}$) that simultaneously exhibit high $T_c$ and predicted structural ductility.

**Method.** First-principles calculations using DFT were employed in a high-throughput screening workflow to compute elastic constants, stacking fault energies, and various mechanical ratios.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on phonon-mediated superconductors selected from an experimentally synthesized database. The analysis involves modeling the bulk mechanical response (elastic tensors) and plastic deformation behavior of crystalline solids.

**Key observables.** Pugh’s ratio ($r_{	ext{Pugh}}$), Pettifor’s ratio ($r_{	ext{Pett}}$), Rice’s ratio, superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), bulk/shear moduli, and generalized stacking fault energies ($\gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ductility criteria are linked to $r_{	ext{Pugh}} \approx 0.57$ and the comparison between calculated ratios and their average values.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on DFT calculations, with initial discrepancies noted between Born expansion and finite-displacement methods, leading to a preference for the latter for high-throughput screening.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares $r_{	ext{Pugh}}$ and $r_{	ext{Pett}}$ from different calculation methods; Figure 2 correlates these mechanical ratios against $	ext{T}_{	ext{iso}c}$ and crystal structure family.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a workflow to calculate elastic tensors for 250 candidates, establishes diagnostic indicators ($r_{	ext{Pugh}}, r_{	ext{Pett}}$), refines these indicators by incorporating plasticity (Rice's ratio into $c^*$), and finally identifies specific materials meeting both high-$T_c$ and ductile criteria.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We performed a first-principles high-throughput screening of the mechanical properties of phonon-mediated superconductors selected from the recent experimentally synthesized superconducting materials database PRX Energy 4, 033012 (2025). We developed the workflows that combine first-principles calculations of elastic constants and generalized stacking fault energies to assess the ductility of superconducting candidates. Starting from the 250 materials identified with promising superconducting critical temperatures, we computed their elastic tensors to evaluate bulk and shear moduli, Pugh's ratio, and Pettifor's ratio from first principles. To further characterize their plastic deformation behavior, we calculated the stacking fault energy and surface energy for selected materials and slip directions, allowing the estimation of Rice's ratio and ductility indicators. We found that several new materials simultaneously exhibit high-T$_c$ and ductility including HfPd$_2$Al, TiRuSb, and ZrNi$_2$Ga with predicted isotropic T$_c$= 6.80K, 12.88K, and 8.23K, respectively. This work offers a quantitative mapping of mechanical performance across a wide range of superconductors and provides a reference to identify new mechanically promising superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally investigates the mechanical interaction between MXene flakes and gold surfaces using advanced AFM techniques. The researchers quantified the interfacial shear stress as 399 MPa while confirming that the material maintains its crystalline structure even after undergoing nano-wear. These findings establish a foundation for utilizing MXenes in next-generation self-powering electronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily materials science, the quantitative measurement of interfacial stress ($	au$) under controlled nanoscale contact mechanics provides fundamental data on material interfaces relevant to energy transduction mechanisms studied in AMO physics (e.g., Casimir forces, surface interactions).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the nanomechanical compliance of MXene flakes against a metal-like interface, specifically determining the interfacial shear stress.

**Main result.** An interfacial shear stress of 399 MPa was measured for $	ext{Ti}_3	ext{C}_2	ext{T}_x$ on gold, and the material retained its crystallinity despite localized nano-wear.

**Method.** Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM), including force spectroscopy and force mapping, was used to measure adhesion forces and derive shear stress. TEM confirmed structural integrity post-testing.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves self-assembled $	ext{Ti}_3	ext{C}_2	ext{T}_x$ MXene flakes interacting with a gold probe tip, mimicking metal interfaces for energy harvesting applications.

**Key observables.** Interfacial shear stress ($	au$), adhesion force ($F_a$), nano-wear patterns, and retained crystallinity in tribofilms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interfacial shear stress of 399 MPa; flake dimensions (e.g., $\langle H angle = 1.25 \pm 0.61 	ext{ nm}$); adhesion forces ($\sim 10-30 	ext{ nN}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are highly dependent on the specific metal-MXene interface, and compacting MXenes compromises individual flake tribological behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures show AFM topography (including long-term stability), adhesion maps comparing different areas, friction force vs. lateral distance profiles, and TEM/EELS confirming crystallinity and thickness.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically uses AFM techniques to map forces and stresses at the MXene/Au interface, followed by structural characterization using TEM/EELS to validate mechanical testing results for applications in nanoelectronics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The demand for novel self-powering and sustainable electronics requires major efforts in identifying new advanced materials for nano-applications. MXene have gathered attention due to their electronic and mechanical properties, however, their nanomechanical compliance against a metal-like interface is still not clearly identified. In this work, we employ atomic force microscopy to characterize the nanomechanical properties of a self-assembled thin flake of titanium carbide MXene (Ti3C2Tx) against a gold probe. The investigation returns an interfacial shear stress of 399 MPa, and the observation of nano-wear localized in the center of the MXene flake. Nevertheless, MXene flakes retained their crystallinity in the tribofilms as confirmed by transmission electron microscopy and electron energy loss spectroscopy. The outcomes of this work set the basis for the use of Ti3C2Tx in novel nano harvesting systems involving metal interfaces (e.g., tribovoltaic nanogenerators) with large scope in nanoelectronics, wearable sensing, electric vehicles, and robotics.

</details>
